Dynamics of the collision of two nearly equal solitary waves for the Zakharov-Kuznetsov equation
Didier Pilod, Frédéric Valet

Abstract
. We study the dynamics of the collision of two solitary waves for the Zakharov-Kuznetsov equation in dimension 2 2 and 3 3 . We describe the evolution of the solution behaving as a sum of 2 2 -solitary waves of nearly equal speeds at time t = − ∞ t=-\infty up to time t = + ∞ t=+\infty . We show that this solution behaves as the sum of two modulated solitary waves and an error term which is small in H 1 H^{1} for all time t ∈ ℝ t\in\mathbb{R} . Finally, we also prove the stability of this solution for large times around the collision. The proofs are a non-trivial extension of the ones of Martel and Merle for the quartic generalized Korteweg-de Vries equation to higher dimensions. First, despite the non-explicit nature of the solitary wave, we construct an approximate solution in an intrinsic way by canceling the error to the equation only in the natural directions of scaling and translation. Then, to control the difference between a solution and the approximate solution, we use a modified energy functional and a refined modulation estimate in the transverse variable. Moreover, we rely on the hamiltonian structure of the ODE governing the distance between the waves, which cannot be approximated by explicit solutions, to close the bootstrap estimates on the parameters. We hope that the techniques introduced here are robust and will prove useful in studying the collision phenomena for other focusing non-linear dispersive equations with non-explicit solitary waves.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 37K40; 37K45; Secondary: 35Q53; 35B40; 35C08; 35Q60

1.
Introduction

1.1.
The Zakharov-Kuznetsov equation

The Zakharov-Kuznetsov (ZK) equation

\partial_{t}u+\partial_{x}\left(\Delta u+u^{2}\right)=0.

(1.1)

where
u=u(t,\mathbf{x})
is a real-valued function,
\mathbf{x}=(x,\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
,
d\geq 2
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
\Delta=\partial_{x}^{2}+\Delta_{\mathbf{y}}^{2}
denotes the Laplacian in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, is a natural high dimensional generalisation of the Korteweg-de Vries (KdV) equation, which corresponds to the case
d=1
.
In dimensions
2
and
3
, this equation has been introduced by Zakharov and Kuznetsov
[
61
]
(see also
[
28
,
3
]
) to describe the propagation of ionic-acoustic waves in a uniformly magnetized cold plasma. The physical derivation of ZK from the Euler-Poisson system was performed rigorously by Lannes, Linares and Saut
[
30
]
(see also
[
54
]
). The ZK equation was also rigorously derived by Han-Kwan
[
21
]
from the Vlasov-Poisson system in the presence of an external magnetic field.

Unlike the Korteweg-de Vries equation (KdV) in dimension
1
or the Kadomtsev-Petviashvili equation (KP) in dimension
2
, ZK is not completely integrable in dimensions
2
or
3
.
Nevertheless, it has a Hamiltonian structure and possesses the following conserved quantities:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u(t,\mathbf{x})d\mathbf{x},\quad M(u(t))=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u(t,\mathbf{x})^{2}d\mathbf{x}\quad\text{and}\quad E(u(t))=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{|\nabla u(t,\mathbf{x})|^{2}}{2}-\frac{u^{3}(t,\mathbf{x})}{3}d\mathbf{x}.

(1.2)

Moreover, the solutions of ZK are invariant under scaling and translation; more precisely, if
u
is a solution of (
1.1
), then, for any
c>0
,
\mathbf{z}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

u_{c,\mathbf{z}_{0}}(t,\mathbf{x})=cu(c^{\frac{3}{2}}t,c^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}_{0}))

(1.3)

is also a solution of (
1.1
). Here, we will focus on the two and three dimensional cases which are
L^{2}
-subcritical.

The initial value problem (IVP) associated to the ZK equation has been shown to be globally well-posed in the energy space
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, in dimension
d=2
by Faminskii
[
15
]
and in dimension
d=3
by Herr and Kinoshita
[
22
]
. The result in dimension
d=2
was improved in
[
33
,
20
,
48
]
culminating with the well-posedness in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
s>-\frac{1}{4}
, in
[
25
]
. The local well-posedness result of Herr and Kinoshita
[
22
]
holds in
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, for
s>-\frac{1}{2}
, which is optimal from a scaling point of view. We also refer to
[
34
,
55
,
48
]
for former well-posedness results in the
3
-dimensional case.

Below, we summarize the global well-posedness results of
[
15
,
22
]
in the energy space
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
d=2,3
: for any
u_{0}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, there exists a unique (in some sense) solution to (
1.1
)
in
C(\mathbb{R}:H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Moreover,
M(u)(t)=M(u_{0})
and
E(u)(t)=E(u_{0})
, for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

1.2.
Solitary waves

The ZK equation admits a family of special solutions propagating at constant speed in the first direction. These solutions, which play an important role in the ZK dynamics, are called
solitary wave
solutions. They are of the form

u(t,\mathbf{x})=u(t,x,\mathbf{y})=Q_{c}(x-ct,\mathbf{y}),\quad\text{with}\quad Q_{c}(\mathbf{x})\underset{|\mathbf{x}|\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0,

(1.4)

where
c>0
,
Q_{c}(\mathbf{x})=cQ(\sqrt{c}\mathbf{x})
and
Q
is the
ground state
of

\displaystyle-\Delta Q+Q-Q^{2}=0.

(1.5)

Indeed, the elliptic equation (
1.5
) has a unique smooth positive radial solution, called
ground state
. We refer for example to
[
57
,
5
]
for the existence and to
[
29
]
for the uniqueness. Even though there is no explicit formula for
Q
as for the generalised KdV equation (gKdV) in dimension
1
, it is well-known that
Q
decays exponentially at infinity. These classical results are gathered in the proposition below.

Proposition 1.1
.

The following assertions hold true.

(i)

There exists a smooth, positive, radial solution of (
1.5
), which is exponentially decreasing at infinity. This solution, denoted by
Q
, is called a ground state of (
1.5
).

(ii)

The ground-state
Q
of (
1.5
) is unique.

(iii)

The first order asymptotic expansion of
Q
at infinity is given by

\displaystyle\exists\mu>0,\quad\lim_{r\rightarrow+\infty}r^{\frac{d-1}{2}}e^{r}Q(r)=\mu.

(1.6)

Remark 1.2
.

Whereas first investigated in
[
57
]
, the asymptotic expansion at infinity of
Q
given in (iii) is a result from
[
19
]
. Its proof is based on the maximum principle and Hopf Lemma. We give in Appendix
B
an alternative proof in dimension
d=2
relying on ODE arguments.

From now on, we will work with the ground state
Q
associated to (
1.5
). By abuse of notations, we sometimes consider
Q
as a function defined on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, with the identification
Q(\mathbf{x})=Q(|\mathbf{x}|)
. In polar coordinates and in dimension
d=2
,
Q
satisfies the equation

\displaystyle-Q^{\prime\prime}(r)-\frac{d-1}{r}Q^{\prime}(r)+Q(r)-Q^{2}(r)=0.

In this work, it will be important to have a sharper asymptotic of
Q
at infinity as the one given in (
1.6
). The next proposition, whose proof is given in Appendix
B
, shows that in dimension
2
, the modified Bessel function of second kind
K_{0}
is a good approximation of
Q
at infinity. We refer for example to
[
1
,
2
]
for the definition and the properties of
K_{0}
, (see also Appendix
B
). This result seems to be new and may be useful in the study of other nonlinear dispersive equations such as the nonlinear Schrödinger equation.

Proposition 1.3
.

In dimension
d=2
, there exists
\kappa>0
such that

\displaystyle\forall r>1,\quad\left|Q(r)-\kappa K_{0}(r)\right|\lesssim\frac{e^{-2r}}{r}.

(1.7)

In dimension
d=3
, there exists
\kappa>0
such that

\displaystyle\forall r>1,\quad\left|Q(r)-\kappa\frac{e^{-r}}{r}\right|\lesssim\frac{e^{-r}}{r^{2}}.

(1.8)

Remark 1.4
.

We will not work directly with (
1.7
). Instead, we use an approximation of
K_{0}
by a finite series (see Proposition
B.5
for more details).

The solitary wave solutions of ZK defined in (
1.4
) are known to be orbitally stable in the energy space
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, for
d=2
and
d=3
, since the work de Bouard
[
11
]
in the
90^{\prime}s
. More recently, the asymptotic stability of the family
\{Q_{c}\}_{c>0}
has been proved in dimension
d=2
by Côte, Muñoz, Simpson and the first authors in dimension
d=2

[
9
]
and by Farah, Holmer, Roudenko and Yang in dimension
d=3

[
16
]
. The proofs in
[
9
,
16
]
are a nontrivial extensions of the arguments of Martel and Merle for the gKdV equation in one dimension
[
35
,
37
,
38
]
to higher dimensions. They are based on monotonicity arguments on oblique hyper-planes and rigidity results classifying the solutions around the solitary waves (Liouville properties). It is worth noting that the virial estimates, which are key ingredient in proving the Liouville properties, rely on numerical arguments (the sign of a scalar product in dimension
2
and the positivity of a linear operator in dimension
3
under suitable orthogonality conditions). This is somehow due to the non-explicit character of the ground state
Q
.

Finally, using new virial estimates, Mendez, Muñoz, Poblete and Pozo
[
46
]
prove that the solutions of ZK vanish in some large time-dependent regions that do not contain solitary waves.

1.3.
Collision phenomena for nonlinear dispersive equations

Discovered in the second half of the
19^{th}
century, solitary waves propagating in nonlinear dispersive equations have long remained of interest to only a few mathematicians. It was not until until the
60s
that these special solutions began to regain attention. To explain some unexpected numerical observations of Fermi, Pasta, Ulam and Tsingou at Los Alamos
[
17
]
, Zabruski, Kruskal
[
60
]
performed some numerical simulations on the KdV equation and observed two striking phenomena. First, the solutions of the equation decompose eventually into a finite sum of solitary waves and a dispersive radiation. Secondly, two solitary waves evolving at different speed would “pass through one another without losing their identity (size or shape)”. The solitary waves of the KdV equation were then renamed
solitons
to emphasize their particle-like character.

These discoveries led to an intense activity to understand the mathematical properties of the KdV equation, culminating in the development of the
inverse scattering method

[
18
,
31
]
. In particular, the behaviour of solutions behaving as the sum of two solitary waves at time
-\infty
and
+\infty
was investigated by Lax in
[
31
]
. We also mention the explicit formulas for multi-solitons of the KdV equation discovered by Hirota in
[
23
]
. These works confirmed the
elasticity
of the collision of two solitary waves of the KdV equation observed numerically in
[
60
]
: the waves remain unchanged in shape and size after the collision. Later on, still relying on the complete integrability theory, Eckhaus and Schuur
[
13
]
showed the
soliton resolution property
for solutions of KdV evolving from smooth and decaying initial data.

We are interested here in the collision of two solitary waves with nearly equal speeds. The following phenomenon was observed numerically in
[
60
]
: as soon as the highest wave gets reasonably close to the smallest wave, the highest wave begins to shrink, transferring some of its mass to the smallest one until the two waves exchange roles and then separate. LeVeque
[
32
]
studied the evolution of the explicit solution
u_{c_{1},c_{2}}
of KdV behaving at time
\pm\infty
as the sum of two solitons with nearly equal speeds
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
(with
c_{2}>c_{1}
). If
\mu_{0}=(c_{2}-c_{1})/(c_{1}+c_{2})
is small enough, then the solution
u_{c_{1},c_{2}}
remains close to a sum of two modulated solitary waves for all times with an error of order
\mu_{0}^{2}
. Moreover, the distance between the centers of the modulated solitary waves is always larger than
2|\ln\mu_{0}|
going at infinity when
\mu_{0}
tends to
0
.

It is worth noting that all the results described above rely heavily on the complete integrability machinery for the KdV equation. The situation for non-integrable equations is far more delicate since there is no explicit formula to describe the evolution of solutions behaving at infinity as the sum of two solitary waves. For this reason, there are only a few theoritical results that describe the non-integrable collision of two solitary waves. For the generalized KdV equation with quartic power in the nonlinearity, Mizumachi
[
47
]
proved that the solution evolving from an intial datum close in an exponentially weighted space to a sum of two solitary waves of nearly equal speed, the highest one being on the left, will remain close to a sum of two solitary waves for all positive times, and that these waves do not cross each other; in other words, the interaction is
repulsive
as in the integrable case.

The overtaking collision of two copropagating solitary waves of almost equal size, with the leading wave being the smallest, was then investigated for the water wave system by Craig, Guyenne, Hammack, Henderson and Sulem in
[
10
]
(see figure 14). It is shown numerically and experimentally that the trajectories of the two solitary waves do not cross, the evolution of their heights is monotonic and the collision amplifies the largest of the solitary wave. Moreover, the presence of a residual term after the collision emphasizes its
inelastic
character.

Some years later, Martel and Merle
[
41
]
were able to give a much more precise description of the interaction of two nearly equal solitary waves for the quartic gKdV equation. By starting with a pure
2
-solitary wave
u_{c_{1}^{-},c_{2}^{-}}
at time
-\infty
satisfying

\lim_{t\rightarrow-\infty}\left\|u_{c_{1}^{-},c_{2}^{-}}(t,\cdot)-Q_{c_{2}^{-}}\left(\cdot-c_{2}^{-}t-x_{2}^{-}\right)-Q_{c_{1}^{-}}\left(\cdot-c_{1}^{-}t-x_{1}^{-}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}=0,

for
c_{2}^{-}>c_{1}^{-}
with
\mu_{0}=(c_{2}^{-}-c_{1}^{-})/(c_{2}^{-}+c_{1}^{-})>0
small enough, they proved that the solution
u_{c_{1}^{-},c_{2}^{-}}
decomposes as

u_{c_{1}^{-},c_{2}^{-}}(t,\cdot)=Q_{c_{1}(t)}(\cdot-z_{1}(t))+Q_{c_{2}(t)}(\cdot-z_{2}(t))+\eta(t),

where
\|\eta(t)\|_{H^{1}}\leq C\mu_{0}^{2}|\ln\mu_{0}|^{\frac{1}{2}}
,
z_{1}(t)-z_{2}(t)\geq c|\ln\mu_{0}|
, for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
\liminf_{t\to+\infty}\|\eta(t)\|_{H^{1}}\geq c\mu_{0}^{3}
. As in the integrable case, there is a transfer of mass from the highest wave to the smallest one in the interaction region. Note however that in contrast with the integrable case, there is also a loss of mass and energy by radiation, and that the size
c_{1}^{+}=\lim_{t\to+\infty}c_{1}(t)
of the largest wave at infinity is greater than
c_{2}^{-}
, reciprocally
c_{2}^{+}=\lim_{t\to+\infty}c_{2}(t)<c_{1}^{-}
. This result thus emphasises the
inelastic
nature of the collision of two solitary waves of nearly equal speeds for the quartic gKdV equation. Moreover, the stability in the energy space
H^{1}
of the collision is also proved in
[
41
]
.

The situation of the collision of one large and one small solitary waves for the quartic gKdV equation was investigated by Martel and Merle
[
40
]
. In this setting, the largest wave crosses the smallest, but again the authors were able to prove the inelasticity of the collision. These results have been extended for the Benjamin-Bona-Mahony equation (BBM) in
[
43
]
, and also for the gKdV equation with a general smooth nonlinearity
f(u)
in
[
39
,
51
]
. In particular, Muñoz proved in
[
51
]
that if
f(u)
is analytic in
0
, then the collision is elastic if and only if
f(u)=u^{2}
,
u^{3}
or
u^{3}-cu^{2}
which corresponds to the three known integrable cases in the gKdV setting (the Korteweg-de Vries equation, the modified Korteweg-de Vries equation and the Gardner equation). In this sense, one could argue that the integrability of a focusing nonlinear dispersive equation is related to the elasticity of the collision of two solitary waves. It is worth noting that, as far as we know, there is no result of this kind for KdV-type equations in higher dimensions. We refer nevertheless to
[
27
,
26
]
for interesting numerical simulations for the Zakharov-Kuznetsov equation in
2
and
3
-dimensions, displaying in particular the inelasticity of the collision.

There are also a few theoritical results on the collision for non-integrable dispersive equations which are not of KdV type. Perelman
[
52
]
studied the collision of a large and a small solitary waves for a perturbation of the cubic
1
-dimensional nonlinear Schrödinger (NLS) equation and proved that after the collision the smallest wave splits into two outgoing waves that are controlled on large time by the cubic NLS dynamics. In
[
42
]
, Martel and Merle proved the inelasticity of the collision for the
5
-dimensional energy critical wave equation. Moutinho considered the
\Phi^{6}
-model
[
49
,
50
]
and proved the almost elasticity of the collision. Finally, let us mention that the study of the collision of solitary waves is an important tool in proving the soliton resolution property for nonlinear dispersive equations. We refer for example to
[
12
,
24
,
8
]
and the references therein in the framework of the nonlinear wave equation.

1.4.
Statement of the results

The main objective of this paper is to study the dynamics of the collision of two solitary waves of nearly equal speeds for the Zakharov-Kuznetsov equation. We are able to describe the evolution of the solution behaving as a sum of
2
-solitary waves of nearly equal speeds at time
t=-\infty
up to time
t=+\infty
. We show that the solution behaves as a sum of two modulated solitary waves and an error term which is small in
H^{1}
. Finally, we also prove the stability of this solution for large times around the collision.

Let
0<c_{1}^{-}<c_{2}^{-}
be the given speeds at time
-\infty
and let
\mathbf{x}_{1}^{-}=(x_{1}^{-},\mathbf{y}_{1}^{-})
and
\mathbf{x}_{2}^{-}=(x_{2}^{-},\mathbf{y}_{2}^{-})
denote the centers of the solitary waves. We consider the solution
u
of (
1.1
) behaving asymptotically at time
-\infty
as the sum of these two solitary waves,
i.e.

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow-\infty}\left\|u(t,\cdot)-Q_{c_{2}^{-}}\left(\cdot-c_{2}^{-}t-x_{2}^{-},\cdot-\mathbf{y}_{2}^{-}\right)-Q_{c_{1}^{-}}\left(\cdot-c_{1}^{-}t-x_{1}^{-},\cdot-\mathbf{y}_{1}^{-}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}=0.

(1.9)

The existence and uniqueness of such solution was proved by the second author in
[
58
]
.

In order to study the evolution of
u
from time
-\infty
to
+\infty
, we first symmetrize the problem. Let

\displaystyle\mu_{0}=\frac{c_{2}^{-}-c_{1}^{-}}{c_{1}^{-}+c_{2}^{-}},\quad c_{0}=\frac{c_{1}^{-}+c_{2}^{-}}{2},\quad\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}=c_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\mathbf{y}_{2}^{-}-\mathbf{y}_{1}^{-}}{2},\quad x_{0}=\frac{x_{1}^{-}+x_{2}^{-}}{2},

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}_{0}=\frac{\mathbf{y}_{1}^{-}+\mathbf{y}_{2}^{-}}{2},\quad t_{0}=\frac{lc_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}+x_{2}^{-}-x_{1}^{-}}{c_{2}^{-}-c_{1}^{-}}

where
l=l(\mu_{0})
is a real number defined below (see Lemma
4.1
). We define the new function
v
by

u(t,x,y)=c_{0}v\big(c_{0}^{\frac{3}{2}}(t+t_{0}),c_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}(x-c_{0}t-x_{0}),c_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}_{0}))

Then
v
is the unique solution to

\partial_{t}v+\partial_{x}\left(\Delta v-v+v^{2}\right)=0.

(1.10)

satisfying

\lim_{t\to-\infty}\left\|v(t,\cdot)-Q_{1+\mu_{0}}(\cdot-(\mu_{0}t-\frac{l}{2}),\cdot+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0})-Q_{1-\mu_{0}}(\cdot+\mu_{0}t-\frac{l}{2},\cdot-\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0})\right\|_{H^{1}}=0.

(1.11)

Therefore, the study of the solution
u
of (
1.1
) satisfying (
1.9
) for arbitrary parameters
0<c_{1}^{-}<c_{2}^{-}
,
x_{1}^{-}
,
x_{2}^{-}
,
\mathbf{y}_{1}^{-}
,
\mathbf{y}_{2}^{-}
is equivalent to the study of the solution
v
of (
1.10
) satisfying the more symmetric asymptotic behaviour (
1.11
) at time
-\infty
.

The dynamics of the difference of the centers of the two solitary waves will be roughly given by the even solution
Z
of the ODE

\displaystyle\ddot{Z}(t)=\frac{2}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Q(x+Z(t),\mathbf{y})\partial_{x}(Q^{2})(x,\mathbf{y})dxd\mathbf{y},

(1.12)

satisfying
\displaystyle\lim_{t\to-\infty}(Z(t),\dot{Z}(t))=(+\infty,-2\mu_{0})
. We denote by
Z_{0}:=Z(0)
. If
\mu_{0}
is chosen small enough, then
\mu_{0}\sim Z_{0}^{-\frac{d-1}{4}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}Z_{0}}
. We refer to Appendix
D
for a detailed study of the solutions to (
1.12
).

t
Z(t)
Z_{0}
t
\dot{Z}(t)
-2\mu_{0}
2\mu_{0}

Figure 1.
Graph of the functions
Z
and
\dot{Z}

In this setting, we state our main result.

Theorem 1.5

(Dynamics of the interaction of two solitary waves)
.

Let
d=2
or
3
. There exist positive constants
C
and
\nu^{\star}
such that the following is true. Let
0<\mu_{0}<\nu^{\star}
,
|\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0}|<\mu_{0}
and let
v
be the unique solution to (
1.10
) satisfying (
1.11
). Then, there exist
(z_{1},z_{2},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}:\mathbb{R}^{2d+2})
such that

\eta(t,\cdot)=v(t,\cdot)-Q_{1+\mu_{2}(t)}(\cdot-z_{2}(t),\cdot-\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}(t))-Q_{1+\mu_{1}(t)}(\cdot-z_{1}(t),\cdot-\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}(t))

(1.13)

satisfies, for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\|\eta(t)\|_{H^{1}}+\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left(\left|\dot{z}_{j}(t)-\mu_{j}(t)\right|+\left|\dot{\boldsymbol{\omega}}_{j}(t)\right|+\left|\mu_{j}(t)+\frac{(-1)^{j}}{2}\dot{Z}(t)\right|\right)\leq C\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{2}};

(1.14)

\displaystyle z_{1}(t)-z_{2}(t)\geq\frac{1}{2}Z_{0}.

(1.15)

Moreover, there exist
\mu_{1}^{+}=\lim_{t\to+\infty}\mu_{1}(t)
and
\mu_{2}^{+}=\lim_{t\to+\infty}\mu_{2}(t)
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to+\infty}\|\eta(t)\|_{H^{1}(x>-\frac{99}{100}t)}=0;

(1.16)

\displaystyle 0\leq\mu_{1}^{+}-\mu_{0}\leq C\mu_{0}^{2};

(1.17)

\displaystyle 0\leq-\mu_{2}^{+}-\mu_{0}\leq C\mu_{0}^{2}.

(1.18)

Remark 1.6
.

As in the one dimensional case, it follows from (
1.15
) that the distance between the two solitary waves is always large and is tending to
\infty
as
\mu_{0}
goes to
0
. Moreover, it will be clear from the proof that the more precise estimate

\displaystyle\left|\min_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left((z_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1})(t)-(z_{2},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})(t)\right)-(Z_{0},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{0})\right|\leq CZ_{0}^{2}\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}},

holds, (see (1.17) in
[
41
]
in the one dimensional case).

In the later computations, we will make use of a small positive constant
\rho<\frac{1}{32}
independent of
\mu_{0}
and define the unique time
T_{1}>0
satisfying
Z(T_{1})=\rho^{-1}Z_{0}
. Notice that at time
T_{1}
, the solution
v
as defined in Theorem
1.5
is still close to the sum of the two decoupled solitary waves. We define the time interval
[-T_{1},T_{1}]
as the
collision region
.

Theorem 1.7

(Stability in the region of collision)
.

Under the notations of Theorem
1.5
and the definition of
T_{1}
above, if a function
w
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|w(-T_{1})-v(-T_{1})\right\|_{H^{1}}\leq Z_{0}^{-5}\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{4}},

(1.19)

then it holds for any
t\in[-T_{1},T_{1}]

\displaystyle\left\|w(t)-v(t)\right\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{4}}.

(1.20)

Remark 1.8
.

By using similar arguments as in the proof of Proposition
4.3
, it is enough to assume that, for some
\mathcal{T}\in[-T_{1},T_{1}]
,

\displaystyle\left\|w(\mathcal{T})-v(\mathcal{T})\right\|_{H^{1}}\leq Z_{0}^{-5}\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{4}},

(1.21)

to obtain (
1.20
).

Remark 1.9
.

After the time
T_{1}
(and before the time
-T_{1}
), one can use the stability result of well-prepared multi solitary waves in
[
53
]
to deduce that the solution
w
remains close in the energy space
H^{1}
to the sum of two modulated solitary waves for all time
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Even though the speeds of these waves are close to the ones of
v
in the decomposition in Theorem
1.5
, the translation parameters
(z_{i},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{i})
may not be close to the ones of
v
, and thus, it is not clear that the solution
w
remains close to the solution
v
for all times.

1.5.
Strategy of the proofs

The proofs of Theorems
1.5
and
1.7
extend the road map introduced by Martel and Merle in
[
41
]
(see also
[
47
]
) to higher dimensions. Below we explain the key ingredients of the proofs and highlight the main differences and new challenges that arise in higher dimensions. For the sake of simplicity, we focus on the
2
-dimensional case in the rest of the paper, since the proofs for the
3
-dimensional case are similar.

Step
1
: constructing a suitable approximate solution.

The first step consists of constructing an approximate solution
V
to (
1.10
) with a good control on the error
\mathcal{E}(V)=\partial_{t}V-\partial_{x}\left(-\Delta V+V-V^{2}\right)
. At first order,
V
should be given by a sum of two modulated solitary waves
V_{0}=R_{1}+R_{2}
, where
R_{i}(t,\mathbf{x})=Q_{1+\mu_{i}(t)}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}_{i}(t))
,
i=1,2
, for a set of well chosen
C^{1}
translation and dilation parameters
(\mathbf{z}_{1},\mathbf{z}_{2},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=(z_{1},z_{2},\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
. Then, the error is of the form

\mathcal{E}(V_{0})=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\overrightarrow{\widetilde{m}_{i}}\cdot\overrightarrow{MR_{i}}+2\partial_{x}(R_{1}R_{2}),\quad\text{where}\quad\overrightarrow{\widetilde{m}_{i}}=\begin{pmatrix}-\dot{z}_{i}+\mu_{i}\\
-\dot{\omega}_{i}\\
\dot{\mu}_{i}\end{pmatrix},\quad\overrightarrow{MR_{i}}=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{x}R_{i}\\
\partial_{y}R_{i}\\
\Lambda R_{i}\end{pmatrix}.

Here,
\overrightarrow{MR_{i}}
represents the natural directions associated with the translation and scaling
1
1

1

We refer to (
2.14
) for a precise definition of the scaling operator
\Lambda R_{i}
.
invariants of the equation (see (
1.3
)),
\overrightarrow{\widetilde{m}}_{i}
is the rough evolution of the dynamical parameters
\Gamma
. Moreover, the interaction term
2\partial_{x}(R_{1}R_{2})
is a plateau of size
z^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-z}
between
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
, where
z=z_{1}-z_{2}
denotes the distance between the two solitary waves in the tangential spatial coordinate
x
(see (
1.6
)). Thus, it is not localized around each solitary wave as for the quartic gKdV equation in
[
41
]
. It is worth noting that this first new difficulty is related to the quadratic power in the non-linearity of (
1.1
) and not to the higher dimension of the problem. A first new idea is to add the term
F=\left(-\Delta+1\right)^{-1}(2R_{1}R_{2})

2
2

2

In the definition of
F
in (
2.32
), we are actually working with
\tilde{R}_{1}
and
\tilde{R}_{2}
, which are obtained from
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
by rescaling around the size
1
and translating in the transverse direction so that the centers of the waves are located on the
x
-axis.
to the approximate solution
V_{0}
. Then, a direct computation shows that

\mathcal{E}(V_{0}+F)=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\vec{\widetilde{m}}_{i}\cdot\overrightarrow{MR_{i}}+2\partial_{x}((R_{1}+R_{2})F)+\partial_{x}(F^{2})+\partial_{t}F.

Even though the main error term
2\partial_{x}((R_{1}+R_{2})F)
is still of size
z^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-z}
, it is now localised around the two solitary waves
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
.

In the
1
-dimensional case of the quartic gKdV equation, the solitary wave
Q
is explicit and
Q(\cdot+z)
has an asymptotic expansion in powers of
e^{-z}
at infinity. This allows the authors in
[
41
]
to improve the order of the error around each solitary wave coming from the expansion of
Q(\cdot+z)
by a factor
e^{-z}
by solving the remainder terms locally around each waves. The situation is much more delicate in higher dimensions. In dimension
2
,
Q(\cdot+z,\cdot)
has an asymptotic development for
|\mathbf{x}|
small compared to
z
of the form
3
3

3

We refer to Proposition
B.12
for a precise statement.

\forall\,n\in\mathbb{N},\quad Q(x+z,y)\underset{z\to+\infty}{\sim}q_{0}(\mathbf{x})z^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-z}+q_{1}(\mathbf{x})z^{-\frac{3}{2}}e^{-z}+\cdots+q_{n}(\mathbf{x})z^{-n-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-z}.

Hence, even solving these asymptotic expansions locally around each solitary wave would not improve the error sufficiently to close the estimates. To bypass this difficulty, we choose to make the error small only in the three natural directions of
\overrightarrow{MR_{i}}
. Another major new difficulty that arises in higher dimension is that the solitary waves no longer have an explicit formulation. For this reason, we define our approximate solution
V
by

V=R_{1}+R_{2}+F+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\overrightarrow{p_{i}}\cdot\overrightarrow{NR_{i}}\quad\text{with}\quad\overrightarrow{p_{i}}(z):=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{i}(z)\\
\beta_{i}(z)\\
\gamma_{i}(z)\end{pmatrix},\quad\overrightarrow{NR_{i}}=\begin{pmatrix}-(-\Delta+1)^{-1}R_{i}\\
-\partial_{x}^{-1}(-\Delta+1)^{-1}\partial_{y}R_{i}\\
-\partial_{x}^{-1}(-\Delta+1)^{-1}\Lambda R_{i}\end{pmatrix},

where the directions in
\overrightarrow{NR_{i}}
are defined in an intrinsic way
4
4

4

In the definition of
V
in (
2.32
), we are actually working with
\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{i}}
which corresponds to the directions
\overrightarrow{NR_{i}}
rescaled around the size
1
and whose centers are retranslated in the transverse direction onto the
x
axis.
. By using
\partial_{x}(-\Delta+1)\overrightarrow{NR_{i}}=\overrightarrow{MR_{i}}
, one can rewrite the error of
V
on the form

\mathcal{E}(V)=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\overrightarrow{m_{i}}\cdot\overrightarrow{MR_{i}}+T+\partial_{x}S,\quad\text{where}\quad\overrightarrow{m_{i}}:=\begin{pmatrix}-\dot{z}_{i}+\mu_{i}+\alpha_{i}\\
-\dot{\omega}_{i}+\beta_{i}\\
\dot{\mu}_{i}+\gamma_{i}\end{pmatrix}.

(1.22)

While the main term in the error
\partial_{x}S
is still of order
z^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-z}
, we adjust the parameters in
\overrightarrow{p_{i}}
so that the scalar products
\langle S,\overrightarrow{MR_{i}}\rangle
have smaller order of approximately
e^{-\frac{3}{2}z}
(see (
2.51
) for a precise statement). This choice induces a refined dynamical system on the geometrical parameters given by
\overrightarrow{m_{i}}
. Here, we can compute explicitly

\gamma_{1}(z)=-\gamma_{2}(z)=-\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q(x+z,y)\partial_{x}(Q^{2})(x,y)dxdy.

so that the distance
z
between the two solitary waves can be approximated by the solution
Z
of the second order nonlinear ODE

\ddot{Z}(t)=\frac{2}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q(x+Z(t),y)\partial_{x}(Q^{2})(x,y)dxdy\approx Z(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-Z(t)},

(1.23)

satisfying
\displaystyle\lim_{t\to-\infty}(Z(t),\dot{Z}(t))=(+\infty,-2\mu_{0})
. Observe that the factor
Z^{-\frac{1}{2}}
is not present in the
1
-dimensional case (see (1.24) in
[
41
]
). Moreover, it is worth noting that since
\gamma_{1}(z)=-\gamma_{2}(z)
and
\beta_{1}(z)=\beta_{2}(z)
, the approximate solution
V
is actually a sum of the two solitary waves
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
and a plateau of size
z^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-z}
localised between the waves (see Figure
2
).

Step
2
: modulation theory and energy estimates.

Once the approximate solution
V
is constructed, we look at a solution
w
of (
1.10
) close to
V
. By using modulation theory, we adjust the geometrical parameters
z_{i}
,
\omega_{i}
and
\mu_{i}
to ensure some orthogonality conditions on the error
\epsilon=w-V
in the natural directions
\partial_{x}R_{i}
,
\partial_{y}R_{i}
and
R_{i}
,
i=1,2
. This implies directly some bounds for
|\overrightarrow{m_{i}}|
in terms of
\|\epsilon\|_{H^{1}}
and the error terms in
\mathcal{E}(V)
. To control
\|\epsilon\|_{H^{1}}
, we use a virial-energy functional inspired from
[
41
]
, but modified by adding the term
\int S\epsilon
, where
S
is the main term in the error
\mathcal{E}(V)
in (
1.22
). This modification of the energy is necessary because, as explained above, we cannot improve the error
\mathcal{E}(V)
in a significant way as in the one dimensional case. This is the second new ingredient that we have to introduce to deal with the
2
-dimensional case. We refer to
[
44
]
for a similar idea to construct strong interactions of solitary waves for a Schrödinger system with cubic nonlinearity in one dimension.

Step
3
: bootstrap estimates.

With these estimates in hand, we are able to prove Theorem
1.7
. We study a solution
w
close to the approximate solution
V
in a large time region
[-T_{1},T_{1}]
around the collision. We prove that the distance between the solitary waves
z(t)
is well approximated by the solution of the ODE
Z(t)
to (
1.23
) satisfying
\displaystyle\lim_{t\to-\infty}(Z(t),\dot{Z}(t))=(+\infty,-2\mu_{0})
(see Proposition
4.3
). To this aim, we perform a series of bootstrap estimates on the sizes of the geometrical parameters and
\|\epsilon\|_{H^{1}}
. Two new difficulties arise in the two dimensional case. Firstly, the control on the transverse translation parameter given by the modulation estimates is not good enough to close the estimates. To address this issue, we introduce new linear quantities in
\epsilon
which allow to obtain sharper estimates on the evolution of the transverse parameters. These quantities correspond to scalar products of
\epsilon
with some non-localised directions. Similar quantities were used in
[
41
]
in the tangential direction. Note however that in order to use these quantities for a general solution
w
, we have to localise them in a suitable way (see the definition of
\mathcal{K}_{i}
in (
4.29
) and Remark
4.6
for more detail). We refer to
[
45
]
for a similar idea in the framework of the modified Benjamin-Ono equation. Secondly, the second order nonlinear ODE governing the evolution of the distance between the two solitary waves cannot be approximated by explicit solutions as in the one dimensional case. Moreover, even an approximation of the ODE (
1.23
) by
\ddot{Z}(t)=Z(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-Z(t)}
would not provide sufficiently sharp estimates on the quantities
|\dot{\mu}_{i}(t)-\gamma_{i}(t)|
. For this reason, we perform in Appendix
D
a detailed study of the ODE (
1.23
) by using a phase portrait and rely on its Hamiltonian structure to obtain more refined estimates on
|z(t)-Z(t)|
(see Proposition
D.10
for a precise statement).

Step
4
: proof of the main theorem.

Now, let us give some details of how to prove Theorem
1.5
. We start with the well-prepared
2
-solitary waves solution of (
1.10
) at time
-\infty
,
v
satisfying (
1.11
) and constructed in
[
58
]
. First, we prove a quantitative orbital stability result around the sum of two solitary waves as long as their centers remain far enough away from each other. It is worth noting that the distance between the solution and the sum of the two solitary waves at a given and potentially large negative time
-t_{0}
has to be carefully quantified as a function
\kappa(-t_{0})
which decays faster than
t_{0}^{-1}
at infinity. This allows us to bring our solution
v
close to the collision time
-T_{1}
. We then apply the stability result through the collision region
[-T_{1},T_{1}]
. At this point the waves have transferred their mass and the highest one is on the right. Finally, we conclude by applying the asymptotic stability result proved in
[
53
]
.

Summary.

We extend the techniques introduced in
[
41
]
for the quartic generalized Korteweg-de Vries equation to higher dimensions. First, despite the non-explicit nature of the solitary wave
Q
, we are able to construct an approximate solution in an intrinsic way by canceling the error to the equation only in a few natural directions. Then, to control the difference between a solution and the approximate solution, we use modified energy functional and a refined modulation estimate in the transverse variable. Moreover, we rely on the hamiltonian structure of the ODE governing the distance between the waves, which cannot be approximated by explicit solutions, to close the bootstrap estimates on the parameters. It is worth noting that we do not use any numerical method, except for the asymptotic stability result in
[
53
]
, which relies on the virial estimates proved in
[
9
]
and
[
16
]
. We hope that the techniques introduced here are robust and will prove useful in studying the collision phenomena for other focusing non-linear dispersive equations with non-explicit solitary waves. In a future work, we plan to refine our construction and prove the inelasticity of the collision of ZK in dimensions
2
and
3
.

The paper is organised as follows: in Section
2
, we construct the approximate solution
V
and derive several estimates on the error
\mathcal{E}(V)
. In Section
3
, we perform the modulation on the geometrical parameters and prove the energy estimates on the error between the solution and the approximate solution. We use these estimates in Section
4
to prove the main stability result in the collision region. Then, we prove Theorems
1.5
and
1.7
in Section
5
. Finally, we state and derive several useful results in the appendices: we give some properties of the Bessel potential
(-\Delta+1)^{-1}
in Appendix
A
; we give some precise asymptotics of the ground state
Q
in Appendix
B
; we derive some technical estimates in Appendix
C
and we study the dynamical system (
1.23
) in Appendix
D
.

Notations

•

Throughout the paper, we will denote by
C
a positive constant which may change from line to line. The notation
a\lesssim b
means that
a\leq Cb
.

•

We use the standard notation for the sets of integers
\mathbb{N}=\left\{0,1,\cdots\right\}
and
\mathbb{N}^{\ast}=\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
.

•

In general, we will denote
\mathbf{x}=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then,
|\mathbf{x}|=(x^{2}+y^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle=(1+|\mathbf{x}|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}.

•

We will work with real-valued function
f=f(\mathbf{x})=f(x,y):\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
. For
1\leq p\leq+\infty
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, respectively
W^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, denotes the standard Lebesgue spaces, respectively Sobolev spaces. We also write
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=W^{s,2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
For
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
two real-valued functions, we denote
by
\langle f,g\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(\mathbf{x})g(\mathbf{x})d\mathbf{x}
their scalar product
5
5

5

Note that there is no risk of confusion with the japanese bracket defined above for an element
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
.

•

We define the function spaces

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}:=\left\{f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}):\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{2},\exists n\in\mathbb{N},\forall\,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\ |\partial_{\mathbf{x}}^{\alpha}f(\mathbf{x})|\lesssim\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{n}e^{-|\mathbf{x}|}\right\}.

(1.24)

and

\mathcal{Z}:=\left\{f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}):\ \begin{aligned} &\partial_{x}f\in\mathcal{Y}\ \text{and}\ \forall k\in\mathbb{N},\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\,\\
&\text{with}\,x\geq 0,\ |\partial_{y}^{k}f(\mathbf{x})|\lesssim e^{-\frac{15}{16}|\mathbf{x}|}\end{aligned}\right\}.

(1.25)

•

For
c>0
, we define the generator of the scaling symmetry
\Lambda_{c}
and its iterated
\Lambda^{2}_{c}
by

\displaystyle\Lambda_{c}(f)(\mathbf{x})=\Lambda_{c}f(\mathbf{x}):=\frac{d}{d\tilde{c}}\left(\tilde{c}f(\sqrt{\tilde{c}}\cdot)\right)_{|\tilde{c}=c}(\mathbf{x})=\left(\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\right)f\right)\left(\sqrt{c}\mathbf{x}\right),

(1.26)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{2}_{c}f(\mathbf{x}):=\frac{d^{2}}{d\tilde{c}^{2}}\left(\tilde{c}f(\sqrt{\tilde{c}}\cdot)\right)_{\tilde{c}=c}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{c}\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}\cdot\nabla\right)f\right)\left(\sqrt{c}\mathbf{x}\right).

(1.27)

In the case
c=1
, we denote

\Lambda Q=\Lambda_{1}Q\quad\text{and}\quad\Lambda^{2}Q=\Lambda_{1}^{2}Q.

(1.28)

•

We will denote by
(-\Delta+1)^{-1}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
the Bessel potential of order
2
, which is defined as a Fourier multiplier by
(1+|{\bf\xi}|^{2})^{-1}
. We refer to Appendix
A
for several important properties related to
(-\Delta+1)^{-1}
.

•

We define the operator
-\partial_{x}^{-1}
on
\mathcal{Y}
by

\displaystyle-\partial_{x}^{-1}:\mathcal{Y}\to\mathcal{Z},\ f\mapsto-\partial_{x}^{-1}f:=\int_{x}^{+\infty}f(\tilde{x},y)d\tilde{x}.

(1.29)

Observe in particular that, for all
f\in\mathcal{Y},

\partial_{x}(-\partial_{x}^{-1})f=-f=(-\partial_{x}^{-1})\partial_{x}f.

(1.30)

2.
Construction of the approximate solution

2.1.
Properties of the linearized operator around the ground state

The linearized operator
L
around the ground state is defined by

\displaystyle L:=-\Delta+1-2Q.

(2.1)

The following proposition gathers some classical results on
L
.

Proposition 2.1
.

The operator
L:H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\subseteq L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfies the following properties.

(i)

Essential spectrum:

L
is a self-adjoint operator and its essential spectrum is given by
\sigma_{ess}(L)=[1,+\infty)
.

(ii)

Non-degeneracy of the spectrum:

ker(L)=\text{span}\,\{\partial_{x}Q,\partial_{y}Q\}
.

(iii)

Negative eigenvalue:

L
has a unique negative eigenvalue
-\lambda_{0}<0
, which is simple, and there exists a radially symmetric positive eigenfunction
\chi_{0}
associated to
-\lambda_{0}
. Without loss of generality,
\chi_{0}
is chosen of
L^{2}
-norm equal to
1
. Furthermore, there exists
\kappa_{0}>0
such that:

\displaystyle\forall r>1,\quad\left|\chi_{0}(r)-\kappa_{0}K_{0}\left(\sqrt{1+\lambda_{0}}r\right)\right|\lesssim r^{-1}e^{-2(1+\lambda_{0})^{\frac{1}{2}}r}.

(2.2)

(iv)

Coercivity:
there exists
C>0
such that, for any
f\in H^{2}
in
L^{2}
satisfying
\langle f,\partial_{x}Q\rangle=\langle f,\partial_{y}Q\rangle=\langle f,Q\rangle=0
:

\displaystyle\left\langle Lf,f\right\rangle\geq C\|f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

(v)

Invertibility:
for any function
h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
orthogonal to
\text{span}\,\{\partial_{x}Q,\partial_{y}Q\}
, there exists a unique function
f\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
orthogonal to
\text{span}\,\{\partial_{x}Q,\partial_{y}Q\}
such that
Lf=h
. Moreover, if
h
is radial, then
f
is also radial and if
h
is even (resp. odd) with respect to one of its two variables, then
f
is even (resp. odd) with respect to the same variable.

(vi)

Regularity:
for
h\in L^{2}
, if
f\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is such that
h=Lf\in\mathcal{Y}
, then
f\in\mathcal{Y}
.

(vii)

Scaling:

L\Lambda Q=-Q
.

Proof.

Assertion (i) follows from classical perturbation arguments such as the Kato-Rellich theorem. The proof of (ii) is more delicate (see
[
59
,
7
]
). We refer to
[
59
]
for the proof of (iii) and (iv). Note however, that the proof of (
2.2
) is given in Appendix
B
.

We now give some details of the proof of (v). The uniqueness part follows directly from (ii). For the existence part, it suffices to prove that any
h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
orthogonal to
\text{Span}\,\{\partial_{x}Q,\partial_{y}Q\}
is in the range of
L
. Equivalently, given
h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
orthogonal to
\text{Span}\,\{\partial_{x}Q,\partial_{y}Q\}
, we look for
f\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that

\displaystyle\left(id_{L^{2}}-2Q\left(-\Delta+1\right)^{-1}\right)(-\Delta+1)f=h.

(2.3)

Let us denote by
T
the operator
2Q(-\Delta+1)^{-1}:L^{2}\rightarrow L^{2}
. This operator is compact and
T^{\star}=(-\Delta+1)^{-1}(2Q\cdot)
. Moreover, observe that
\text{Ker}(id-T^{\star})=\text{Ker}(L)
. Thus, it follows from the Fredholm alternative that
h\in R(id-T)=\text{Ker}(id-T^{\star})^{\perp}
, so that there exists
\widetilde{f}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
such that
(id-T)\widetilde{f}=h
. Then,
f=(-\Delta+1)^{-1}\widetilde{f}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and satisfies (
2.3
), which concludes the existence part of (v).

Assume now that
h
is radial. Let
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
and
R_{\theta}
be the rotation on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
of angle
\theta
. Since
Q
and
\Delta
are invariant under rotation, we have
L\left(f\circ R_{\theta}\right)=h
, so that
f-f\circ R_{\theta}\in\text{Ker}(L)
. Moreover,
f
is orthogonal to
\text{span}\left\{\partial_{x}Q,\partial_{y}Q\right\}
, so that
f\circ R_{\theta}
is orthogonal to
\text{Span}\left\{\partial_{x}Q\circ R_{\theta},\partial_{y}Q\circ R_{\theta}\right\}=\text{Span}\left\{\partial_{x}Q,\partial_{y}Q\right\}
. Hence,
f=f\circ R_{\theta}
, and we conclude that
f
is radial. The proof of the parity with respect to one of the variables follows similarly.

To prove (vi), assume that
h\in\mathcal{Y}\subseteq H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and let
f\in H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be a solution of
Lf=h
. First, it follows from a standard bootstrap argument that
f\in H^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. To prove the decay properties of
f
, we rewrite
f
as
f=(-\Delta+1)^{-1}(2Qf+h)
. It is clear from (
1.6
) and the Sobolev embedding that
Qf\in\mathcal{Y}
. Therefore, we conclude from Lemma
A.2
that
f\in\mathcal{Y}
, which finishes the proof of (vi).

Finally, the proof of
(vii)
follows directly by differentiating the equation satisfied by
Q_{c}
with respect to
c
and using (
1.28
).

∎

Next we introduce some useful functions related to the ground state. We define

X=-(-\Delta+1)^{-1}Q,\quad Y=-\partial_{x}^{-1}(-\Delta+1)^{-1}\partial_{y}Q\quad\text{and}\quad W=-\partial_{x}^{-1}(-\Delta+1)^{-1}\Lambda Q.

(2.4)

Observe that
X\in\mathcal{Y}
and
Y,\,W\in\mathcal{Z}
(see Lemma
B.1
). To work in a more condensed form, we also introduce the vectors
\overrightarrow{MQ}
and
\overrightarrow{NQ}
defined by

\overrightarrow{MQ}:=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{x}Q\\
\partial_{y}Q\\
\Lambda Q\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad\overrightarrow{NQ}:=\begin{pmatrix}X\\
Y\\
W\end{pmatrix}.

(2.5)

The following lemma explains how to choose some coefficients in order to ensure important orthogonality conditions.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{Y}
. There exists a unique vector
\overrightarrow{p}_{0}=\left(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0},\gamma_{0}\right)^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that

\displaystyle 2Q\overrightarrow{p}_{0}\cdot\overrightarrow{NQ}+f\perp\{\partial_{x}Q,\partial_{y}Q,\Lambda Q\}.

(2.6)

The coefficients
\alpha_{0}
,
\beta_{0}
and
\gamma_{0}
are given by

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}=\frac{1}{\langle Q,\Lambda Q\rangle+\langle(-\Delta+1)^{-1}Q,Q\rangle}\left(\langle f,\Lambda Q\rangle-\frac{\langle 2QW,\Lambda Q\rangle}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\langle f,\partial_{x}Q\rangle\right),

(2.7)

\displaystyle\beta_{0}=\frac{1}{\langle\partial_{x}^{-1}\partial_{y}Q,\partial_{y}Q\rangle}\langle f,\partial_{y}Q\rangle,\quad\gamma_{0}=\frac{-1}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\langle f,\partial_{x}Q\rangle.

(2.8)

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\left|\alpha_{0}\right|+\left|\beta_{0}\right|+\left|\gamma_{0}\right|\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}}.

(2.9)

Proof.

First, observe by using the identities in Lemma
B.2
that

\displaystyle\langle 2Q\overrightarrow{p}_{0}\cdot\overrightarrow{NQ}+f,\partial_{x}Q\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle\gamma_{0}+\langle f,\partial_{x}Q\rangle;

\displaystyle\langle 2Q\overrightarrow{p}_{0}\cdot\overrightarrow{NQ}+f,\partial_{y}Q\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle\partial_{x}^{-1}\partial_{y}Q,\partial_{y}Q\rangle\beta_{0}+\langle f,\partial_{y}Q\rangle;

\displaystyle\langle 2Q\overrightarrow{p}_{0}\cdot\overrightarrow{NQ}+f,\Lambda Q\rangle

\displaystyle=-\left(\langle Q,\Lambda Q\rangle+\langle(-\Delta+1)^{-1}Q,Q\rangle\right)\alpha_{0}+\langle 2QW,\Lambda Q\rangle\gamma_{0}+\langle f,\Lambda Q\rangle.

Note from Lemma
B.2
that
\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle
,
-\langle\partial_{x}^{-1}\partial_{y}Q,\partial_{y}Q\rangle
and
\langle Q,\Lambda Q\rangle+\langle(-\Delta+1)^{-1}Q,Q\rangle
are positive quantities. Thus, the choice of
\alpha_{0}
,
\beta_{0}
and
\gamma_{0}
as in the statement of the lemma is the unique possible choice to ensure the desired orthogonality relations.

Finally, estimate (
2.9
) follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.
∎

2.2.
Specific functions to describe the soliton interaction

In this subsection, we introduce some important notations and specific functions, which will be useful to construct the ansatz of the interaction of two solitary waves in the next subsections.

For
i=1,2
, let
\mathbf{z}_{i}(t)=(z_{i}(t),\omega_{i}(t))\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, respectively
\mu_{i}(t)\in\mathbb{R}
, be
C^{1}
functions denoting the centers of the solitons, respectively the sizes of the modulated solitons. We also denote by
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{i}(t)=(z_{i}(t),0)
the recentered variables
\mathbf{z}_{i}
on the axis
\{y=0\}
. Moreover, we denote

\displaystyle z(t):=z_{1}(t)-z_{2}(t),\quad\bar{z}(t):=z_{1}(t)+z_{2}(t),

(2.10)

\displaystyle\omega(t):=\omega_{1}(t)-\omega_{2}(t),\quad\bar{\omega}(t):=\omega_{1}(t)+\omega_{2}(t),

(2.11)

\displaystyle\mu(t):=\mu_{1}(t)-\mu_{2}(t),\quad\bar{\mu}(t):=\mu_{1}(t)+\mu_{2}(t).

(2.12)

Sometimes, when there is no risk of confusion, we drop the variable
t
of these functions.

We first introduce a compact notation for the two modulated solitons
R_{i}
, and their rescaled and recentered versions
\tilde{R}_{i}
. More precisely, for
i=1,2
, we denote

R_{i}(t,\mathbf{x}):=Q_{1+\mu_{i}(t)}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}_{i}(t))\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{R}_{i}(t,\mathbf{x}):=Q(\mathbf{x}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{i}(t))

(2.13)

and

\Lambda R_{i}(t,\mathbf{x}):=\left(\Lambda_{1+\mu_{i}(t)}Q\right)(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}_{i}(t))\quad\text{and}\quad\Lambda\tilde{R}_{i}(t,\mathbf{x}):=\Lambda Q(\mathbf{x}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{i}(t)).

(2.14)

Note in particular from the equation of
Q
in (
1.5
) that

\displaystyle-\Delta R_{i}+(1+\mu_{i})R_{i}-R_{i}^{2}=0,

(2.15)

\displaystyle-\Delta\tilde{R}_{i}+\tilde{R}_{i}-\tilde{R}_{i}^{2}=0.

(2.16)

We also define the linearized operator around
\tilde{R}_{i}
and
R_{i}
by

L_{\tilde{R}_{i}}:=-\Delta+1-2\tilde{R}_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad L_{R_{i}}=-\Delta+(1+\mu_{i})-2R_{i}.

(2.17)

Then, it follows from Proposition
2.1
(viii) that

\displaystyle L_{\tilde{R}_{i}}(\Lambda\tilde{R}_{i})=-\tilde{R}_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad\left(-\Delta+1-2R_{i}\right)\Lambda R_{i}=-R_{i}-\mu_{i}\Lambda R_{i}.

(2.18)

Finally, we introduce some useful functions
X_{i}=X_{i}(t,\mathbf{x})
,
Y_{i}=Y_{i}(t,\mathbf{x})
and
W_{i}=W_{i}(t,\mathbf{x})
to understand the interactions of the two solitons. For
i=1,2
, we define

X_{i}:=-(-\Delta+1)^{-1}\tilde{R}_{i},\quad Y_{i}:=-\partial_{x}^{-1}(-\Delta+1)^{-1}\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{i}\ \text{and}\ W_{i}:=-\partial_{x}^{-1}(-\Delta+1)^{-1}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{i}.

(2.19)

Observe that the functions
X_{i}
,
Y_{i}
and
W_{i}
are the translated of the functions
X
,
Y
and
W
introduced in (
2.4
) by
z_{i}(t)
in the first variable. Hence,
X_{i}\in\mathcal{Y}
and
Y_{i},\,W_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}
(see Lemma
B.1
). However, for any fixed
y\in\mathbb{R}
, the limit when
x\to-\infty
of
Y(x,y)
and
W(x,y)
is non zero. For this reason, we define

h(y):=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}(-\Delta+1)^{-1}\partial_{y}Q(x,y)\,dx,\quad l(y):=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}(-\Delta+1)^{-1}\left(\Lambda Q\right)(x,y)\,dx.

(2.20)

The particular structure studied in this article consists of almost same sizes and symmetric solitary waves interacting through a plateau that can be approximated by

\displaystyle P(t,\mathbf{x}):=W_{1}(t,\mathbf{x})-W_{2}(t,\mathbf{x}).

(2.21)

R_{1}
R_{2}
W_{2}
W_{1}
P

Figure 2.
Schematic reperesentation of the two solitary waves
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
, of the functions
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
, and of the function
P
with a plateau between the solitary waves

To work in a more condensed form, we introduce the vectors

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{MR_{i}}:=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{x}R_{i}\\
\partial_{y}R_{i}\\
\Lambda R_{i}\end{pmatrix},\quad\overrightarrow{M\tilde{R}_{i}}:=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{x}\tilde{R}_{i}\\
\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{i}\\
\Lambda\tilde{R}_{i}\end{pmatrix},\quad\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{i}}:=\begin{pmatrix}X_{i}\\
Y_{i}\\
W_{i}\end{pmatrix}\text{ and }\overrightarrow{n}(y):=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
h(y)\\
l(y)\end{pmatrix}.

(2.22)

Then, we observe from the definitions of
\overrightarrow{M\tilde{R}_{i}}
and
\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{i}}
that, for
i=1,2
,

\displaystyle\partial_{x}(-\Delta+1)\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{i}}=-\overrightarrow{M\tilde{R}_{i}},

(2.23)

and with (
2.17
) and the relation (
2.18
)

\displaystyle L_{\tilde{R}_{i}}\overrightarrow{M\tilde{R}_{i}}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\\
-\tilde{R}_{i}\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad(-\Delta+1-2R_{i})\overrightarrow{MR_{i}}=\begin{pmatrix}-\mu_{i}\partial_{x}R_{i}\\
-\mu_{i}\partial_{y}R_{i}\\
-\mu_{i}\Lambda R_{i}-R_{i}\end{pmatrix}

(2.24)

2.3.
Definition and properties of the approximate solution

The goal of the next subsections is to construct a suitable ansatz
V
for the interaction of the two solitary waves.

With the notations of Subsection
2.2
,
V=V(t,\mathbf{x})
should be on the form

V=R_{1}+R_{2}+V_{A}

(2.25)

and be a good approximation of (
1.10
). To be more precise, we define the error to (
1.10
) by

\mathcal{E}(V):=\partial_{t}V-\partial_{x}\left(-\Delta V+V-V^{2}\right).

(2.26)

The approximate solution
V
will depend on time through the set of geometrical parameters
\Gamma=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},z_{1},z_{2},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})
appearing in the definition of
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
in (
2.13
). These parameters will be adjusted in Section
3.1
by modulation theory. For the moment, we will assume that they satisfy the following rough estimates.

Assumption 2.3
.

Let
I
be a time interval,
Z_{0}^{\star}\geq 1
and
0<\nu_{0}\leq\nu_{0}^{\star}\leq 1
, where
Z_{0}^{*}
will be chosen large enough and
\nu_{0}^{\star}
will be chosen small enough. Let
\Gamma=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},z_{1},z_{2},\omega_{1},\omega_{2}):I\to\mathbb{R}^{6}
be a
C^{1}
function and let
z
be the real-valued function defined in (
2.10
). We assume that, for all
t\in I
,

\displaystyle z(t)=z_{1}(t)-z_{2}(t)\geq Z_{0}^{\star}

(2.27)

\displaystyle|\omega_{1}|\leq\nu_{0},\ |\omega_{2}|\leq\nu_{0},\ |\mu_{1}|\leq\nu_{0},\ |\mu_{2}|\leq\nu_{0}

(2.28)

\displaystyle-z(t)\leq z_{2}(t)\leq-\frac{1}{4}z(t),\quad\frac{1}{4}z(t)\leq z_{1}(t)\leq z(t),

(2.29)

To gain some insight on how to define the function
V_{A}
, we start by looking at
\mathcal{E}(R_{1}+R_{2})
. By differentiating and using (
2.15
), we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(R_{1}+R_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\begin{pmatrix}-\dot{z}_{i}+\mu_{i}\\
-\dot{\omega}_{i}\\
\dot{\mu}_{i}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\overrightarrow{MR_{i}}+\partial_{x}\left(2R_{1}R_{2}\right).

(2.30)

To cancel out the main contribution of the interaction term
\partial_{x}\left(2R_{1}R_{2}\right)
, we introduce the correction term
V_{A}
.

For
i=1,2
, let
\overrightarrow{p_{i}}(z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a
C^{1}
vector-valued function defined by

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{p_{i}}(z):=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{i}(z)\\
\beta_{i}(z)\\
\gamma_{i}(z)\end{pmatrix}.

(2.31)

Then, we define
V_{A}
by

\displaystyle V_{A}:=F+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\overrightarrow{p_{i}}\cdot\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{i}},\quad\text{with}\quad F:=(-\Delta+1)^{-1}(2\tilde{R}_{1}\tilde{R}_{2}),

(2.32)

where the vector
\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{i}}
is defined in (
2.22
). We also denote by
V_{A,1}
, respectively
V_{A,2}
, the contributions of
V_{A}
around
R_{1}
, respectively around
R_{2}
. They are defined by

\displaystyle V_{A,1}

\displaystyle:=F+\overrightarrow{p_{1}}\cdot\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{1}};

(2.33)

\displaystyle V_{A,2}

\displaystyle:=F+\overrightarrow{p_{2}}\cdot\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{2}}+\overrightarrow{p_{1}}\cdot\overrightarrow{n}.

(2.34)

The main results in this section are gathered in the following proposition.

Proposition 2.4
.

Under Assumption
2.3
, there exist
Z_{0}^{\star}\geq 1
large enough and
0<\nu_{0}^{\star}\leq 1
small enough such that the following is true. For
i=1,2
, there exists a
C^{1}
vector-valued function
\overrightarrow{p_{i}}=\overrightarrow{p_{i}}(z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that the following properties hold for
t\in I
.

(i)

Estimate for
V_{A}
.

V_{A}\in C^{1}(I:H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
with, for all
t\in I
,
V_{A}(t,\mathbf{x})\to 0
, when
|\mathbf{x}|\to\infty
, and

\displaystyle\|V_{A}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla V_{A}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim z^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-z};

(2.35)

\displaystyle\|V_{A}\|_{H^{2}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{15}{16}z};

(2.36)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}V_{A}\|_{H^{2}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{15}{16}z}\sum_{i=1}^{2}|\dot{z}_{i}|;

(2.37)

(ii)

Estimate for
V
.
For
i=1,2
,

\displaystyle\left\||\overrightarrow{MR_{i}}|(V-R_{i})\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{15}{16}z}.

(2.38)

(iii)

Estimates on the parameters.
For
i=1,2
, the vector
\overrightarrow{p_{i}}(z)
defined in (
2.31
) satisfies

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}(z)=\alpha_{1}(z),\quad\beta_{2}(z)=\beta_{1}(z)=0,\quad\gamma_{2}(z)=-\gamma_{1}(z)

(2.39)

\displaystyle\gamma_{i}(z)=\frac{-1}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\left\langle Q\left(\cdot-(-1)^{i}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}\right),\partial_{x}(Q^{2})\right\rangle

(2.40)

\displaystyle|\alpha_{i}(z)|+|\gamma_{i}(z)|\lesssim z^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-z},

(2.41)

(v)

Equation of the ansatz
V
.
The error
\mathcal{E}_{V}=\mathcal{E}(V)
can be decomposed into

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{V}=\mathcal{E}(V)=\mathcal{E}(R_{1}+R_{2}+V_{A})=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\overrightarrow{m_{i}}\cdot\overrightarrow{MR_{i}}+T+\partial_{x}S,

(2.42)

where, for
i=1,2
, the directions
\overrightarrow{MR_{i}}
are defined in (
2.22
), the modulation equations
\overrightarrow{m_{i}}
are defined by

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{m_{i}}:=\begin{pmatrix}-\dot{z}_{i}+\mu_{i}+\alpha_{i}\\
-\dot{\omega}_{i}+\beta_{i}\\
\dot{\mu}_{i}+\gamma_{i}\end{pmatrix},

(2.43)

and the error terms
S
and
T
satisfy

S=\Sigma+\Psi_{\Sigma}+\Psi_{S}

(2.44)

with

\displaystyle\|\Sigma\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim z^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-z};

(2.45)

\displaystyle\|\Psi_{\Sigma}\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{15}{16}z}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(|\mu_{i}|+|\omega_{i}|\right);

(2.46)

\displaystyle\|\Psi_{S}\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{31}{16}z}+e^{-\frac{15}{16}z}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(\mu_{i}^{2}+\omega_{i}^{2}\right);

(2.47)

\displaystyle\|T\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{15}{16}z}\sum_{i=1}^{2}|\dot{z}_{i}|+z^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-z}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(|\mu_{i}|+|\omega_{i}|\right),

(2.48)

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}S\right\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim z^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-z}\sum_{i=1}^{2}|\dot{z}_{i}|+e^{-\frac{15}{16}z}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(|\dot{\mu}_{i}|+|\dot{\omega}_{i}|\right)+e^{-\frac{15}{16}z}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{2}|\dot{z}_{i}|\right)\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(|\mu_{i}|+|\omega_{i}|\right).

(2.49)

(vi)

Orthogonality estimates for
\Sigma
and
S
.
For
i=1,2
, the quantity
\Sigma
satisfies

\displaystyle\left|\langle\Sigma,\partial_{x}\tilde{R}_{i}\rangle\right|+\left|\langle\Sigma,\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{i}\rangle\right|+\left|\langle\Sigma,\Lambda\tilde{R}_{i}\rangle\right|\lesssim e^{-\frac{31}{16}z},

(2.50)

\displaystyle\left|\langle S,\partial_{x}R_{i}\rangle\right|+\left|\langle S,\partial_{y}R_{i}\rangle\right|+\left|\langle S,\Lambda R_{i}\rangle\right|\lesssim e^{-\frac{31}{16}z}+e^{-\frac{15}{16}z}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(|\mu_{i}|+|\omega_{i}|\right).

(2.51)

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of Proposition
2.4
.

2.4.
Decomposition of the error
\mathcal{E}(V)

We compute the error associated to this new approximation

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(R_{1}+R_{2}+V_{A})=\mathcal{E}(R_{1}+R_{2})+\mathcal{E}(V_{A})+\partial_{x}\left(2(R_{1}+R_{2})V_{A}\right).

To compute
\mathcal{E}(V_{A})
, we observe by using (
2.23
) that

\displaystyle-\partial_{x}\left((-\Delta+1)V_{A}\right)=-\partial_{x}\left(2\tilde{R}_{1}\tilde{R}_{2}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\overrightarrow{p_{i}}\cdot\overrightarrow{M\tilde{R}_{i}}.

Hence, we deduce that

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(V_{A})

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}V_{A}-\partial_{x}\left(2\tilde{R}_{1}\tilde{R}_{2}\right)+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\overrightarrow{p_{i}}\cdot\overrightarrow{M\tilde{R}_{i}}+\partial_{x}\left(V_{A}^{2}\right).

Therefore, by using (
2.30
), we rearrange the error
\mathcal{E}(V)
in the more concise form (
2.42
), where

S:=2(R_{1}R_{2}-\tilde{R}_{1}\tilde{R}_{2})+2(R_{1}+R_{2})V_{A}+V_{A}^{2}

(2.52)

and

T:=\partial_{t}V_{A}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\overrightarrow{p_{i}}\cdot\left(\overrightarrow{M\tilde{R}_{i}}-\overrightarrow{MR_{i}}\right)

(2.53)

Now we rearrange the term
S
. First, with the notations (
2.33
)-(
2.34
), we decompose the non-linear term
2(R_{1}+R_{2})V_{A}
in the following way

\displaystyle 2(R_{1}+R_{2})V_{A}

\displaystyle=2(R_{1}-\tilde{R}_{1}+R_{2}-\tilde{R}_{2})V_{A}+2\tilde{R}_{1}V_{A,1}+2\tilde{R}_{2}V_{A,2}

\displaystyle\quad+2\tilde{R}_{1}\left(\overrightarrow{p_{2}}\cdot\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{2}}\right)+2\tilde{R}_{2}\left(\overrightarrow{p_{1}}\cdot\left(\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{1}}-\overrightarrow{n}\right)\right).

Thus
S
can be written as in (
2.44
) where

\Sigma=\Sigma_{1}+\Sigma_{2}

and

\displaystyle\Sigma_{1}

\displaystyle:=2\tilde{R}_{1}\overrightarrow{p_{1}}\cdot\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{1}}+2\tilde{R}_{1}F=2\tilde{R}_{1}V_{A,1},

(2.54)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{2}

\displaystyle:=2\tilde{R}_{2}\overrightarrow{p_{2}}\cdot\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{2}}+2\tilde{R}_{2}F+2\tilde{R}_{2}\overrightarrow{p_{1}}\cdot\overrightarrow{n}=2\tilde{R}_{2}V_{A,2},

(2.55)

\displaystyle\Psi_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle:=2\left(\mu_{1}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{1}+\omega_{1}\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{1}\right)V_{A,1}+2\left(\mu_{2}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{2}+\omega_{2}\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{2}\right)V_{A,2}\noindent

\displaystyle\quad+2\left(\mu_{1}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{1}+\omega_{1}\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{1}\right)\tilde{R}_{2}+2\tilde{R}_{1}\left(\mu_{2}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{2}+\omega_{2}\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{2}\right)

(2.56)

\displaystyle\Psi_{S}

\displaystyle:=2\tilde{R}_{1}\left(\overrightarrow{p_{2}}\cdot\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{2}}\right)+2\tilde{R}_{2}\left(\overrightarrow{p_{1}}\cdot\left(\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{1}}-\overrightarrow{n}(y)\right)\right)+2(R_{1}R_{2}-\tilde{R}_{1}\tilde{R}_{2})+V_{A}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+2(R_{1}-\tilde{R}_{1}+R_{2}-\tilde{R}_{2})V_{A}-\Psi_{\Sigma}.

(2.57)

2.5.
Choice of the coefficients
\overrightarrow{p_{i}}

We adjust the coefficients
\overrightarrow{p_{i}}
such that the contributions
\Sigma_{i}
satisfy an adequate orthogonality relation.

Lemma 2.5
.

Under Assumption
2.3
with
Z^{\star}_{0}>0
large enough, for
i=1,2
, there exists a
C^{1}
vector-valued function
\overrightarrow{p_{i}}=\overrightarrow{p_{i}}(z)=(\alpha_{i}(z),\beta_{i}(z),\gamma_{i}(z))^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that, for
t\in I
,

\displaystyle\langle\Sigma_{i},\partial_{x}\tilde{R}_{i}\rangle=\langle\Sigma_{i},\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{i}\rangle=\langle\Sigma_{i},\Lambda\tilde{R}_{i}\rangle=0,

(2.58)

\displaystyle\beta_{i}(z)=0,\quad\gamma_{i}(z)=\frac{-1}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\left\langle Q\left(\cdot-(-1)^{i}\tilde{\mathbf{z}}\right),\partial_{x}(Q^{2})\right\rangle

(2.59)

\displaystyle|\alpha_{i}(z)|+\left|\alpha^{\prime}_{i}(z)\right|+|\beta_{i}(z)|+\left|\beta^{\prime}_{i}(z)\right|+|\gamma_{i}(z)|+|\gamma_{i}^{\prime}(z)|\lesssim z^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-z}.

(2.60)

As a consequence,
\gamma_{2}(z)=-\gamma_{1}(z)
and
\alpha_{2}(z)=\alpha_{1}(z)
.

Proof.

We start with the case
i=1
. We translate the equation of
\Sigma_{1}
in (
2.54
) by
-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{1}=-(z_{1},0)
. Then, we will work on the quantity

\displaystyle\widehat{\Sigma}_{1}(\cdot,z):=\Sigma_{1}(\cdot+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{1})=2Q\overrightarrow{p_{1}}\cdot\overrightarrow{NQ}+2Q\left(-\Delta+1\right)^{-1}\left(2QQ(\cdot+\tilde{\mathbf{z}})\right).

(2.61)

By Lemma
2.2
, there exists a unique
\overrightarrow{p_{1}}=(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\gamma_{1})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that

\displaystyle 2Q\overrightarrow{p_{1}}\cdot\overrightarrow{NQ}+2Q(-\Delta+1)^{-1}\left(2QQ(\cdot+\tilde{\mathbf{z}})\right)\ \perp\ \left\{\partial_{x}Q,\partial_{y}Q,\Lambda Q\right\}.

Moreover, it follows from (
1.5
) and (
2.7
)-(
2.8
) that the coefficients
\overrightarrow{p_{1}}(z)=(\alpha_{1}(z),\beta_{1}(z),\gamma_{1}(z))^{T}
are given by

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=c\left(\langle 2Q(-\Delta+1)^{-1}\left(2QQ(\cdot+\tilde{\mathbf{z}})\right),\Lambda Q\rangle-\frac{\langle 2QW,\Lambda Q\rangle}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\left\langle Q(\cdot+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}),\partial_{x}(Q^{2})\right\rangle\right),

\displaystyle\beta_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=\left(\langle\partial_{x}^{-1}\partial_{y}Q,\partial_{y}Q\rangle\right)^{-1}\left\langle Q(\cdot+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}),\partial_{y}(Q^{2})\right\rangle,

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=-\left(\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle\right)^{-1}\left\langle Q(\cdot+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}),\partial_{x}(Q^{2})\right\rangle,

with
c=\left(\langle Q,\Lambda Q\rangle+\langle(-\Delta+1)^{-1}Q,Q\rangle\right)^{-1}
.
Observe by parity that
\beta_{1}(z)=0
.

Now, we deal with the case
i=2
. We translate the equation of
\Sigma_{2}
in (
2.55
) by
-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2}=-(z_{2},0)
, so that we will focus on the quantity

\displaystyle\widehat{\Sigma}_{2}(\cdot,z):=\Sigma_{2}(\cdot+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2})

\displaystyle:=2Q\overrightarrow{p_{2}}\cdot\overrightarrow{NQ}+2Q(-\Delta+1)^{-1}\left(2QQ(\cdot-\tilde{\mathbf{z}})\right)+2Q\overrightarrow{p_{1}}\cdot\overrightarrow{n}.

(2.62)

As in the case
i=1
, we want to ensure the orthogonality relations (
2.58
) for
i=2
. By Lemma
2.2
, there exists a unique
\overrightarrow{p_{2}}=(\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}^{,}\gamma_{2})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that

\displaystyle 2Q\overrightarrow{p_{2}}\cdot\overrightarrow{NQ}+2Q\left(-\Delta+1\right)^{-1}\left(2QQ(\cdot-\tilde{\mathbf{z}})\right)+2Q\overrightarrow{p_{1}}\cdot\overrightarrow{n}\ \perp\ \left\{\partial_{x}Q,\partial_{y}Q,\Lambda Q\right\}

Since
\beta_{1}(z)=0
,
2Q\overrightarrow{p_{1}}\cdot\overrightarrow{n}=2\gamma_{1}(z)Ql(y)
. Moreover, note from (
2.20
) that
l=l(y)
is even.
Thus, the coefficients
\overrightarrow{p_{2}}(z)=(\alpha_{2}(z),\beta_{2}(z),\gamma_{2}(z))^{T}
are given by

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}(z)

\displaystyle=c\left(\langle 2Q(-\Delta+1)^{-1}\left(2QQ(\cdot-\tilde{\mathbf{z}})\right),\Lambda Q\rangle+2\gamma_{1}(z)\langle Ql(y),\Lambda Q\rangle\right.

\displaystyle\quad\quad\left.-\frac{\langle 2QW,\Lambda Q\rangle}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\left\langle Q(\cdot-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}),\partial_{x}(Q^{2})\right\rangle\right),

\displaystyle\beta_{2}(z)

\displaystyle=\left(\langle\partial_{x}^{-1}\partial_{y}Q,\partial_{y}Q\rangle\right)^{-1}\left\langle Q(\cdot-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}),\partial_{y}(Q^{2})\right\rangle,

\displaystyle\gamma_{2}(z)

\displaystyle=-\left(\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle\right)^{-1}\left\langle Q(\cdot-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}),\partial_{x}(Q^{2})\right\rangle.

with
c=\left(\langle Q,\Lambda Q\rangle+\langle(-\Delta+1)^{-1}Q,Q\rangle\right)^{-1}
.
Since
Q
is radial, we have
Q(\mathbf{x}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}})=Q(\tilde{\mathbf{z}}-\mathbf{x})
. Hence, it follows by parity that
\beta_{2}(z)=0
,
\gamma_{2}(z)=-\gamma_{1}(z)
and

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}(z)

\displaystyle=c\left(\langle 2Q(-\Delta+1)^{-1}\left(2QQ(\cdot+\tilde{\mathbf{z}})\right),\Lambda Q\rangle+\frac{\langle 2Q(W-l),\Lambda Q\rangle}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\left\langle Q(\cdot+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}),\partial_{x}(Q^{2})\right\rangle\right)=\alpha_{1}(z),

where we used on the last step that
W(x,y)-l(y)=-W(-x,y)
.

Finally, estimate
(
2.60
) follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and estimate (
C.3
).
This concludes the proof of Lemma
2.5
.
∎

2.6.
Estimates for the components of the approximate solution

Since
\alpha_{1}(z)=\alpha_{2}(z)
and
\gamma_{1}(z)=-\gamma_{2}(z)
(see Lemma
2.5
), we can rewrite
V_{A}
as

V_{A}=F+\alpha_{1}(z)\sum_{i=1}^{2}X_{i}+\gamma_{1}(z)P,

where
X_{1}
,
X_{2}
and
P
are defined in (
2.19
) and (
2.21
), so that
V_{A}(\mathbf{x})\to 0
as
|\mathbf{x}|\to\infty
. Finally estimates (
2.35
)–(
2.37
) are a consequence of the following claims combined with (
2.60
).

Claim 2.6
.

Let
P
be defined as in (
2.21
). Then
P\in C^{1}(I:H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
. Moreover, we have, for all
t\in I
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\left|P(t,\mathbf{x})\right|+\left|\nabla P(t,\mathbf{x})\right|\lesssim 1;

(2.63)

\displaystyle\left\|P(t,\cdot)\right\|_{H^{2}}\lesssim z^{\frac{3}{2}};

(2.64)

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}P(t,\cdot)\right\|_{H^{2}}\lesssim|\dot{z}_{1}|+|\dot{z}_{2}|.

(2.65)

Proof.

First, thanks to the assumptions on
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
in (
2.3
) and Lemma
A.6
, we rewrite
P
as

P(t,\mathbf{x})=\int_{x-z_{1}}^{x-z_{2}}\left(-\Delta+1\right)^{-1}\Lambda Q(\tilde{x},y)d\tilde{x}=\left(-\Delta+1\right)^{-1}\int_{x-z_{1}}^{x-z_{2}}\Lambda Q(\tilde{x},y)d\tilde{x}.

Since
\left(-\Delta+1\right)^{-1}\Lambda Q(\tilde{x},y)\in\mathcal{Y}
(see Lemma
A.2
), we have
\left(-\Delta+1\right)^{-1}\Lambda Q(\tilde{x},y)\lesssim e^{-\frac{|y|}{2}}e^{-\frac{|\tilde{x}|}{2}}
, so that

\left|P(t,\mathbf{x})\right|\lesssim\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}e^{-\frac{|\tilde{x}|}{2}}d\tilde{x}\lesssim 1,

which proves the first estimate in (
2.63
). The proof of the second estimate in (
2.63
) follows arguing in a similar way.

Next, we claim that

\left|\int_{x-z_{1}}^{x-z_{2}}\Lambda Q(\tilde{x},y)d\tilde{x}\right|\lesssim e^{-\frac{|y|}{2}}\left(z{\bf 1}_{2z_{2}<x<2z_{1}}+e^{-\frac{|x|}{4}}\right).

(2.66)

which implies (
2.64
) by using that
(-\Delta+1)^{-1}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is a bounded operator.
To prove (
2.66
), we first observe that
\left|\Lambda Q(\tilde{x},y)\right|\lesssim e^{-\frac{|\tilde{x}|}{2}}e^{-\frac{|y|}{2}}
since
\Lambda Q\in\mathcal{Y}
. On the one hand, in the case
2z_{2}<\tilde{x}<2z_{1}
, we have

\left|\int_{x-z_{1}}^{x-z_{2}}\Lambda Q(\tilde{x},y)d\tilde{x}\right|\lesssim e^{-\frac{|y|}{2}}\int_{x-z_{1}}^{x-z_{2}}d\tilde{x}=ze^{-\frac{|y|}{2}}.

On the other hand, in the case where
x>2z_{1}>0
, we have
x-z_{1}>x/2
, so that

\left|\int_{x-z_{1}}^{x-z_{2}}\Lambda Q(\tilde{x},y)d\tilde{x}\right|\lesssim e^{-\frac{|y|}{2}}\int_{x/2}^{+\infty}e^{-\frac{|\tilde{x}|}{2}}d\tilde{x}\lesssim e^{-\frac{|x|}{4}}e^{-\frac{|y|}{2}}.

The estimate is similar in the case where
x<2z_{2}<0
.

Finally, observe that

\partial_{t}P(t,\mathbf{x})=\dot{z}_{1}\left(-\Delta+1\right)^{-1}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{1}-\dot{z}_{2}\left(-\Delta+1\right)^{-1}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{2}.

This, combined with the fact that
\Lambda Q\in\mathcal{Y}
and
(-\Delta+1)^{-1}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is a bounded operator concludes the proof of (
2.65
).
∎

Claim 2.7
.

Let
F
be defined as in (
2.32
). Then
F\in C^{1}(I:H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
. Moreover, we have, for all
t\in I
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\left|F(t,\mathbf{x})\right|+\left|\nabla F(t,\mathbf{x})\right|\lesssim z^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-z};

(2.67)

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{t}F(t,\mathbf{x})\right|+\left|\nabla\partial_{t}F(t,\mathbf{x})\right|\lesssim\left(|\dot{z}_{1}|+|\dot{z}_{2}|\right)z^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-z};

(2.68)

\displaystyle\left\|F(t,\cdot)\right\|_{H^{2}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{15}{16}z};

(2.69)

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}F(t,\cdot)\right\|_{H^{2}}\lesssim\left(|\dot{z}_{1}|+|\dot{z}_{2}|\right)e^{-\frac{15}{16}z}.

(2.70)

Proof.

Estimate (
2.67
) and (
2.68
) are consequences of (
C.3
), (
A.9
) and of

\partial_{t}F=-2\dot{z}_{1}\left(-\Delta+1\right)^{-1}\left((\partial_{x}\tilde{R}_{1})\tilde{R}_{2}\right)-2\dot{z}_{2}\left(-\Delta+1\right)^{-1}\left(\tilde{R}_{1}(\partial_{x}\tilde{R}_{2})\right).

Thus, estimates (
2.69
) and (
2.70
) follow combining the fact that
(-\Delta+1)^{-1}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to H^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is a bounded operator with (
C.2
).
∎

Finally, we prove (
2.38
).
Without loss of generality, we assume that
i=1
. We deduce from the definition of
V
in (
2.25
) and estimates (
2.36
) and (
C.7
) that

\left\||\overrightarrow{MR_{1}}|(V-R_{1})\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\left\||\overrightarrow{MR_{1}}|R_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\|V_{A}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{15}{16}z}.

2.7.
Estimates for the error terms

Estimate for
\Sigma
.
It follows from the definition of
\Sigma=\Sigma_{1}+\Sigma_{2}
(see (
2.54
)-(
2.55
)) that

\|\Sigma\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(|\alpha_{i}(z)|+|\gamma_{i}(z)|\right)+\|Q\|_{H^{1}}\left(\|F\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla F\|_{L^{\infty}}\right),

which, combined to (
2.41
) and (
2.67
) implies (
2.45
).

Estimate for
\Psi_{\Sigma}
.
It follows from the definition of
\Psi_{\Sigma}
in (
2.56
) that

\displaystyle\|\Psi_{\Sigma}\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(|\alpha_{i}(z)|+|\gamma_{i}(z)|\right)+\|F\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla F\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(|\mu_{i}|+|\omega_{i}|\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\|\Lambda\tilde{R}_{1}\tilde{R}_{2}\|_{H^{1}}+\|\tilde{R}_{1}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{2}\|_{H^{1}}+\|\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{1}\tilde{R}_{2}\|_{H^{1}}+\|\tilde{R}_{1}\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{2}\|_{H^{1}}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(|\mu_{i}|+|\omega_{i}|\right),

which, combined to (
2.41
), (
2.67
) and (
C.7
), implies (
2.46
).

Estimate for
\Psi_{S}
.
By using (
2.57
), we rewrite
\Psi_{S}
as

\begin{split}\Psi_{S}&=2\tilde{R}_{1}\left(\overrightarrow{p_{2}}\cdot\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{2}}\right)+2\tilde{R}_{2}\left(\overrightarrow{p_{1}}\cdot\left(\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{1}}-\overrightarrow{n}(y)\right)\right)+2\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(R_{i}-\tilde{R}_{i}-\mu_{i}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{i}-\omega_{i}\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{i}\right)V_{A,i}\\
&\quad+V_{A}^{2}+2\left(R_{1}R_{2}-\tilde{R}_{1}\tilde{R}_{2}-\left(\mu_{1}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{1}+\omega_{1}\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{1}\right)\tilde{R}_{2}-\tilde{R}_{1}\left(\mu_{2}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{2}+\omega_{2}\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{2}\right)\right)\\
&\quad+2\left(R_{1}-\tilde{R}_{1}\right)\left(\overrightarrow{p_{2}}\cdot\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{2}}\right)+2\left(R_{2}-\tilde{R}_{2}\right)\left(\overrightarrow{p_{1}}\cdot\left(\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{1}}-\overrightarrow{n}\right)\right)\end{split}

(2.71)

We estimate separately each term in (
2.71
). These estimates are gathered in the next claim, which concludes the proof of (
2.47
).

Claim 2.8
.

The following estimates hold.

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{R}_{1}\left(\overrightarrow{p_{2}}\cdot\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{2}}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|\tilde{R}_{2}\left(\overrightarrow{p_{1}}\cdot\left(\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{1}}-\overrightarrow{n}\right)\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{31}{16}z};

(2.72)

\displaystyle\left\|V_{A}^{2}\right\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{31}{16}z};

(2.73)

\displaystyle\left\|R_{1}R_{2}-\tilde{R}_{1}\tilde{R}_{2}-\left(\mu_{1}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{1}+\omega_{1}\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{1}\right)\tilde{R}_{2}-\tilde{R}_{1}\left(\mu_{2}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{2}+\omega_{2}\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{2}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{15}{16}z}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(\mu_{i}^{2}+\omega_{i}^{2}\right);

(2.74)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left\|\left(R_{i}-\tilde{R}_{i}-\mu_{i}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{i}-\omega_{i}\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{i}\right)V_{A,i}\right\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim z^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-z}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(\mu_{i}^{2}+\omega_{i}^{2}\right);

(2.75)

\displaystyle\left\|(R_{1}-\tilde{R}_{1})\left(\overrightarrow{p_{2}}\cdot\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{2}}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|(R_{2}-\tilde{R}_{2})\left(\overrightarrow{p_{1}}\cdot\left(\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{1}}-\overrightarrow{n}\right)\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{31}{16}z}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(|\mu_{i}|+|\omega_{i}|\right).

(2.76)

Proof.

We have from the definition of
\overrightarrow{p_{i}}
in (
2.31
) and the ones of
\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{i}}
and
\overrightarrow{n}
in (
2.22
) that

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{R}_{1}\left(\overrightarrow{p_{2}}\cdot\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{2}}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}\leq|\alpha_{2}(z)|\left\|\tilde{R}_{1}X_{2}\right\|_{H^{1}}+|\gamma_{2}(z)|\left\|\tilde{R}_{1}W_{2}\right\|_{H^{1}};

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{R}_{2}\left(\overrightarrow{p_{1}}\cdot\left(\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{1}}-\overrightarrow{n}(y)\right)\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}\leq|\alpha_{1}(z)|\left\|\tilde{R}_{2}X_{1}\right\|_{H^{1}}+|\gamma_{1}(z)|\left\|\tilde{R}_{2}\left(W_{1}-l\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}.

Therefore, we conclude the proof of (
2.72
) by gathering (
2.60
), (
C.9
), (
C.10
) and (
C.11
).

Estimate (
2.73
) follows from (
2.35
)-(
2.36
), while estimate (
2.74
) is proved in Lemma
C.4
.

Next, we deduce from the definition of
V_{A,1}
in (
2.33
) that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2}

\displaystyle\left\|\left(R_{i}-\tilde{R}_{i}-\mu_{i}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{i}-\omega_{i}\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{i}\right)V_{A,i}\right\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left\|\left(R_{i}-\tilde{R}_{i}-\mu_{i}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{i}-\omega_{i}\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{i}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(|\alpha_{i}|+|\gamma_{i}|\right)+\|F\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\nabla F\|_{L^{\infty}}\right).

Thus, we conclude the proof of (
2.75
) by using (
2.60
), (
2.67
) and (
C.5
).

Finally, the proof of (
2.76
) follows as the one of (
2.72
) by using Remark
C.6
instead of Lemma
C.5
.
∎

Estimate for
T
.
We deduce from the definition of
T
in (
2.53
) that

\|T\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim\|\partial_{t}V_{A}\|_{H^{1}}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(|\alpha_{i}(z)|\|\partial_{x}R_{i}-\partial_{x}\tilde{R}_{i}\|_{H^{1}}+|\gamma_{i}(z)|\|\Lambda R_{i}-\Lambda\tilde{R}_{i}\|_{H^{1}}\right).

Therefore, we conclude the proof of (
2.48
) by combining this estimate with (
2.37
), (
2.41
) and (
C.6
).

Estimate for
\partial_{t}S
.

The time derivative of
S
decomposed as in (
2.44
) gives

\displaystyle\partial_{t}S

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\Sigma+\partial_{t}\Psi_{\Sigma}+\partial_{t}\Psi_{S}.

For the first term, from (
2.54
), (
2.31
), (
2.60
) and (
2.67
)-(
2.68
), it holds

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}\Sigma_{1}\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim\left|\dot{z}\partial_{z}\overrightarrow{p_{1}}(z)\right|+\left|\dot{z}_{1}\right|\left(\left|\overrightarrow{p_{1}}\right|+\left\|F\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)+\left\|\partial_{t}F\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\left(|\dot{z}_{1}|+|\dot{z}_{2}|\right)z^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-z}.

The same estimate holds for
\partial_{t}\Sigma_{2}
. Proceeding similarly for
\Psi_{\Sigma}
and
\Psi_{S}
, we get

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}\Psi_{\Sigma}\right\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(|\dot{\mu}_{i}|+|\dot{\omega}_{i}|\right)e^{-\frac{15}{16}z}+\left(|\dot{z}_{1}|+|\dot{z}_{2}|\right)\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(|\mu_{i}|+|\omega_{i}|\right)e^{-\frac{15}{16}z},

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}\Psi_{S}\right\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(|\dot{z}_{1}|+|\dot{z}_{2}|\right)\left(e^{-\frac{31}{16}z}+e^{-\frac{15}{16}z}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(\mu_{i}^{2}+\omega_{i}^{2}\right)\right)+e^{-\frac{15}{16}z}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(|\dot{\mu}_{i}|+|\dot{\omega}_{i}|\right)\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(|\mu_{i}|+|\omega_{i}|\right).

Gathering the three previous inequalities proves (
2.49
).

Estimate for
\langle\Sigma,\overrightarrow{M\tilde{R}_{i}}\rangle
.
We focus on the term
\langle\Sigma,\partial_{x}\tilde{R}_{2}\rangle
. By using the definition of
\Sigma=\Sigma_{1}+\Sigma_{2}
in (
2.54
)-(
2.54
) and the orthogonality relation (
2.58
), we have

\displaystyle\left|\langle\Sigma,\partial_{x}\tilde{R}_{2}\rangle\right|=\left|\langle\Sigma_{1},\partial_{x}\tilde{R}_{2}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\left|\langle\tilde{R}_{1}\overrightarrow{p_{1}}\cdot\overrightarrow{N\tilde{R}_{1}},\partial_{x}\tilde{R}_{2}\rangle\right|+2\left|\langle\tilde{R}_{1}F,\partial_{x}\tilde{R}_{2}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(|\alpha_{1}(z)|+|\gamma_{1}(z)|+\|F\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\|\tilde{R}_{1}\partial_{x}\tilde{R}_{2}\|_{L^{1}}.

Hence, we conclude the proof of (
2.50
) by combining this estimate with (
2.41
), (
2.67
) and (
C.7
).

Estimate for
\langle S,\overrightarrow{MR_{i}}\rangle
.

The proof of (
2.51
) follows by gathering (
2.50
) with (
2.45
), (
2.46
), (
2.47
) and (
C.6
).

3.
Decomposition of a solution close to the approximate solution

Consider a solution
v
of (
1.10
) that is close to the approximate solution
V
constructed in Section
2
on a certain time interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
. Recall that is
V
is on the form
V=R_{1}+R_{2}+V_{A}
(see (
2.25
)), where
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
are defined in (
2.13
). Thus,
V
depends on time through the set of geometric parameters
\Gamma=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},z_{1},z_{2},\omega_{1},\omega_{2})
,
i.e.

V(t,\mathbf{x})=V(\mathbf{x};\Gamma(t))
.

In all this section, we assume that the geometric parameters satisfy the rough estimates in Assumption
2.3
and that
V_{A}(t,\mathbf{x})=V_{A}(\mathbf{x};\Gamma(t))
satisfies (
2.35
), (
2.36
), (
2.37
), (
2.38
). We also assume that the error
\mathcal{E}_{V}
decomposes as in (
2.42
), where
\overrightarrow{m_{i}}
is defined in (
2.43
