\displaystyle\|\epsilon(t)\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\lesssim\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{2}}+C_{1}\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{4}}e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(T_{2})}+C_{1}^{2}\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{2}}e^{-\rho Z(T_{2})}+C_{1}^{3}\mu_{0}^{\frac{17}{4}}Z(t)\lesssim\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{2}}

(4.73)

by using that
T_{1}\geq t
in (
4.4
) so that
Z(t)\leq Z(T_{1})\lesssim Z_{0}
and
Z(T_{2})\geq Z_{0}
.

Closing the bound on
\bar{\mu}
and
\bar{z}
on
[T_{2},T^{\star})
.
From (
4.24
)-(
4.25
) and (
4.73
), we have

\displaystyle\left|\dot{\bar{\mu}}\right|\lesssim\mu_{0}e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z}+\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{2}},\quad\text{and}\quad\left|\dot{\bar{z}}-\bar{\mu}\right|\lesssim Z^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-Z}+\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{4}}.

(4.74)

By multiplying the right-hand side of (
4.74
) by
\dot{Z}(t)\mu_{0}^{-1}\gtrsim 1
, we get with (
4.16
) at time
T_{2}
, for any
t\in[T_{2};T^{\star})
,

\displaystyle\left|\bar{\mu}(t)\right|\lesssim\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{4}}\quad\text{and}\quad\left|\bar{z}(t)\right|\lesssim Z_{0}\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}}.

(4.75)

Closing the bound on
\omega
and
\bar{\omega}
on
[T_{2},T^{\star})
.
From (
4.47
), with
c_{Q}\neq 0
, it holds with (
4.73
)

\left|\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{K}_{1}-c_{Q}\dot{\omega}_{1}\right|+\left|\frac{d}{dt}\left(\mathcal{K}_{2}-2\mathcal{K}_{1}\right)-c_{Q}\dot{\omega}_{2}\right|\lesssim\mu_{0}^{\frac{9}{4}}.

(4.76)

By using
\dot{Z}(t)\gtrsim\mu_{0}
, an integration from
T_{2}
to
t
gives, with (
4.33
), (
4.73
) and (
4.71
)

\displaystyle\left|\omega_{1}(t)-\frac{1}{2}\omega_{0}\right|+\left|\omega_{2}(t)+\frac{1}{2}\omega_{0}\right|\lesssim Z_{0}\mu_{0}^{\frac{5}{4}}.

(4.77)

To improve the bounds on
\mu
and
z
, we introduce the time
T_{4}\in(T_{2},T_{1})
defined as the unique time such that

\displaystyle\mu_{0}^{2}=Z_{0}MZ(T_{4})^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-Z(T_{4})},

(4.78)

where
M>10
is a constant to be chosen later. Note that
T_{4}
is well-defined thanks to the intermediate value theorem.

Closing the bound on
\mu
and
z
on
[T_{2},T^{\star})
with
T^{\star}<T_{4}
.
As in the proof of (
4.18
), we use the functional
H
defined in (
4.59
). We claim that

\displaystyle\left|H(T_{2})-2\mu_{0}^{2}\right|\lesssim\mu_{0}^{\frac{11}{4}}.

(4.79)

Indeed, as in the proof of (
4.60
), we use (
4.18
) at time
t=T_{2}
to obtain

\displaystyle\left|\mu(T_{2})^{2}-\dot{Z}(T_{2})^{2}\right|\lesssim\mu_{0}^{\frac{11}{4}}

and with (
D.8
) and (
4.18
) at time
t=T_{2}

\displaystyle\left|\int\left(Q(\cdot+z(T_{2}),\cdot)-Q(\cdot+Z(T_{2}),\cdot)Q^{2}\right)\right|\lesssim\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}}Z(T_{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-Z(T_{2})}\lesssim\mu_{0}^{\frac{11}{4}},

which concludes the proof of (
4.79
).

The time derivative of
H
is bounded, by using (
4.62
), (
4.24
)-(
4.25
), (
4.26
) and (
4.73
)

\displaystyle\left|\dot{H}\right|\lesssim\mu_{0}^{2}e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z}+\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{4}}Z^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-Z}+\mu_{0}^{\frac{9}{2}}.

After multiplying the right-hand side of the above inequality by
\dot{Z}(t)\mu_{0}^{-1}\gtrsim 1
, an integration from
T_{2}
to
t
provides, with
Z(t)\leq Z(T_{1})\lesssim Z_{0}
and (
4.79
),

\displaystyle\left|H(t)-2\mu_{0}^{2}\right|\lesssim\mu_{0}^{\frac{11}{4}}\lesssim MZ_{0}\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}}Z(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-Z(t)},

where we used the definition of
T_{4}
in (
4.78
) in the last step.
From (
4.20
), (
4.19
) and (
D.8
), we have

\displaystyle Z(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-Z(t)}\lesssim\frac{2}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q(x+z(t),y)Q^{2}(x,y)dxdy,

which, combined to (
4.24
), (
4.73
), (
4.19
), (
4.78
) and
\dot{Z}(t)\lesssim\mu_{0}
, yields

\displaystyle\left|\mu(t)^{2}-\dot{z}(t)^{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\mu(t)-\dot{z}(t)\right|\left(\left|\dot{z}(t)-\mu(t)\right|+2\left|\mu(t)-\dot{Z}(t)\right|+2\dot{Z}(T_{1})\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mu_{0}^{\frac{11}{4}}\lesssim MZ_{0}\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}}Z(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-Z(t)}.

It follows from the three previous inequalities that, by defining
\nu:=CMZ_{0}\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}}
for a constant
C
large enough,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\dot{z}(t)^{2}+\frac{2(1-\nu)}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q(x+z(t),y)Q^{2}(x,y)dxdy

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 2\mu_{0}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\dot{z}(t)^{2}+\frac{2(1+\nu)}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q(x+z(t),y)Q^{2}(x,y)dxdy.

With (
4.18
) at time
T_{2}
, we can apply Proposition
D.10
with
\epsilon_{0}=C\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}}
to obtain for any
t\in[T_{2},T^{\star})
,

\displaystyle\left|z(t)-Z(t)\right|\lesssim MZ_{0}\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}}.

(4.80)

Thus from (
4.56
), (
4.25
) and (
4.73
), we have

\displaystyle\left|\dot{\mu}-\ddot{Z}\right|=\left|\dot{\mu}+2\gamma_{1}(Z)\right|\lesssim MZ_{0}\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}}Z^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-Z}+\mu_{0}e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z}+\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{2}}.

We multiply the right-hand side by
\dot{Z}(t)\mu_{0}^{-1}\gtrsim 1
, integrate from
T_{2}
to
t
and use (
4.18
) to find

\displaystyle\left|\mu(t)-\dot{Z}(t)\right|\lesssim MZ_{0}\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{4}}.

(4.81)

By choosing
C_{1}>1
large enough dependent on
M
, using (
4.73
), (
4.75
), (
4.77
), (
4.80
) and (
4.81
), we strictly improve the bootstrap inequalities (
4.19
). Hence, we conclude that
T^{\star}>T_{4}
.

Closing the bound on
\mu
and
z
on
[T_{4},T^{\star})
.
On this time interval we will need to fix
M
large enough. From (
4.56
) and (
4.19
), we have

\displaystyle\left|\gamma_{1}(z)-\gamma_{1}(Z)\right|\lesssim|z-Z|\left|\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(Z)\right|\lesssim C_{1}Z_{0}^{2}\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}}Z^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-Z},

and thus from (
4.25
) and (
4.73
),

\displaystyle\left|\dot{\mu}-\ddot{Z}\right|=\left|\dot{\mu}+2\gamma_{1}(Z)\right|\lesssim\mu_{0}e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z}+\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{2}}+C_{1}Z_{0}^{2}\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}}Z^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-Z}.

By multiplying the right-hand side by
\dot{Z}(t)\mu_{0}^{-1}\gtrsim 1
, we integrate from
T_{4}
to
t\in[T_{4},T^{\star})
with the initial condition (
4.81
) at time
T_{4}
to obtain

\displaystyle\left|\mu(t)-\dot{Z}(t)\right|\lesssim\left(C_{1}M^{-1}+M\right)Z_{0}\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{4}}.

As a consequence, using (
4.25
), we have

\displaystyle\left|\dot{z}(t)-\dot{Z}(t)\right|\lesssim\left(C_{1}M^{-1}+M\right)Z_{0}\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{4}}.

We multiply the right-hand side by
\dot{Z}(t)\mu_{0}^{-1}\gtrsim 1
, use (
4.80
) and integrate from
T_{4}
to
t\in[T_{4},T^{\star})
to find

\displaystyle\left|z(t)-Z(t)\right|\lesssim\left(C_{1}M^{-1}+M\right)Z_{0}^{2}\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}}.

Now, we choose
M
(independent of
C_{1}
) large enough and then
C_{1}
large so that the two following inequalities are satisfied

\displaystyle\left|\mu(t)-\dot{Z}(t)\right|\leq\frac{C_{1}}{2}Z_{0}\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{4}},\quad\left|z(t)-Z(t)\right|\leq\frac{C_{1}}{2}Z_{0}^{2}\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}}.

Increasing
C_{1}
if necessary in (
4.73
), (
4.75
) and (
4.77
) strictly improves the bounds of (
4.19
) on
[T_{4},T^{\star})
. As a consequence,
T^{\star}=T_{1}
, which concludes the proof of Proposition
4.3
.

5.
Dynamics of the two-solitary waves structure

The aim of this section is to study the dynamics of the two-solitary waves structure
v
solution to (
1.10
) behaving at time
t=-\infty
as a sum of two well-prepared symmetric solitary waves (see (
1.11
)).
In the first subsection, we prove a quantitative orbital stability result on a finite time interval as long as the two solitary waves remain far enough. In Subsection
5.2
, we prove Theorem
1.5
by describing the evolution of the solution
v
from
-\infty
to
+\infty
. Finally, Subsection
5.3
is devoted to the proof of Theorem
1.7
.

In all this section, for
\mu_{0}
small, we use the function
Z
defined in Lemma (
4.1
) with the asymptote
2\mu_{0}t+l
at
+\infty
and its properties stated in Subsection
4.1
.

5.1.
Orbital stability of the
2
-solitary waves structure for large negative time

We state below the main result of this subsection.

Proposition 5.1
.

There exist
\nu_{4}^{\star}>0
and
C>0
such that the following holds. Let
\mu_{0}\in(0,\nu_{4}^{\star})
and
\kappa
be a continuous increasing function
\kappa:\mathbb{R}_{-}\rightarrow(0,1)
satisfying

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow-\infty}t\kappa(t)=0.

(5.1)

Let
Z
,
T_{1}
,
l
and
K
be defined as in Lemma
4.1
and Lemma
4.2
. There exists a time
\tilde{t}_{0}
satisfying

\displaystyle-\tilde{t}_{0}<\min(-T_{1},-\frac{l+K}{\mu_{0}})

(5.2)

such that, if
t_{0}>\tilde{t}_{0}
,
|\omega_{0}|\leq\mu_{0}
and
w\in\mathcal{C}([-t_{0},-T_{1}],H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
is a solution to (
1.10
) satisfying

\displaystyle\left\|w(-t_{0})-\sum_{i=1}^{2}Q_{1+(-1)^{i}\mu_{0}}\left(\cdot+\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}Z(-t_{0}),\cdot+\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}\omega_{0}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}\leq\kappa(-t_{0})\mu_{0},

(5.3)

then there exists a unique function
\Gamma=(z_{1},z_{2},\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in\mathcal{C}^{1}([-t_{0},-T_{1}]:\mathbb{R}^{6})
, such that by defining
\epsilon
as in (
3.1
), we have, for any
t\in[-t_{0},-T_{1}]
and for any
i=1,2
,

\displaystyle\left\|\epsilon(t)\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left|\mu_{i}(t)+\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}\dot{Z}(t)\right|\leq C\left(\kappa(-t_{0})\mu_{0}+e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)}\right),

(5.4)

\displaystyle\left|z_{i}(t)+\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}Z(t)\right|+\left|\omega_{i}(t)+\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}\omega_{0}\right|\leq C\left(\kappa(-t_{0})t_{0}+Z(t)^{-\frac{1}{4}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}Z(t)}\right).

Moreover, the following inequality holds, for any
i=1,2
and
t\in[-t_{0},T_{1}]

\displaystyle\left|\dot{z}_{i}(t)-\mu_{i}(t)\right|+|\dot{\omega}_{i}(t)|\leq C\left(\kappa(-t_{0})\mu_{0}+e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)}\right).

(5.5)

Remark 5.2
.

The neighbourhood of the
2
-solitary waves for which the stability result applies is quantified by the function
\kappa
introduced in Proposition
5.1
.

Proof.

For any
t\geq-t_{0}
, as long as
w(t)
stays close to a rescaled approximation
V(\Gamma)
, we introduce the decomposition
(\Gamma(t),\epsilon(t))
of the solution
w(t)
as constructed in Lemma
3.1
. Let us decompose the solution at time
t=-t_{0}
. From assumption (
5.3
), set

\displaystyle\Gamma_{0}:=\left(\frac{1}{2}Z(-t_{0}),-\frac{1}{2}Z(-t_{0}),\frac{\omega_{0}}{2},-\frac{\omega_{0}}{2},\frac{1}{2}\dot{Z}(-t_{0}),-\frac{1}{2}\dot{Z}(-t_{0})\right).

Thus, by continuity of
w
in
H^{1}
, we obtain from (
2.36
) and the triangle inequality that

\displaystyle w(t)\in\mathcal{U}_{\frac{1}{2}Z(t_{0}),2\mu_{0},\eta},\quad\text{where}\quad\eta=\kappa(-t_{0})\mu_{0}+Ce^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(-t_{0})},

for
t
in a small neighbourhood of
t_{0}
. By choosing the parameter
\nu_{4}^{\star}
small enough, one can then apply Lemma
3.1
. As noticed in Remark
3.2
, we have

\displaystyle\|\epsilon(-t_{0})\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim\kappa(-t_{0})\mu_{0}+e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(-t_{0})},

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(\left|z_{i}(-t_{0})+\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}Z(-t_{0})\right|+\left|\omega_{i}(-t_{0})+\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}\omega_{0}\right|+\left|\mu_{i}(-t_{0})+\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}\dot{Z}(-t_{0})\right|\right)

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\kappa(-t_{0})\mu_{0}+e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(-t_{0})}.

(5.6)

In particular, by the definition of
\mathcal{F}_{-}
in (
3.26
), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{-}(-t_{0})\lesssim\left(\kappa(-t_{0})\mu_{0}+e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(-t_{0})}\right)^{2}.

(5.7)

We now prove the rest of the proposition by a bootstrap argument. For a positive constant
C_{\star}>0
to be chosen later, we introduce the bootstrap estimates

\displaystyle\left\|\epsilon(t)\right\|_{H^{1}}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left|\mu_{i}(t)+\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}\dot{Z}(t)\right|\leq C_{\star}\left(\kappa(-t_{0})\mu_{0}+e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)}\right),

(5.8)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(\left|z_{i}(t)+\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}Z(t)\right|+\left|\omega_{i}(t)+\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}\omega_{0}\right|\right)\leq C_{\star}\left(\kappa(-t_{0})t_{0}+Z(t)^{-\frac{1}{4}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}Z(t)}\right).

(5.9)

We define

T^{\star}:=\sup\left\{T\in[-t_{0},-T_{1})\,:\,\text{for all }t\in[-t_{0},T),\quad w(t)\in\mathcal{U}_{2Z_{1}^{\star},\frac{1}{2}\nu_{1}^{\star},\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{\star}}\text{ and }\eqref{estimates:stability_bootstrap}-\eqref{estimates:stability_bootstrap_z_i}\text{ hold}\right\},

where
Z_{1}^{\star}
,
\nu_{1}^{\star}
and
\sigma^{\star}
are the parameters defined in Lemma
3.1
. Observe from (
5.3
) and the continuity of
t\mapsto w(t)
in
H^{1}
that
T^{\star}
is well-defined.

To prove that
T^{\star}=-T_{1}
, for
C_{\star}
large enough, we argue by contradiction and assume that
T^{\star}<-T_{1}
. Then estimates (
5.8
)-(
5.9
) hold on
[-t_{0},T^{\star})
. We will prove that these estimates can be strictly improved on
[-t_{0},T^{\star})
to obtain a contradiction.

On the interval
[-t_{0},T^{\star})
, we have, recalling
z(t)=z_{1}(t)-z_{2}(t)
,

\displaystyle\left|z(t)-Z(t)\right|\lesssim C_{\star}\left(\kappa(-t_{0})t_{0}+Z(t)^{-\frac{1}{4}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}Z(t)}\right).

(5.10)

Note that by choosing
\tilde{t}_{0}
large and
\nu_{3}^{\star}
both dependent on
C_{\star}
, we obtain
|z(t)-Z(t)|\leq 1
. Hence, we deduce from the mean value theorem

\displaystyle\left|z(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-z(t)}-Z(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-Z(t)}\right|\lesssim|z(t)-Z(t)|Z(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-Z(t)}.

(5.11)

As a consequence, it follows from Proposition
2.4
(v) and (vi) that, for
t\in[-t_{0},T^{\star})
,

\displaystyle\left\|S(t)\right\|_{H^{2}}\lesssim Z(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-Z(t)}+e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(|\mu_{i}(t)|+|\omega_{i}(t)|\right),

\displaystyle\left\|T(t)\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left|\left\langle S,\partial_{x}R_{i}\right\rangle\right|+\left|\left\langle S,\partial_{y}R_{i}\right\rangle\right|+\left|\left\langle S,\Lambda R_{i}\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim e^{-\frac{31}{16}Z(t)}+e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(|\mu_{i}(t)|+|\omega_{i}(t)|+|\dot{z}_{i}(t)|\right),

which provides with (
5.8
), (
5.9
) and recalling that
|\dot{Z}(t)|\leq 2\mu_{0}
(see Lemma
4.1
),

\displaystyle\|S(t)\|_{H^{2}}\lesssim Z(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-Z(t)}+\left(\mu_{0}+C_{\star}\kappa(-t_{0})t_{0}\right)e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)},

(5.12)

\displaystyle\left\|T(t)\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left|\left\langle S,\partial_{x}R_{i}\right\rangle\right|+\left|\left\langle S,\partial_{y}R_{i}\right\rangle\right|+\left|\left\langle S,\Lambda R_{i}\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\mu_{0}e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)}+C_{\star}\left(Z(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\frac{23}{16}Z(t)}+\kappa(-t_{0})t_{0}Z(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-Z(t)}\right)+e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)}\sum_{i=1}^{2}|\dot{z}_{i}(t)|.

(5.13)

From (
3.7
)-(
3.8
) combined with (
2.40
), (
4.1
), (
5.8
), (
5.10
)-(
5.13
) and (
D.8
), we obtain the quantified dynamical system, for
i=1,2
,

\displaystyle\left|\dot{z}_{i}(t)+\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}\dot{Z}(t)\right|

\displaystyle+\left|\dot{\omega_{i}}(t)\right|\lesssim C_{\star}\left(\kappa(-t_{0})\mu_{0}+e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)}\right),

(5.14)

\displaystyle\left|\dot{\mu}_{i}(t)+\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}\ddot{Z}(t)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\mu_{0}e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)}+C_{\star}Z(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\frac{23}{16}Z(t)}+C_{\star}\kappa(-t_{0})\mu_{0}e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)}

\displaystyle\quad+C_{\star}\kappa(-t_{0})t_{0}Z(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-Z(t)}+C_{\star}^{2}\left(\kappa(-t_{0})\mu_{0}\right)^{2}-\dot{Z}(t)e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)}.

(5.15)

First, we estimate
\mu_{i}
, for
i=1,2
. By multiplying the three first terms of the right-hand side of (
5.15
) by
-\dot{Z}(t)\mu_{0}^{-1}\geq 1
(see (
4.5
)) and by integrating from
-t_{0}
to
t
, we have combining with (
5.6
),

\displaystyle\left|\mu_{i}(t)+\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}\dot{Z}(t)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\kappa(-t_{0})\mu_{0}+e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)}+C_{\star}\left(\frac{e^{-\frac{23}{16}Z(t)}}{\mu_{0}Z^{\frac{1}{2}}(t)}+\kappa(-t_{0})e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)}+\frac{\kappa(-t_{0})t_{0}e^{-Z(t)}}{\mu_{0}Z(t)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad+C_{\star}^{2}\frac{Z(-t_{0})}{\mu_{0}}\kappa(-t_{0})^{2}\mu_{0}^{2}.

Now we have from (
5.2
) and (
5.1
)

\displaystyle Z(-t_{0})\lesssim t_{0}\mu_{0},\quad\text{so that}\quad Z(-t_{0})\kappa(-t_{0})\lesssim\mu_{0},

(5.16)

and from the definition of
T_{1}
in (
4.4
), (
4.3
) and
0<\rho<\frac{1}{32}
, for any
t\in[-t_{0},T^{\star})
,

\displaystyle e^{-Z(t)}\lesssim e^{-\frac{Z_{0}}{\rho}}\lesssim\mu_{0}^{16}.

(5.17)

Hence, we deduce by choosing
\nu_{3}^{\star}
small enough and
C_{\star}
large enough that

\displaystyle\left|\mu_{i}(t)+\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}\dot{Z}(t)\right|\leq\frac{C_{\star}}{4}\left(\kappa(-t_{0})\mu_{0}+e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)}\right).

(5.18)

Now, we turn to the bounds on
z_{i}
and
\omega_{i}
for
i=1,2
. By multiplying (
5.14
) by
-\dot{Z}(t)\mu_{0}^{-1}\geq 1
, an integration from
-t_{0}
to
t
yields, using the initial bound (
5.6
) and (
5.16
)-(
5.17
),

\displaystyle\left|z_{i}(t)+\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}Z(t)\right|+\left|\omega_{i}(t)+\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}\omega_{0}\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\lesssim\kappa(t_{0})\mu_{0}+e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(-t_{0})}+C_{\star}\left(\kappa(-t_{0})Z(-t_{0})+\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)}\right).

Therefore, by choosing
\mu_{0}\leq\nu_{3}^{\star}
small enough, we conclude that

\displaystyle\left|z_{i}(t)+\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}Z(t)\right|+\left|\omega_{i}(t)-\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}\omega_{0}\right|\leq\frac{C_{\star}}{4}\left(\kappa(-t_{0})t_{0}+Z(t)^{-\frac{1}{4}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}Z(t)}\right),

which strictly improves the bound (
5.9
).

To estimate
\epsilon(t)
, we use the functional
\mathcal{F}_{-}
defined in (
3.26
). From the definition of
\Theta
in (
3.32
), using that
\kappa(-t_{0})t_{0}
is uniformly bounded on
\mathbb{R}
, combining (
4.5
), (
5.8
), (
5.12
)-(
5.13
), (
5.17
) and taking
\mu_{0}
small enough, we obtain for any
t\in[-t_{0},T_{1}]

\displaystyle\Theta(t)\lesssim C_{\star}\mu_{0}e^{-\frac{15}{8}Z(t)}+C_{\star}\kappa(-t_{0})\mu_{0}^{2}e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)}+C_{\star}^{2}\kappa(-t_{0})^{2}\mu_{0}^{2}e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)}.

(5.19)

We obtain combining (
3.35
), (
5.8
), (
5.10
)-(
5.11
),(
5.19
),

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{F}_{-}(t)

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{\star}\mu_{0}e^{-\frac{15}{8}Z(t)}+C_{\star}\kappa(-t_{0})\mu_{0}^{2}e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)}+C_{\star}\kappa(-t_{0})^{2}t_{0}\mu_{0}^{2}e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)}

\displaystyle+C_{\star}^{2}\mu_{0}\kappa(-t_{0})t_{0}e^{-\frac{15}{8}Z(t)}+C_{\star}^{3}\kappa(-t_{0})^{3}t_{0}\mu_{0}^{2}e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)}+C_{\star}^{2}\kappa(-t_{0})\mu_{0}^{3}e^{-\rho Z(t)}+C_{\star}^{3}\kappa(-t_{0})^{3}\mu_{0}^{3}

We integrate from
-t_{0}
to
t
after multiplying the right-hand side of the above inequality by
-\dot{Z}(t)\mu_{0}^{-1}>1
to obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{-}(t)-\mathcal{F}_{-}(-t_{0})\lesssim C_{\star}e^{-\frac{15}{8}Z(t)}+C_{\star}\kappa(-t_{0})\mu_{0}e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)}+C_{\star}^{2}\kappa(-t_{0})^{2}t_{0}\mu_{0}e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)}

\displaystyle\quad+C_{\star}^{2}\kappa(-t_{0})t_{0}e^{-\frac{15}{8}Z(t)}+C_{\star}^{3}\kappa(-t_{0})^{3}t_{0}\mu_{0}e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)}+C_{\star}^{2}\kappa(-t_{0})^{2}\mu_{0}^{2}e^{-\rho Z(t)}+C_{\star}^{3}\kappa(-t_{0})^{3}\mu_{0}^{2}Z(-t_{0}).

Observe from Young’s inequality that

\displaystyle C_{\star}^{2}\kappa(-t_{0})^{2}t_{0}\mu_{0}e^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)}+C_{\star}^{2}\kappa(-t_{0})t_{0}e^{-\frac{15}{8}Z(t)}\lesssim C_{\star}^{2}\kappa(-t_{0})t_{0}\left(\left(\kappa(-t_{0}\mu_{0}\right)^{2}+e^{-\frac{15}{8}Z(t)}\right).

Moreover, we have by using (
5.17
) by choosing
\mu_{0}
small depending on
\rho
and
C_{\star}
,

\displaystyle C_{\star}^{2}\kappa(-t_{0})^{2}\mu_{0}^{2}e^{-\rho Z(t)}\lesssim C_{\star}^{2}\kappa(-t_{0})^{2}\mu_{0}^{2}\mu_{0}^{16\rho}\lesssim C_{\star}\left(\kappa(t)\mu_{0}\right)^{2}.

Thus it follows from (
5.16
), (
5.17
), the above estimates, the coercivity of
\mathcal{F}_{-}
in (
3.33
), the bound at time
-t_{0}
of
\mathcal{F}_{-}
in (
5.7
) and taking
\mu_{0}\leq\nu_{4}^{\star}
small enough that there exists a positive universal constant
C
such that

\displaystyle\|\epsilon(t)\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\leq\left(CC_{\star}+CC_{\star}^{2}\kappa(-t_{0})t_{0}\right)\left(\kappa(-t_{0})^{2}\mu_{0}^{2}+e^{-\frac{15}{8}Z(t)}\right).

Therefore, by taking
t_{0}
large enough such that
C\kappa(-t_{0})t_{0}\leq\frac{1}{8}
and
C_{\star}
large enough such that
C_{\star}>8C
, and using (
5.18
) the bound (
5.8
) is strictly improved on
[-t_{0},T^{\star})
.

Thus, by a standard continuity argument, we have just contradicted the definition of
T^{\star}
, which concludes the proof of the first part of the proposition. Note that (
5.5
) is a direct consequence of the quantified dynamical system (
5.14
) by choosing
C
large enough.
∎

5.2.
Proof of Theorem
1.5
.

Let
v
be the pure
2
-solitary waves at
-\infty
,
i.e.

v
is the unique solution to (
1.10
) satisfying (
1.11
). We refer to
[
58
]
for the existence and uniqueness of the function
v
. In this section, we rely on the approximate solution
V
constructed in Section
2
to describe the evolution of
v
from
-\infty
to
+\infty
.

Recall that the positive time
T_{1}
defined in (
4.4
) satisfies
Z(T_{1})=\rho^{-1}Z_{0}
, where
\rho\in(0;\frac{1}{32})
has been fixed in the proof of Proposition
4.3
. Notice that, by (
D.12
) and the bound on
\rho
in (
4.4
), it holds

\displaystyle\forall t\in\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\quad Z(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-Z(t)}\leq Z_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-Z_{0}}\lesssim\mu_{0}^{2},

(5.20)

\displaystyle\forall|t|\geq T_{1},

\displaystyle\quad Z(t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-Z(t)}\leq\mu_{0}^{10}.

(5.21)

Corollary 5.3
.

Let
\nu_{4}^{\star}
be defined in Proposition
5.1
. There exists a constant
C>0
such that the following holds.
Let
\mu_{0}<\nu_{4}^{\star}
and
v(t)
be the unique solution of (
1.10
) satisfying (
1.11
).
Then, there exists a function
\Gamma:(-\infty,-T_{1}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{6}
such that the orthogonality conditions (
3.2
) hold on
(-\infty,-T_{1}]
, and for all
t\leq-T_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|v(t)-V(\Gamma(t))\right\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(\left|\mu_{i}(t)+\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}\dot{Z}(t)\right|+\left|\dot{z}_{i}(t)-\mu_{i}(t)\right|+|\dot{\omega}_{i}(t)|\right)\leq Ce^{-\frac{15}{16}Z(t)},

(5.22)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(\left|z_{i}(t)+\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}Z(t)\right|+\left|\omega_{i}(t)+\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}\omega_{0}\right|\right)\leq CZ(t)^{-\frac{1}{4}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}Z(t)}.

(5.23)

Proof.

It was proved in
[
58
]
that there exist two constants
C>0
and
\delta>0
, and a time
T_{0}>0
such that, for any
t\in(-\infty,-T_{0})
,

\displaystyle\left\|v(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{2}Q_{1+(-1)^{i}\mu_{0}}\left(\cdot-\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}\left(2\mu_{0}t+l\right),\cdot+\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}\omega_{0}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}\leq Ce^{\delta t}.

By the triangle inequality and Lemma
4.1
(ii), there exists
0<\delta_{1}\leq\delta
such that for any
t\in(-\infty,-T_{0})
,

\displaystyle\left\|v(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{2}Q_{1+(-1)^{i}\mu_{0}}\left(\cdot+\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}Z(t),\cdot+\frac{(-1)^{i}}{2}\omega_{0}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}\leq Ce^{\delta_{1}t}.

By defining the function
\kappa(t):=e^{\frac{\delta_{1}}{2}t}
and
\tilde{T}_{0}>T_{0}
such that
Ce^{-\frac{\delta_{1}}{2}\tilde{T}_{0}}\leq\mu_{0}<\nu_{4}^{\star}
, Proposition
5.1
yields for any
-t_{0}<\min(-\tilde{t}_{0},-\tilde{T}_{0})
the existence of a unique function
\Gamma
defined on
(-t_{0},-T_{1}]
such that estimates (
5.4
) hold on
(-t_{0},-T_{1}]
. By uniqueness, the function
\Gamma
is well-defined on
(-\infty,-T_{1}]
and estimates (
5.4
) hold for any
t_{0}
large. This remark concludes the proof of Corollary
5.3
by letting
t_{0}\to+\infty
in (
5.4
)-(
5.5
).
∎

We now prove a sharper version of Theorem
1.5
on
(-\infty,T_{1}]
by comparing the solution
v
to the approximate solution
V
.

Proposition 5.4
.

There exist some positive constants
C
and
\nu_{5}^{\star}
such that the following is true. Let
0<\mu_{0}<\nu_{5}^{\star}
and let
v
be the unique solution to (
1.10
) satisfying (
1.11
). Then, there exists a unique
\Gamma=(z_{1},z_{2},\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in C^{1}((-\infty,T_{1}]:\mathbb{R}^{6})
that satisfies for all
t\leq T_{1}
, by defining
\epsilon(t,\cdot):=v(t,\cdot)-V(\Gamma(t),\cdot)
,

\displaystyle\|\epsilon(t)\|_{H^{1}}\leq C\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{4}},

\displaystyle\left|\mu(t)-\dot{Z}(t)\right|+\left|\dot{z}_{i}(t)-\mu_{i}(t)\right|+\left|\dot{\omega}_{i}(t)\right|\leq CZ_{0}\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{4}},

\displaystyle\left|\bar{\mu}(t)\right|\leq C\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{4}}

(5.24)

\displaystyle\left|z(t)-Z(t)\right|\leq CZ_{0}^{2}\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}},

\displaystyle\left|\omega(t)-\omega_{0}\right|+\left|\bar{\omega}(t)\right|\leq CZ_{0}\mu_{0}^{\frac{5}{4}},

\displaystyle\left|\bar{z}(t)\right|\leq CZ_{0}\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}}.

Proof.

We proceed by splitting the time interval
(-\infty;T_{1}]
into the two intervals
(-\infty,-T_{1})
and
[-T_{1},T_{1}]
.

On the time interval
(-\infty;-T_{1}]
, Corollary
5.3
with
\nu_{5}^{\star}<\nu_{4}^{\star}
provides the existence of
\Gamma=(z_{1},z_{2},\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
defined on
(-\infty,-T_{1}]
satisfying inequalities (
5.22
)-(
5.23
) for any
t\leq-T_{1}
. This implies that (
4.7
) is verified for
w^{1}=v(-T_{1})
and
\Gamma^{1}=\Gamma(-T_{1})
.

On the time interval
[-T_{1},T_{1}]
, we adjust
\nu_{5}^{\star}<\nu_{3}^{\star}
to apply Proposition
4.3
. Then, there exists
C>0
and
\Gamma_{2}
defined on
[-T_{1},T_{1}]
such that (
4.8
) holds on the whole interval
[-T_{1},T_{1}]
. Notice that
\Gamma_{2}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-extension of
\Gamma
by the uniqueness of the modulation theory in Lemma
3.1
. We thus denote by
\Gamma
the function defined on
(-\infty,T_{1}]
. Finally, (
5.24
) on
[-T_{1},T_{1}]
is a direct application of (
4.8
) and of the quantified dynamical system in (
4.24
).
∎

Notice that the previous proposition holds on
(-\infty,T_{1}]
by comparing the solution
v
with the precise approximation
V
. It is also possible to compare the solution
v
with the sum of two translated and rescaled ground-states, which corresponds to the first part of Theorem
1.5
.

Corollary 5.5
.

There exist some positive constants
C
and
\nu_{5}^{\star}
such that the following is true. Let
0<\mu_{0}<\nu_{5}^{\star}
and let
v
be the unique solution to (
1.10
) satisfying (
1.11
). Then, there exists a unique
\Gamma=(z_{1},z_{2},\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in C^{1}((-\infty,T_{1}]:\mathbb{R}^{6})
that satisfies for all
t\leq T_{1}
, by defining
\eta(t,\cdot):=v(t,\cdot)-\sum_{i=1}^{2}Q_{1+\mu_{i}(t)}(\cdot-(z_{i}(t),\omega_{i}(t)))

\displaystyle\|\eta(t)\|_{H^{1}}\leq C\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{2}},

\displaystyle\left|\mu(t)-\dot{Z}(t)\right|+\left|\dot{z}_{i}(t)-\mu_{i}(t)\right|+\left|\dot{\omega}_{i}(t)\right|\leq C\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{2}},

\displaystyle\left|\bar{\mu}(t)\right|\leq C\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{2}}

(5.25)

\displaystyle\left|z(t)-Z(t)\right|\leq CZ_{0}^{2}\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}},

\displaystyle\left|\omega(t)-\omega_{0}\right|+\left|\bar{\omega}(t)\right|\leq CZ_{0}\mu_{0}^{\frac{5}{4}},

\displaystyle\left|\bar{z}(t)\right|\leq CZ_{0}\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}}.

Proof.

By applying Proposition
5.4
to
v
, we define the function
\Gamma
such that (
5.24
) holds on
(-\infty,T_{1}]
. It implies in particular that
z(t)>\frac{8}{9}Z(t)
on
(-\infty,-T_{1}]
.Since
\frac{9}{5}<2
, (
5.20
) implies
e^{-Z(t)}\leq\mu_{0}^{\frac{9}{5}}
. Thus, it follows from (
2.25
) and (
2.36
) that, for any
t\leq T_{1}
,

\displaystyle\left\|V_{A}(\Gamma(t))\right\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{15}{16}z(t)}\leq e^{-\frac{5}{6}Z(t)}\lesssim\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{2}}.

From the estimates on
\epsilon
and
z_{i}
in (
5.24
), it holds, for any
t\leq T_{1}
,

\displaystyle\left\|v(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{2}Q_{1+\mu_{i}(t)}\left(\cdot-\mathbf{z}_{i}(t)\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}\leq\left\|v(t)-V(\Gamma(t))\right\|_{H^{1}}+\|V_{A}(\Gamma(t))\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{2}}.

We now apply a modulation result around the two solitary waves. Since the statement is similar to the one of Lemma
3.1
and to Proposition
3.3
, we omit the proof. Then, we obtain the existence of a function
\tilde{\Gamma}=(\tilde{z}_{1},\tilde{z}_{2},\tilde{\omega}_{1},\tilde{\omega}_{2},\tilde{\mu}_{1},\tilde{\mu}_{2})
such that, by defining

\displaystyle\tilde{R}_{i}(t,\mathbf{x}):=Q_{1+\tilde{\mu}_{i}(t)}(\cdot-(\tilde{z}_{i}(t),\tilde{\omega}_{i}(t)))\quad\text{and}\quad\eta(t):=v(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{2}\tilde{R}_{i}(t),

we have for all
t\leq T_{1}
and
i=1,2
,

\displaystyle\int\eta(t)\partial_{x}\tilde{R}_{i}(t)=\int\eta(t)\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{i}(t)=\int\eta(t)\tilde{R}_{i}(t)=0,

\displaystyle\left|\dot{\tilde{z}}_{i}(t)-\tilde{\mu}_{i}(t)\right|+\left|\dot{\tilde{\omega}}_{i}(t)\right|+\|\eta(t)\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{2}}.

Furthermore, a pointwise estimate at any time
t\leq T_{1}
around two ground-states as in Remark
3.2
around
V
provides for any
i=1,2

\displaystyle\left|\mu_{i}(t)-\tilde{\mu}_{i}(t)\right|+\left|z_{i}(t)-\tilde{z}_{i}(t)\right|+\left|\omega_{i}(t)-\tilde{\omega}_{i}(t)\right|\lesssim\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{2}}.

Combining those estimates with (
5.24
) concludes the proof of estimates (
5.25
).
∎

On the time interval
[T_{1},+\infty)
, we apply the orbital and asymptotic stability results proved by the authors in
[
53
]
in the case of a function
w
close to a sum of two solitary waves whose velocities are bounded by
\underline{c}=\frac{1}{2}
and
\bar{c}=\frac{3}{2}
. Note that in
[
53
]
, the result is stated for a solution
u
of (
1.1
). Here by using a translation in space, we restate Theorem 1.1 in
[
53
]
in the setting of a solution
w
to (
1.10
).

Theorem 5.6

(
[
53
]
, Theorem 1.1)
.

There exist some positive constants
k
,
K
and
A
such that the following is true. For two velocities
1+\mu_{1}^{0}
and
1+\mu_{2}^{0}
satisfying
\frac{1}{2}<1+\mu_{2}^{0}<1+\mu_{1}^{0}<\frac{3}{2}
and
\sigma:=\mu_{1}^{0}-\mu_{2}^{0}<\frac{1}{2}
, we define
\alpha^{\star}:=k\sigma
and
Z^{\star}:=K|\ln\sigma|
. Let
\alpha<\alpha^{\star}
,
Z>Z^{\star}
,
(z_{i}^{0},\omega_{i}^{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
i=1,2
, and
w_{0}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfying

\displaystyle\left\|w_{0}-\sum_{i=1}^{2}Q_{1+\mu_{i}^{0}}\left(\cdot-\left(z_{i}^{0},\omega_{i}^{0}\right)\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}<\alpha\quad\text{and}\quad z_{1}^{0}-z_{2}^{0}>Z,

(5.26)

and let
w\in C(\mathbb{R}:H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
be the solution of (
1.10
) evolving from the initial condition
w(T_{1})=w_{0}
. Then, there exist functions
(z_{i},\omega_{i},1+\mu_{i})\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(0,+\infty))
,
i=1,2
such that:

(i)

(Orbital stability) for all
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\left\|w(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{2}Q_{1+\mu_{i}(t)}\left(\cdot-(z_{i}(t),\omega_{i}(t))\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(\left|\dot{z}_{i}(t)-\mu_{i}(t)\right|+\left|\dot{\omega}_{i}(t)\right|\right)\leq A\left(\alpha+e^{-\frac{1}{32}\sqrt{\underline{c}}Z}\right);

(5.27)

\displaystyle z_{1}(t)-z_{2}(t)\geq\frac{1}{2}(Z+\sigma t);

(5.28)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left|\mu_{i}(t)-\mu_{i}^{0}\right|\leq A\alpha.

(5.29)

(ii)

(Asymptotic stability) for
i=1,2
the limit
\mu_{i}^{+}=\lim_{+\infty}\mu_{i}(t)
exists and

\displaystyle\left|\mu_{i}^{+}-\mu_{i}^{0}\right|\leq A\left(\alpha+e^{-\frac{1}{32}\sqrt{\underline{c}}Z}\right);

(5.30)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\left\|w(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{2}Q_{1+\mu_{i}(t)}\left(\cdot-\left(z_{i}(t),\omega_{i}(t)\right)\right)\right\|_{H^{1}\left(x>-\frac{199}{200}t\right)}=0;

(5.31)

\displaystyle(\dot{z}_{i}(t),\dot{\omega}_{i}(t))\underset{t\rightarrow+\infty}{\longrightarrow}(\mu_{i}^{+},0).

We check that the hypotheses of Theorem
5.6
are satisfied for our solution
v
. We choose the initial velocities
1+\mu_{i}^{0}=1+\mu_{i}(T_{1})
and the initial positions
(z_{i}^{0},\omega_{i}^{0})=(z_{i}(T_{1}),\omega_{i}(T_{1}))
. It holds
-\frac{1}{2}<\mu_{2}^{0}<\mu_{1}^{0}<\frac{1}{2}
from the estimates on
\mu
and
\bar{\mu}
in (
5.25
) and (
4.5
). Concerning
\sigma=\mu_{1}(T_{1})-\mu_{2}(T_{1})=\mu(T_{1})
, since
\dot{Z}
is an increasing function from
-2\mu_{0}
to
2\mu_{0}
, we have with the estimate on
\mu(T_{1})
in (
5.24
) that
\sigma\leq 3\mu_{0}<3\nu_{5}^{\star}\leq\frac{1}{2}
. Furthermore, by (
4.5
), it holds
\sigma\geq\frac{1}{2}\mu_{0}
for
\nu_{5}^{\star}
small enough. As a consequence, we deduce

\displaystyle\alpha^{\star}>\frac{1}{2}k\mu_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad Z^{\star}<K|\ln(\frac{1}{2}\mu_{0})|.

(5.32)

We now check that the condition (
5.26
) is satisfied at the initial time
T_{1}
. From the estimate on
\eta
in (
5.25
), it holds

\displaystyle\left\|v(T_{1})-\sum_{i=1}^{2}Q_{1+\mu_{i}(T_{1})}\left(\cdot-\mathbf{z}_{i}(T_{1})\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}\leq C\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{2}}<\alpha^{\star}

where the last bound holds with
\nu_{5}^{\star}
small enough and the lower bound on
\alpha^{\star}
in (
5.32
). Since
Z(T_{1})=\rho^{-1}Z_{0}
, with
\rho\in(0,\frac{1}{32})
, we infer from the estimate on
z(T_{1})
in (
5.25
) and the inequality

Z_{0}\gtrsim|\ln(\mu_{0})|
(see (
4.3
)), that
z(T_{1})>Z^{\star}
provided
\rho
small enough (independent of
\mu_{0}
).

Since all the conditions are satisfied and
e^{-\frac{1}{32}\sqrt{\underline{c}}\frac{1}{\rho}Z_{0}}\leq\mu_{0}^{2}
, Theorem
5.6
provides the existence of three functions
(z_{i},\omega_{i},1+\mu_{i})\in\mathcal{C}([T_{1};+\infty):\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(0,+\infty))
, such that, for all
t\geq T_{1}
,

\displaystyle\left\|v(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{2}Q_{1+\mu_{i}(t)}\left(\cdot-(z_{i}(t),\omega_{i}(t))\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(\left|\dot{z}_{i}(t)-\mu_{i}(t)\right|+\left|\dot{\omega}_{i}(t)\right|+\left|\mu_{i}(t)-\mu_{i}(T_{1})\right|\right)\lesssim\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{2}};

(5.33)

\displaystyle z(t)=z_{1}(t)-z_{2}(t)\geq\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{Z_{0}}{\rho}+\mu(T_{1})(t-T_{1})\right),

(5.34)

and for
i=1,2
the limit
\mu_{i}^{+}=\lim_{+\infty}\mu_{i}(t)
exists and

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\left\|v(t)-\sum_{i=1}^{2}Q_{1+\mu_{i}(t)}\left(\cdot-\left(z_{i}(t),\omega_{i}(t)\right)\right)\right\|_{H^{1}(x>-\frac{99}{100}t)}=0;

(5.35)

\displaystyle\left|\mu_{i}^{+}-\mu_{i}(T_{1})\right|\lesssim\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{2}},\quad(\dot{z}_{i}(t),\dot{\omega}_{i}(t))\underset{t\rightarrow+\infty}{\longrightarrow}(\mu_{i}^{+},0).

(5.36)

The proof of (
1.14
)-(
1.16
) is thus an application of Corollary
5.5
and (
5.33
)-(
5.36
). Notice that the function
\Gamma
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
around
T_{1}
since its construction on both time intervals results from the same modulation property around the sum of two rescaled and translated ground-state.

To finish the proof of Theorem
1.5
, it only remains to prove (
1.17
)-(
1.18
).
These inequalities are direct consequences of the following lemma.

Lemma 5.7
.

For
i=1,2
, it holds

\displaystyle 0\leq\mu_{0}^{-1}\limsup_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\|\eta(t)\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\lesssim(-1)^{i+1}\mu_{i}^{+}-\mu_{0}\lesssim\mu_{0}^{-1}\liminf_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\|\eta(t)\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\lesssim\mu_{0}^{2}.

The counterpart of this lemma for the gKdV equation is Lemma 4.1 in
[
41
]
, whose proof relies on the conservation of the mass and the energy for the solution
v
. We give here an slightly different proof, still relying on the same arguments.

Proof.

From (
1.13
), (
1.16
) and (
5.34
), the energy and the mass (defined in (
1.2
)) of the error
\eta(t)
have finite limits as
t\rightarrow+\infty
, and we define

\displaystyle m_{\eta}^{+}:=\lim_{t\rightarrow+\infty}M(\eta(t))\quad\text{and}\quad e_{\eta}^{+}:=\lim_{t\rightarrow+\infty}E(\eta(t)).

Thus, by using the conservation of the mass and the energy of the solution
v
(see (
1.2
)) between the time
-\infty
and
+\infty
, the existence of the limits
\mu_{i}^{+}=\lim_{+\infty}\mu_{i}(t)
and (
1.11
), we infer that

\displaystyle M(Q_{1+\mu_{0}})+M(Q_{1-\mu_{0}})=M(Q_{1+\mu_{1}^{+}})+M(Q_{1+\mu_{2}^{+}})+m_{\eta}^{+},

\displaystyle E(Q_{1+\mu_{0}})+E(Q_{1-\mu_{0}})=E(Q_{1+\mu_{1}^{+}})+E(Q_{1+\mu_{2}^{+}})+e_{\eta}^{+},

which together with
M(Q_{c})=cM(Q)
and
E(Q_{c})=c^{2}E(Q)
yields

\displaystyle 0=\left(\mu_{1}^{+}+\mu_{2}^{+}\right)M(Q)+m_{\eta}^{+}\quad\text{and}\quad 2\mu_{0}^{2}E(Q)=\left(2(\mu_{1}^{+}+\mu_{2}^{+})+(\mu_{1}^{+})^{2}+(\mu_{2}^{+})^{2}\right)E(Q)+e_{\eta}^{+}.

(5.37)

Combining the two identities in (
5.37
) with
E(Q)=-\frac{1}{4}\int Q^{2}=-\frac{1}{4}M(Q)
(see Appendix B in
[
9
]
), we obtain, for
i=1,2
,

\displaystyle(\mu_{i}^{+})^{2}-\mu_{0}^{2}=(M(Q))^{-1}\left(m_{\eta}^{+}\left(1-\mu_{i}^{+}-(2M(Q))^{-1}m_{\eta}^{+}\right)+2e_{\eta}^{+}\right).

On the one hand, we observe combining (
4.6
), (
5.25
) and (
5.36
) that

\left|\mu_{1}^{+}-\mu_{0}\right|+\left|\mu_{2}^{+}+\mu_{0}\right|\lesssim\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{2}}.

(5.38)

On the other hand, it follows from the Sobolev embedding
L^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\hookrightarrow H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and (
1.14
) that

\displaystyle\limsup_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\|\eta(t)\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{2}m_{\eta}^{+}+e_{\eta}^{+}\lesssim\liminf_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\|\eta(t)\|_{H^{1}}^{2}.

Therefore, we deduce by choosing
\mu_{0}
small enough that, for
i=1,2
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\|\eta(t)\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\lesssim(\mu_{i}^{+})^{2}-\mu_{0}^{2}\lesssim\liminf_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\|\eta(t)\|_{H^{1}}^{2},

which combined to (
5.38
) concludes the proof of Lemma
5.7
.
∎

5.3.
Proof of Theorem
1.7

Let
w
be a solution to (
1.10
) satisfying (
1.19
). Observe from Corollary
5.3
and the triangle inequality that
w(-T_{1})
satisfies the conditions (
4.7
). Hence, we deduce from Proposition
4.3
that
w
satisfies (
4.8
) on
[-T_{1},T_{1}]
, which together with Proposition
5.4
and the triangle inequality concludes the proof of Theorem
1.7
.

We now briefly explain how to prove Remark
1.8
. By slightly refining the arguments in the proof of Proposition
4.3
, we can prove that, for any
t\in[-T_{1},T_{1}]
,

\displaystyle v(t,\mathbf{x})=V(t,\mathbf{x})+\epsilon(t,\mathbf{x}),\qquad\Gamma(t)=\left(z_{1}(t),z_{2}(t),\omega_{1}(t),\omega_{2}(t),\mu_{1}(t),\mu_{2}(t)\right),

(5.39)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\|\epsilon(t)\|_{H^{1}}\leq Z_{0}^{-5}\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{4}},&&\left|\mu(t)-\dot{Z}(t)\right|\leq CZ_{0}^{-5}\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{4}},&&\left|\bar{\mu}(t)\right|\leq CZ_{0}^{-5}\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{4}},\\
&\left|z(t)-Z(t)\right|\leq CZ_{0}^{-3}\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}},&&\left|\omega(t)-\omega_{0}\right|+\left|\bar{\omega}(t)\right|\leq CZ_{0}^{-4}\mu_{0}^{\frac{5}{4}},&&\left|\bar{z}(t)\right|\leq CZ_{0}^{-4}\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}}.\end{aligned}

Let
\mathcal{T}\in[-T_{1},T_{1}]
and
w
be a solution to (
1.10
) satisfying (
1.21
). By (
5.39
) and the triangle inequality,
w(\mathcal{T})
satisfies

\|w(\mathcal{T})-V(\mathcal{T})\|_{H^{1}}\leq Z_{0}^{-4}\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{4}}.

Then, we deduce arguing as in the proof of Proposition
4.3
on
[\mathcal{T},T_{1}]
that there exists
\tilde{\Gamma}
such that, for all
t\in[\mathcal{T},T_{1}]
,

\displaystyle w(t,\mathbf{x})=V(\mathbf{x};\tilde{\Gamma}(t))+\widetilde{\epsilon}(t,\mathbf{x}),\qquad\widetilde{\Gamma}(t)=\left(\widetilde{z}_{1}(t),\widetilde{z}_{2}(t),\widetilde{\omega}_{1}(t),\widetilde{\omega}_{2}(t),\widetilde{\mu}_{1}(t),\widetilde{\mu}_{2}(t)\right),

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\|\widetilde{\epsilon}(t)\|_{H^{1}}\leq C\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{4}},&&\left|\widetilde{\mu}(t)-\dot{Z}(t)\right|\leq CZ_{0}\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{4}},&&\left|\bar{\widetilde{\mu}}(t)\right|\leq C\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{4}},\\
&\left|\widetilde{z}(t)-Z(t)\right|\leq CZ_{0}^{2}\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}},&&\left|\widetilde{\omega}(t)-\omega_{0}\right|+\left|\bar{\widetilde{\omega}}(t)\right|\leq CZ_{0}\mu_{0}^{\frac{5}{4}},&&\left|\bar{\widetilde{z}}(t)\right|\leq CZ_{0}\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}}.\end{aligned}

Now, consider the function
w^{\dagger}(t,\mathbf{x})=w(-t,-\mathbf{x})
. Applying the same arguments as before to
w^{\dagger}
, we deduce on
[-\mathcal{T},T_{1}]
:

\displaystyle w^{\dagger}(t,\mathbf{x})=V(\mathbf{x};\Gamma^{\dagger}(t))+\epsilon^{\dagger}(t,\mathbf{x}),\qquad\Gamma^{\dagger}(t)=\left(z_{1}^{\dagger}(t),z_{2}^{\dagger}(t),\omega_{1}^{\dagger}(t),\omega_{2}^{\dagger}(t),\mu_{1}^{\dagger}(t),\mu_{2}^{\dagger}(t)\right),

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\|\epsilon^{\dagger}(t)\|_{H^{1}}\leq C\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{4}},&&\left|\mu^{\dagger}(t)-\dot{Z}(t)\right|\leq CZ_{0}\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{4}},&&\left|\bar{\mu}^{\dagger}(t)\right|\leq C\mu_{0}^{\frac{7}{4}},\\
&\left|z^{\dagger}(t)-Z(t)\right|\leq CZ_{0}^{2}\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}},&&\left|\omega^{\dagger}(t)-\omega_{0}\right|+\left|\bar{\omega}^{\dagger}(t)\right|\leq CZ_{0}\mu_{0}^{\frac{5}{4}},&&\left|\bar{z}^{\dagger}(t)\right|\leq CZ_{0}\mu_{0}^{\frac{3}{4}}.\end{aligned}

We conclude the proof of (
1.21
) by gathering the above estimates.

Appendix A
The Bessel potential
(-\Delta+1)^{-1}

In this appendix, we derive some useful properties of the operator
(-\Delta+1)^{-1}
, which is naturally defined for function
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
as a Fourier multiplier by

(-\Delta+1)^{-1}f=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left((1+4\pi^{2}|\cdot|^{2})^{-1}\widehat{f}\right).

(A.1)

In order to study the properties of
(-\Delta+1)^{-1}
, we introduce the modified Bessel function of second kind
K_{0}
and recall several properties (see for example
[
1
]
paragraph 9.7.2 and
[
2
]
). First,
K_{0}:\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is a positive function which solves the equation

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{2}f+\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}f-f=0.

(A.2)

Moreover,

K_{0}(r)\underset{r\to 0}{\sim}|\ln(r)|,

(A.3)

and there exists
C>0
, such that for all
i\in\{0,1\}
and
r>1
,

\displaystyle\left|K_{0}^{(i)}(r)-(-1)^{i}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{e^{-r}}{r^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right|\leq C\frac{e^{-r}}{r^{\frac{3}{2}}}.

(A.4)

We also recall that the kernel of the Bessel potential in dimension
d=2
, defined by

G_{2}(\mathbf{x})=\mathcal{F}^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{1+4\pi^{2}|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}\right),
can be expressed in terms of the Bessel function
K_{0}
through the formula (see
[
2
]
, and also
[
56
]
),

\displaystyle G_{2}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{+\infty}e^{-\pi\frac{|\mathbf{x}|^{2}}{\delta}}e^{-\frac{\delta}{4\pi}}\frac{d\delta}{\delta}=\frac{1}{2\pi}K_{0}(|\mathbf{x}|).

(A.5)

We observe from (
A.3
) and (
A.4
) that
G_{2}
is integrable over
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Thus, it follows from (
A.1
) and the Young theorem on convolution that for any
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

(-\Delta+1)^{-1}f=G_{2}\star f.

(A.6)

First, we prove that
(-\Delta+1)^{-1}
preserves the parity in any variable.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

•

If
f
is radial, then
(-\Delta+1)^{-1}f
is also radial.

•

If
f=f(x,y)
is even, respectively odd, in its first variable
x
, then
(-\Delta+1)^{-1}f
is also even, respectively odd, in its first variable.

•

If
f=f(x,y)
is even, respectively odd, in its second variable
y
, then
(-\Delta+1)^{-1}f
is also even, respectively odd, in its second variable.

Proof.

Since
G_{2}
is radial, the proof of Lemma
A.1
follows directly form identity (
A.6
).
∎

Next, we prove that the operator
(-\Delta+1)^{-1}
preserves the decay in
\mathcal{Y}
whose definition is given in (
1.24
).

Lemma A.2
.

Let
h\in\mathcal{Y}
. Then
(-\Delta+1)^{-1}h\in\mathcal{Y}
.

Proof.

Let
h\in\mathcal{Y}
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
. Then, by definition of
\mathcal{Y}
, there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
|\partial_{\mathbf{x}}^{\alpha}h(\mathbf{x})|\lesssim\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{n}e^{-|\mathbf{x}|}
. By (
A.5
), we have

\displaystyle\left|e^{|\mathbf{x}|}(-\Delta+1)^{-1}\partial_{\mathbf{x}}^{\alpha}h(\mathbf{x})\right|\lesssim e^{|\mathbf{x}|}

\displaystyle\int_{|\mathbf{y}|\leq 2|\mathbf{x}|}K_{0}(|\mathbf{y}|)|\partial_{\mathbf{x}}^{\alpha}h(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})|d\mathbf{y}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+e^{|\mathbf{x}|}\int_{|\mathbf{y}|>2|\mathbf{x}|}K_{0}(|\mathbf{y}|)|\partial_{\mathbf{x}}^{\alpha}h(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})|d\mathbf{y}=I(\mathbf{x})+II(\mathbf{x}).

(A.7)

To deal with the first contribution on the right-hand side of (
A
), we observe from the triangle inequality that
e^{|\mathbf{x}|}\leq e^{|\mathbf{y}|}e^{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|}
and
\langle\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\rangle^{n}\lesssim\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{n}
in the region
|\mathbf{y}|\leq 2|\mathbf{x}|
. Thus, we deduce after passing in polar coordinates and using (
A.3
) and (
A.4
) that

\displaystyle I(\mathbf{x})\lesssim\left(\int_{|\mathbf{y}|\leq 2|\mathbf{x}|}K_{0}(|\mathbf{y}|)e^{|\mathbf{y}|}\langle\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\rangle^{n}d\mathbf{y}\right)\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\langle\mathbf{y}\rangle^{-n}e^{|\mathbf{y}|}|\partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\alpha}h(\mathbf{y})|\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\int_{0}^{1}|\ln r|rdr+\int_{1}^{2|\mathbf{x}|}r^{\frac{1}{2}}dr\right)\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{n}\lesssim\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{n+2}.

In the region
|\mathbf{y}|>2|\mathbf{x}|
, we use the trivial bound
e^{|\mathbf{x}|}\leq e^{\frac{|\mathbf{y}|}{2}}
. Hence, it follows from (
A.4
) that

II(\mathbf{x})\lesssim\left(\int_{|\mathbf{y}|>2|\mathbf{x}|}K_{0}(|\mathbf{y}|)e^{\frac{|\mathbf{y}|}{2}}d\mathbf{y}\right)\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(|\partial_{\mathbf{y}}^{\alpha}h(\mathbf{y})|\right)\lesssim\left(\int_{1}^{+\infty}e^{-\frac{r}{2}}r^{\frac{1}{2}}dr\right)\lesssim 1.

These two estimates conclude the proof of Lemma
A.2
.
∎

Next, we prove that the operator
(-\Delta+1)^{-1}
preserves polynomial and exponential
6
6

6

with a factor in the exponential strictly smaller than
1
(see Remark
A.4
).
growth at infinity. Consider the vector space
E
defined by

\displaystyle E:=\left\{f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\ :\ \forall\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{2},\quad N(\partial_{\mathbf{x}}^{\alpha}f)<\infty\right\}\quad\text{with}\quad N(f):=\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|f(\mathbf{x})e^{-\frac{|\mathbf{x}|}{2}}\right|.

Equipped with the family of semi-norms
\left(N(\partial_{\mathbf{x}}^{\alpha}\cdot)\right)_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}
,
E
is a Fréchet space. In particular,
E
contains the functions whose all derivatives have polynomial growth at infinity. We first prove that space
E
is invariant under the action of the operator
(-\Delta+1)^{-1}
.

Proposition A.3
.

The operator
(-\Delta+1):E\rightarrow E
is a homeomorphism, whose inverse operator is defined by

\displaystyle(-\Delta+1)^{-1}:E\to E,\quad f\mapsto G_{2}\star f.

(A.8)

Remark A.4
.

From the proof, this property remains true for functions growing at infinity at most as
e^{\delta|\mathbf{x}|}
, for any
0<\delta<1
.

Proof.

First, by the definition of
E
, the operator
(-\Delta+1)
is a continuous map from
E
into itself. To prove that the application is bijective, we use the explicit definition of the inverse.

Let
f\in E
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
. We verify that
G_{2}\star\partial_{\mathbf{x}}^{\alpha}f
is a well-defined function belonging to
E
. Indeed, by using the triangle inequality, we have
e^{-\frac{|\mathbf{x}|}{2}}\leq e^{-\frac{|\mathbf{z}|}{2}}e^{\frac{|\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{x}|}{2}}
, so that from (
A.5
)

\displaystyle N(G_{2}\star\partial_{\mathbf{x}}^{\alpha}f)\leq\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}G_{2}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z})|\partial_{\mathbf{x}}^{\alpha}f(\mathbf{z})|e^{-\frac{|\mathbf{x}|}{2}}d\mathbf{z}\lesssim\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}K_{0}(|\mathbf{y}|)e^{\frac{|\mathbf{y}|}{2}}d\mathbf{y}\right)N(\partial_{\mathbf{x}}^{\alpha}f).

Moreover, it follows from the asymptotics of
K_{0}
at
0
in (
A.3
) and at infinity in (
A.4
) that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}K_{0}(|\mathbf{y}|)e^{\frac{|\mathbf{y}|}{2}}d\mathbf{y}\lesssim 1
. Therefore, we conclude that

\displaystyle N(G_{2}\star\partial_{\mathbf{x}}^{\alpha}f)\lesssim N(\partial_{\mathbf{x}}^{\alpha}f).

One can see from (
A.1
) and (
A.5
) that, for all
f\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,

\displaystyle(-\Delta+1)\left(G_{2}\star f\right)=G_{2}\star(-\Delta+1)f=f.

Since the Schwartz space
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is dense in
E
for the topology induced by the semi-norms
(\tilde{N}_{\beta})_{\beta\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}
, where for all
f\in E
,
\tilde{N}_{\beta}(f):=\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left|\partial_{\mathbf{x}}^{\beta}f(\mathbf{x})e^{-\frac{3}{4}|\mathbf{x}|}\right|
, we obtain that the convolution with
G_{2}
is the inverse of
(-\Delta+1)
on
E
. As a consequence,
(-\Delta+1)
is a homeomorphism from
E
to itself.
∎

The next result is a consequence from the fact that the operator
(-\Delta+1)^{-1}
also preserves the polynomial growth at infinity.

Corollary A.5
.

Let
f\in E
and let
z\gg 1
be a fixed parameter.

(i)

Assume that there exists a positive constant
c=c(z)
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
|f(\mathbf{x})|\leq c(z)\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{n}
. Then, for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\left|(-\Delta+1)^{-1}f(\mathbf{x})\right|\lesssim c(z)\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{n}.

(A.9)

(ii)

Assume that there exist positive constants
c_{1}=c_{1}(z)
,
c_{2}=c_{2}(z)
,
k=k(z)
with
k(z)\gg 1
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
|f(\mathbf{x})|\leq\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{n}\left(c_{1}(z)+c_{2}(z)\mathbf{1}_{|\mathbf{x}|>k(z)}\right)
. Then, for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\left|(-\Delta+1)^{-1}f(\mathbf{x})\right|\lesssim\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{n}\left(c_{1}(z)+c_{2}(z)e^{-\frac{k(z)}{4}}+c_{2}(z)\mathbf{1}_{|\mathbf{x}|>\frac{k(z)}{2}}\right).

(A.10)

Proof.

By using the asymptotic expansions of
K_{0}
at
0
and at infinity (see (
A.3
) and (
A.4
)) and the rough estimate
\langle\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\rangle^{n}\lesssim\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{n}\langle\mathbf{y}\rangle^{n}
, we have that

\displaystyle\left|(-\Delta+1)^{-1}f(\mathbf{x})\right|=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}G_{2}(\mathbf{y})f(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})d\mathbf{y}\right|\lesssim c(z)\left\langle\mathbf{x}\right\rangle^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}K_{0}(|\mathbf{y}|)\left\langle\mathbf{y}\right\rangle^{n}d\mathbf{y}\lesssim c(z)\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{n}.

(A.11)

which concludes the proof of (
A.9
).

The proof of (
A.10
) follows the same strategy as the one of (
A.9
). Arguing as above, we have

\displaystyle\left|(-\Delta+1)^{-1}f(\mathbf{x})\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim c_{1}(z)\left\langle\mathbf{x}\right\rangle^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}K_{0}(|\mathbf{y}|)\left\langle\mathbf{y}\right\rangle^{n}d\mathbf{y}+c_{2}(z)\left\langle\mathbf{x}\right\rangle^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}K_{0}(|\mathbf{y}|)\left\langle\mathbf{y}\right\rangle^{n}\mathbf{1}_{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|>k(z)}d\mathbf{y}

\displaystyle=I(\mathbf{x})+II(\mathbf{x}).

By using the asymptotic expansions of
K_{0}
at
0
and at infinity, we see that
|I(\mathbf{x})|\lesssim c_{1}(z)\left\langle\mathbf{x}\right\rangle^{n}
. To handle the contribution
II(\mathbf{x})
, we split the domain of integration into the regions
|\mathbf{y}|\leq k(z)/2
and
|\mathbf{y}|>k(z)/2
, so that

\displaystyle II(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=c_{2}(z)\left\langle\mathbf{x}\right\rangle^{n}\int_{|\mathbf{y}|\leq\frac{k(z)}{2}}K_{0}(|\mathbf{y}|)\left\langle\mathbf{y}\right\rangle^{n}\mathbf{1}_{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|>k(z)}d\mathbf{y}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+c_{2}(z)\left\langle\mathbf{x}\right\rangle^{n}\int_{|\mathbf{y}|>\frac{k(z)}{2}}K_{0}(|\mathbf{y}|)\left\langle\mathbf{y}\right\rangle^{n}\mathbf{1}_{|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}|>k(z)}d\mathbf{y}\quad=II_{1}(\mathbf{x})+II_{2}(\mathbf{x}).

On the one hand, in the region
|\mathbf{y}|\leq k(z)/2
, we have
|\mathbf{x}|\geq k(z)/2
, so that

II_{1}(\mathbf{x})\lesssim c_{2}(z)\left\langle\mathbf{x}\right\rangle^{n}\mathbf{1}_{|\mathbf{x}|>\frac{k(z)}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}K_{0}(|\mathbf{y}|)\left\langle\mathbf{y}\right\rangle^{n}d\mathbf{y}\lesssim c_{2}(z)\left\langle\mathbf{x}\right\rangle^{n}\mathbf{1}_{|\mathbf{x}|>\frac{k(z)}{2}}.

On the other hand, in the region
|\mathbf{y}|>k(z)/2
, we observe from the decay property of
K_{0}
at infinity in (
A.4
) that

II_{2}(\mathbf{x})\lesssim c_{2}(z)\left\langle\mathbf{x}\right\rangle^{n}\int_{|\mathbf{y}|>\frac{k(z)}{2}}e^{-|\mathbf{y}|}\left\langle\mathbf{y}\right\rangle^{n}d\mathbf{y}\lesssim c_{2}(z)e^{-\frac{k(z)}{4}}\left\langle\mathbf{x}\right\rangle^{n}.

Therefore, we conclude the proof of (
A.10
) gathering these estimates.
∎

Finally, we prove that
(-\Delta+1)^{-1}
commutes with the operator
\partial_{x}^{-1}
defined in (
1.29
).

Lemma A.6
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{Y}
. Then
\partial_{x}^{-1}(-\Delta+1)^{-1}f=(-\Delta+1)^{-1}\partial_{x}^{-1}f
.

Proof.

The proof follows from (
A.8
), the integrability of
G_{2}
and Fubini’s theorem.
∎

Appendix B
Properties of the ground-state
Q

B.1.
Basic properties of
Q
and related functions

In this subsection, we derive basic properties of the ground state
Q
of (
1.5
), the function
\Lambda Q
defined in (
1.28
) and other related functions (
X
,
Y
and
W
are defined in (
2.4
), while
\Lambda^{2}Q
is defined in (
1.28
)).

Lemma B.1
.

The following properties hold true.

(i)

Q,\,\Lambda Q,\,\Lambda^{2}Q,\,X\in\mathcal{Y}
and
Y,\,W\in\mathcal{Z}
.

(ii)

Q
,
\Lambda Q
and
X
are radial function,
W
is even in its second variable and
Y
is odd in its second variable.

Proof.

The proof of (i) follows from Proposition
1.1
and Lemma
A.1
, while the proof of (ii) follows from Proposition
1.1
and Lemma
A.2
.
∎

Lemma B.2
.

The following identities hold true.

\displaystyle\int Q^{2}

\displaystyle=\int Q;

(B.1)

\displaystyle\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int Q;

(B.2)

\displaystyle\langle Q,\partial_{x}Q\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle Q,\partial_{y}Q\rangle=\langle\partial_{x}Q,\partial_{y}Q\rangle=\langle\partial_{x}Q,\Lambda Q\rangle=\langle\partial_{y}Q,\Lambda Q\rangle=0;

(B.3)

\displaystyle\langle X,\partial_{x}Q\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle Y,\partial_{x}Q\rangle=\langle W,\partial_{y}Q\rangle=\langle X,\partial_{y}Q\rangle=\langle Y,\Lambda Q\rangle=0;

(B.4)

\displaystyle\langle 2QX,\partial_{x}Q\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle 2QX,\partial_{y}Q\rangle=0;

(B.5)

\displaystyle\langle 2QX,\Lambda Q\rangle

\displaystyle=-\left\langle Q,\Lambda Q\right\rangle-\left\langle(-\Delta+1)^{-1}Q,Q\right\rangle<0;

(B.6)

\displaystyle\langle 2QY,\partial_{x}Q\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle 2QY,\Lambda Q\rangle=0;

(B.7)

\displaystyle\langle 2QY,\partial_{y}Q\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle\partial_{x}^{-1}\partial_{y}Q,\partial_{y}Q\rangle>0;

(B.8)

\displaystyle\langle 2QW,\partial_{y}Q\rangle

\displaystyle=0;

(B.9)

\displaystyle\langle 2QW,\partial_{x}Q\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int Q>0.

(B.10)

Remark B.3
.

We were not able to compute explicitely the value of
\langle 2QW,\Lambda Q\rangle
. The value of this coefficient (positive, negative or null) does not impact the rest of the computations, see for instance Lemma
2.2
.

Proof.

The identity (
B.1
) is a consequence of the equation of
Q
in (
1.5
) and integration by parts. For (
B.2
), with the definition of
\Lambda Q
in (
1.28
) and (
B.1
):

\displaystyle\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle=\int\Lambda QQ=\left(\frac{d}{dc}\int\frac{Q_{c}^{2}}{2}\right)_{c=1}=\frac{1}{2}\int Q^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\int Q.

The proofs of (
B.3
), (
B.4
), (
B.5
), (
B.7
) and (
B.9
) follow from directly from Lemma
B.1
(ii).

To prove (
B.6
), we observe from Proposition
2.1
(vii) that
2Q\Lambda Q=(-\Delta+1)\Lambda Q+Q
. Hence,

\displaystyle\left\langle 2QX,\Lambda Q\right\rangle=-\left\langle(-\Delta+1)^{-1}Q,(-\Delta+1)\Lambda Q+Q\right\rangle=-\left\langle Q,\Lambda Q\right\rangle-\left\langle(-\Delta+1)^{-1}Q,Q\right\rangle<0,

thanks to (
B.2
) and Plancerel’s identity.

Now, we prove (
B.8
). We deduce from the identity
(-\Delta+1)Q=Q^{2}
and Lemma
A.6
that

\displaystyle\left\langle 2QY,\partial_{y}Q\right\rangle=-\left\langle\partial_{x}^{-1}(-\Delta+1)^{-1}\partial_{y}Q,\left(-\Delta+1\right)\partial_{y}Q\right\rangle=-\left\langle\partial_{x}^{-1}\partial_{y}Q,\partial_{y}Q\right\rangle.

Using an integration by parts in
x
and that
y\mapsto\partial_{y}Q(x,y)
is odd, we compute

\displaystyle-\langle\partial_{x}^{-1}\partial_{y}Q,\partial_{y}Q\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{y}Q(x,y)dx\right)^{2}dy=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{y}Q(x,y)dx\right)^{2}dy>0,

since
\partial_{y}Q(x,y)<0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
y>0
.

Finally, we use the identity
(-\Delta+1)Q=Q^{2}
, Lemma
A.6
and integration by parts to deduce that

\displaystyle\left\langle 2QW,\partial_{x}Q\right\rangle=-\left\langle\partial_{x}^{-1}(-\Delta+1)^{-1}\Lambda Q,\left(-\Delta+1\right)\partial_{x}Q\right\rangle=\left\langle\Lambda Q,Q\right\rangle,

which combined with (
B.2
) concludes the proof of (
B.10
).
∎

Lemma B.4
.

Let
0<\nu<\frac{1}{32}
. Let
\mu>0
,
(z,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
0<\mu\leq\nu
and
|(z,\omega)|\leq\nu
. Then, for any
\mathbf{x}=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\left|Q_{1+\mu}(\mathbf{x}+(z,\omega))-Q(\mathbf{x})-\mu\Lambda Q(\mathbf{x})-z\partial_{x}Q(\mathbf{x})-\omega\partial_{y}Q(\mathbf{x})\right|\lesssim\left(\mu^{2}+z^{2}+\omega^{2}\right)e^{-\frac{31}{32}|\mathbf{x}|}.

Proof.

First, observe from the Taylor formula that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \left|Q_{1+\mu}(\mathbf{x})-Q(\mathbf{x})-\mu\Lambda Q(\mathbf{x})\right|&=\left|\mu^{2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\frac{d^{2}}{dc^{2}}\left(Q_{c}(\mathbf{x})\right)_{|c=1+s\mu}ds\right|\\
&\qquad\lesssim\mu^{2}\sup_{|\tilde{\mu}|\leq\delta}\left|\Lambda^{2}_{1+\tilde{\mu}}Q(\mathbf{x})\right|\lesssim\mu^{2}e^{-(1-\delta)|\mathbf{x}|};\end{aligned}

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \left|Q(\mathbf{x}+(z,\omega))-Q(\mathbf{x})-(z,\omega)\cdot\nabla Q(\mathbf{x})\right|&=\left|\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\frac{d^{2}}{ds^{2}}Q(\mathbf{x}+s(z,\omega))ds\right|\\
&\qquad\lesssim\left(z^{2}+\omega^{2}\right)e^{-|\mathbf{x}|};\end{aligned}

(B.11)

\displaystyle|\mu|\left|\Lambda Q(\mathbf{x})-\Lambda Q(\mathbf{x}+(0,\omega))\right|=\left|\mu\omega\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\partial_{y}\Lambda Q(\mathbf{x}+(0,s\omega))ds\right|\lesssim|\mu\omega|e^{-|\mathbf{x}|}.

Therefore, the proof of Lemma
B.4
follows by choosing
0<\nu<\frac{1}{32}
and using the triangle inequality.
∎

B.2.
Asymptotic expansions of
Q
and
\chi_{0}

In this sub-section, we give the asymptotic development of
Q
and its derivatives at infinity at any finite order
k
.

To define the asymptotic expansion of
Q
, we introduce the functions
L_{k}

\displaystyle L_{k}(r):=\frac{e^{-r}}{\sqrt{r}}\sum_{l=0}^{k}\frac{a_{l}}{r^{l}},\quad\text{ with }a_{0}:=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\text{ and for }l\geq 1,\quad a_{l}:=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{(-1)^{l}}{8^{l}l!}\prod_{j=1}^{l}(2j-1)^{2}.

(B.12)

The main result is stated in the following proposition.

Proposition B.5
.

There exists a constant
\kappa>0
, so that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a constant
C=C_{k}>0
such that

\displaystyle\forall i\in\{0,1,2\},\forall r\geq 1,\quad\left|Q^{(i)}(r)-\kappa L_{k}^{(i)}(r)\right|\leq C\frac{e^{-r}}{r^{k+\frac{3}{2}}}.

Remark B.6
.

By choosing
i=0
and
k=0
, we recover the first order asymptotic expansion of
Q
in (
1.6
).

Remark B.7
.

The radius of convergence of
\sum a_{l}r^{l}
is equal to
0
. For this reason, the approximation of
Q
in Proposition
B.5
can only hold at a finite order
k
.

Remark B.8
.

The proof of the asymptotic expansion of
Q
in dimension
3
given in (
1.8
) relies on the exact same arguments as the ones in dimension
2
.

The goal of this subsection is to give a proof of Proposition
B.5
. It is divided into several lemmas.

We rely on the rough bound of the ground-state proved in
[
4
]

\exists\delta\in(0,1),\exists C>0,\forall r>1,\quad Q(r)\leq Ce^{-\delta r}.

(B.13)

Let us first improve the decay of
Q
in (
B.13
) by using the same arguments as in
[
57
,
4
]
.

Lemma B.9
.

There exists
R_{0}>0

C>0
such that

\displaystyle\exists C>0,\forall r>R_{0},\quad Q(r)\leq C\frac{e^{-r}}{r^{\frac{1}{4}}}.

Proof.

First step:
We prove that
f(r):=-3\frac{Q^{\prime}}{r}-\frac{Q}{r^{2}}-4Q^{2}-\frac{Q}{r}<0
, for
r
large enough. Indeed, taking the derivative of this function gives

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(r)

\displaystyle=Q^{\prime}\left(-\frac{1}{r}+\frac{5}{r^{2}}-8Q\right)+Q\left(-\frac{3}{r}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}+\frac{2}{r^{3}}+3\frac{Q}{r}\right)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}(e^{-r}f)

\displaystyle=e^{-r}\left(Q^{\prime}\left(\frac{2}{r}+\frac{5}{r^{2}}-8Q\right)+Q\left(-\frac{2}{r}+\frac{2}{r^{2}}+\frac{2}{r^{3}}+4Q+3\frac{Q}{r}\right)\right)<0,

where the last inequality holds for
r\geq R_{0}
with
R_{0}
large enough, by the signs of
Q>0
and of the derivative
Q^{\prime}<0
and the exponential decay in (
1.6
). Since
f
tends to
0
at
+\infty
, an integration from
r>R_{0}
to
+\infty
yields

\displaystyle\forall r>R_{0},\quad f(r)>0.

Second step:
We prove that
\frac{Q}{r}+4Q+4Q^{\prime}<0
, for
r
large enough. As before, the derivative gives

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\left(\frac{Q}{r}+4Q+4Q^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=-3\frac{Q^{\prime}}{r}-\frac{Q}{r^{2}}-4Q^{2}+4Q+4Q^{\prime}

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\left(e^{-r}\left(\frac{Q}{r}+4Q+4Q^{\prime}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=e^{-r}\left(-3\frac{Q^{\prime}}{r}-\frac{Q}{r^{2}}-4Q^{2}-\frac{Q}{r}\right)=e^{-r}f(r)>0,

where the last inequality comes from the first step. With a null limit at infinity, an integration from
r>R_{0}
to
\infty
yields

\displaystyle\forall r\geq R_{0},\quad\frac{Q}{r}+4Q+4Q^{\prime}<0.

Third step:
Bound on the required term. By direct computation with the previous step

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\left(r^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{2r}Q^{2}\right)=\frac{r^{\frac{1}{2}}}{2}e^{2r}Q\left(\frac{Q}{r}+4Q+4Q^{\prime}\right)<0.

Thus the positive function
r^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{2r}Q^{2}(r)
decays at infinity, and is bounded on
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
by a constant
c>0
, which concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

With the same proof, we can obtain that
Q(r)\leq cr^{-\alpha}e^{-r}
, for any
\alpha\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
. However, Lemma
B.9
is sufficient in the rest of the appendix to obtain a precise asymptotic expansion of
Q
.

We prove that the asymptotic behaviour of the function
Q
is related to the one of the modified Bessel function
K_{0}
in (
A.4
).
We precise the asymptotic behaviour of
K_{0}
at
+\infty
by the functions
L_{k}
defined in (
B.12
), inspired by
[
1
,
6
]
.

Lemma B.10

(Asymptotic behaviour of
K_{0}
)
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
, there exists a constant
C=C_{k,i}>0
such that

\displaystyle\forall r\geq 1,\quad\left|K_{0}^{(i)}(r)-L_{k}^{(i)}(r)\right|\leq C\frac{e^{-r}}{r^{k+\frac{3}{2}}}.

Proof.

By computation, we notice that

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{2}\left(\frac{K_{0}}{L_{k}}\right)+\left(\frac{1}{r}+2\frac{L_{k}^{\prime}}{L_{k}}\right)\partial_{r}\left(\frac{K_{0}}{L_{k}}\right)=\frac{K_{0}}{L_{k}^{2}}\left(-L_{k}^{\prime\prime}-\frac{L_{k}^{\prime}}{r}+L_{k}\right).

(B.14)

A computation on the finite sums gives us

\displaystyle-L_{k}^{\prime\prime}-\frac{L_{k}^{\prime}}{r}+L_{k}=\frac{e^{-r}}{\sqrt{r}}\left(\sum_{n=1}^{k+1}-\frac{a_{n-1}}{r^{n}}(2n-2)+\sum_{n=2}^{k+2}-\frac{a_{n-2}}{r^{n}}\left(n-\frac{3}{2}\right)^{2}\right)=\frac{e^{-r}}{\sqrt{r}}\frac{-a_{k}}{r^{k+2}}\left(k+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}.

(B.15)

By using this development in (
B.14
), the bound (
A.4
) and (
B.12
), and implicit constants independent of
k
and
r

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{r}\left(e^{\ln(r)+2\ln(L_{k})}\partial_{r}\left(\frac{K_{0}}{L_{k}}\right)\right)\right|=\left|K_{0}\frac{e^{-r}}{r^{k+\frac{3}{2}}}a_{k}\left(k+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}\right|\lesssim\frac{e^{-2r}}{r^{k+2}}.

(B.16)

By integrating from
r
to
R>r

\displaystyle\left|rL_{k}^{2}(r)\partial_{r}\left(\frac{K_{0}(r)}{L_{k}(r)}\right)+C(R)\right|\lesssim\frac{e^{-2r}}{r^{k+2}}.

(B.17)

Notice that
C(R)\rightarrow 0
as
R\rightarrow+\infty
, by the asymptotic behaviours in (
A.4
) and (
B.12
):

\displaystyle|C(R)|=\left|R\left(K_{0}^{\prime}(R)L_{k}(R)-K_{0}(R)L_{k}^{\prime}(R)\right)\right|\leq R(|K_{0}^{\prime}(R)|+|L_{k}^{\prime}(R)|)(|L_{k}(R)|+K_{0}(R))\underset{R\rightarrow+\infty}{\rightarrow}0.

Since
\displaystyle\lim_{r\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{K_{0}(r)}{L_{k}(r)}=1
, a second integration gives

\displaystyle\left|K_{0}(r)-L_{k}(r)\right|\lesssim\frac{|L_{k}(r)|}{r^{k+1}}\lesssim\frac{e^{-r}}{r^{k+\frac{3}{2}}}.

For the asymptotic behaviour of the derivative of
K_{0}
, it suffices to develop (
B.17
) to get the asymptotic development of
K_{0}^{\prime}
up to order
k+\frac{3}{2}
. For the higher order derivative, we proceed by induction on the equation satisfied by
K_{0}
and the derivatives of the equation satisfied by
L_{k}
in (
B.15
).
∎

Lemma B.11

(Approximation of
Q
by
K_{0}
)
.

There exists a constant
\kappa>0
and a constant
c>0
, such that

\displaystyle\forall i\in\{0,1,2\},\ \forall r>1,\quad|Q^{(i)}(r)-\kappa K_{0}^{(i)}(r)|\leq c\frac{e^{-2r}}{r}.

Proof.

The proof relies on the same arguments as the previous proof. By direct computations,

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{2}\left(\frac{Q}{K_{0}}\right)+\left(\frac{1}{r}+2\frac{K_{0}^{\prime}}{K_{0}}\right)\partial_{r}\left(\frac{Q}{K_{0}}\right)=-\frac{Q^{2}}{K_{0}}.

(B.18)

By using the asymptotic bound in Lemma
B.9
and the asymptotic equivalent in Lemma
B.10
, we find that

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\left(e^{\ln(r)+2\ln(K_{0})}\partial_{r}\left(\frac{Q}{K_{0}}\right)\right)=-Q^{2}rK_{0},\quad\text{with}\quad 0\leq Q^{2}rK_{0}\lesssim e^{-3r}.

(B.19)

By (
B.19
), the function
rK_{0}^{2}\partial_{r}\left(\frac{Q}{K_{0}}\right)
is a decreasing and bounded function for
r
large enough, and so it has a finite limit when
r\to+\infty
, denoted by
A_{\infty}
. Hence, integrating (
B.19
) from
r
to
+\infty
and using (
A.4
) yield

\displaystyle-e^{-3r}\lesssim A_{\infty}-rK_{0}^{2}(r)\partial_{r}\left(\frac{Q(r)}{K_{0}(r)}\right)\leq 0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad-e^{-r}\lesssim\frac{A_{\infty}}{rK_{0}^{2}(r)}-\partial_{r}\left(\frac{Q(r)}{K_{0}(r)}\right)\leq 0.

(B.20)

Another integration from
r
to
R
yields

\displaystyle-e^{-r}\lesssim\int_{r}^{R}\frac{A_{\infty}}{r^{\prime}K_{0}(r^{\prime})^{2}}dr^{\prime}+\frac{Q(r)}{K_{0}(r)}-\frac{Q(R)}{K_{0}(R)}\leq 0.

(B.21)

From Lemma
B.9
and
B.10
,
\frac{Q(R)}{K_{0}(R)}=O(\sqrt{R})
and
\frac{1}{r^{\prime}K_{0}(r^{\prime})^{2}}\sim e^{2r}
at
\infty
, so that by (
B.21
), it must be the case that
A_{\infty}=0
. Thus by (
B.20
), the function
\frac{Q}{K_{0}}
increases at infinity, and by (
B.21
), it is bounded, so it has a finite limit denoted
\kappa
at infinity. Notice that by the signs of
Q
and
K_{0}
, we have
\kappa>0
. So far we have obtained

\displaystyle-\frac{e^{-{2r}}}{\sqrt{r}}\lesssim Q(r)-\kappa K_{0}(r)\leq 0.

This result almost corresponds to the one of the lemma. To improve it, it suffices to notice that the bound in
Q
in Lemma
B.9
can be improved with the last inequality by
Q(r)\lesssim r^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-r}
. By redoing the computations from the beginning of the proof with this new bound, we obtain the result of the lemma.

Now we prove the equivalent of the derivative. By (
B.20
)

\displaystyle\left|Q^{\prime}(r)K_{0}(r)-Q(r)K_{0}^{\prime}(r)\right|\lesssim\frac{e^{-3r}}{r^{\frac{3}{2}}},

\displaystyle\left|Q^{\prime}(r)-K_{0}^{\prime}(r)\right|\lesssim\frac{\left|\left(Q(r)-K_{0}(r)\right)K_{0}^{\prime}(r)\right|}{K_{0}(r)}+\frac{e^{-3r}}{r^{\frac{3}{2}}K_{0}(r)}\lesssim\frac{e^{-2r}}{r}.

To obtain the next order derivative, it suffices to use the equation satisfied by
Q
and the previous estimates.
∎

Proof of Proposition
B.5
.

The proof is given by the triangle inequality with Lemma
B.10
and Lemma
B.11
.
∎

Proof of (
2.2
).

The proof that
\chi_{0}
is close to a rescaled version of
K_{0}
is similar to the one of Lemma
B.11
. Indeed, the rotationally invariant function
\chi_{0}
satisfies the following equations, with
\lambda_{0}>0
:

\displaystyle-\partial_{r}^{2}\chi_{0}(r)-\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}\chi_{0}(r)+\chi_{0}(r)-2Q(r)\chi_{0}(r)+\lambda_{0}\chi_{0}(r)=0,

\displaystyle-\partial_{r}^{2}\left(\chi_{0}\left(\frac{r}{\sqrt{1+\lambda_{0}}}\right)\right)-\left(\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}\right)\left(\chi_{0}\left(\frac{r}{\sqrt{1+\lambda_{0}}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\chi_{0}\left(\frac{r}{\sqrt{1+\lambda_{0}}}\right)-\frac{2}{\sqrt{1+\lambda_{0}}}Q\left(\frac{r}{\sqrt{1+\lambda_{0}}}\right)\chi_{0}\left(\frac{r}{\sqrt{1+\lambda_{0}}}\right)=0.

With the same computations as in Lemma
B.9
, we obtain the exponential decay of
\chi_{0}(\frac{\cdot}{\sqrt{1+\lambda_{0}}})
. As Lemma
B.11
, we obtain that:

\displaystyle\exists\kappa_{0}>0,\exists c>0,\forall r>1,\quad\left|\chi_{0}\left(\frac{r}{\sqrt{1+\lambda_{0}}}\right)-\kappa_{0}K_{0}(r)\right|\leq c\frac{e^{-2r}}{r}.

A last change of variable concludes the approximation (
2.2
).
∎

B.3.
Asymptotic expansion of a translated ground-state

We now continue with the asymptotic expansion of a ground-state translated in the first direction. We recall that
Q
is the ground state associated to (
1.5
).

Proposition B.12
.

There exist polynomials
\left(P_{q}(\mathbf{x})\right)_{q\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast}}
even in
y
and of degree at most
2q
such that the following holds. Let us define the functions

\displaystyle\forall n\in\mathbb{N}^{\ast},\forall z>1,\forall\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad Q_{app}^{n}(\mathbf{x},z):=\kappa\frac{e^{-z}}{z^{\frac{1}{2}}}e^{-x}\left(a_{0}+\sum_{1\leq q\leq n}\frac{P_{q}(\mathbf{x})}{z^{q}}\right),

where
\kappa>0
is given by Lemma
B.11
and
a_{0}=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}
.
Then, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
, there exists
C=C_{n}>0
, such that, for any
\mathbf{j}=(j_{x},j_{y})
in
\{0,1,2\}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\forall\beta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}),\forall z>4,\forall|\mathbf{x}|\leq z^{\beta},\quad\left|\partial_{\mathbf{x}}^{\mathbf{j}}Q(\mathbf{x}+(z,0))-\partial_{\mathbf{x}}^{\mathbf{j}}Q^{n}_{app}(\mathbf{x},z)\right|\leq C\frac{e^{-z-x}}{z^{\frac{1}{2}+(n+1)(1-2\beta)}}.

Proof.

By the condition
z>4
, we have
z-z^{\beta}>1
for
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
. Thus Proposition
B.5
applies to
Q(\mathbf{x}+(z,0))
7
7

7

Since
Q
is radial, we recall the identifications
Q(\mathbf{x})=Q(|\mathbf{x}|)
in the statement of Proposition (
B.5
)
, and we have for any
k\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\left|Q(\mathbf{x}+(z,0))-\kappa L_{k}(|(x+z,y)|)\right|\leq C_{k}\frac{e^{-|(x+z,y)|}}{|(x+z,y)|^{k+\frac{3}{2}}}\leq C_{k}\frac{e^{-z-x}}{z^{k+\frac{3}{2}}},

(B.22)

where for the last inequality, we have used
|x+z|=x+z\geq z-z^{\beta}>1
for
|x|\leq z^{\beta}
.

It suffices to develop the function
L_{k}(|(x+z,y)|)
, for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
large enough, to conclude the proof of the proposition. We introduce the rescaled variables
X:=\frac{x}{z}
,
Y:=\frac{y}{z}
and
\tilde{Y}:=\frac{y^{2}}{z}
. Note that
|X|<1
,
|Y|<1
and
|\tilde{Y}|<1
for
|\mathbf{x}|<z^{\beta}
under the condition
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
. Let us first begin with the power terms in
L_{k}
. We define

\displaystyle S_{l}(X,Y):=\frac{a_{l}}{z^{\frac{1}{2}+l}}\frac{1}{(1+X)^{\frac{1}{2}+l}}\left(1+\frac{Y^{2}}{(1+X)^{2}}\right)^{-(\frac{1}{2}+l)}

so that

\displaystyle L_{k}(|\mathbf{x}+(z,0)|)=\exp(-\left|\mathbf{x}+(z,0)\right|)\sum_{l=0}^{k}S_{l}(X,Y).

The power series of the functions
S_{l}
can be written for
(X,Y)\in(-1,1)^{2}
in the form

\displaystyle S_{l}(X,Y)=\frac{1}{z^{\frac{1}{2}+l}}\sum_{(s,t)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}b_{l,s,t}X^{s}Y^{2t},

(B.23)

for some real coefficients
b_{l,s,t}
with
b_{l,0,0}=a_{l}
, for any
l\in\mathbb{N}
. We continue with the expansion of the exponential term in
L_{k}
. By using the same variables, we rewrite the exponential term as

\displaystyle\exp\left(-|\mathbf{x}+(z,0)|\right)=\exp\left(-\left(z+x\right)R(X,Y)\right),

with

\displaystyle R(X,Y):=\left(1+\frac{Y^{2}}{(1+X)^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

We use the power series expansion of
R
for
(X,Y)\in(-1,1)^{2}
, with real coefficients
c_{j,m}
,

\displaystyle R(X,Y)=1+\sum_{j=1}^{+\infty}Y^{2j}\sum_{m=0}^{+\infty}c_{j,m}X^{m}.

By using this expansion, we have

\displaystyle\exp\left(-|\mathbf{x}+(z,0)|\right)

\displaystyle=e^{-z-x}\exp\left(-z\left(1+\frac{x}{z}\right)\left(R(X,Y)-1\right)\right)

\displaystyle=e^{-z-x}\exp\left(-(1+X)\tilde{Y}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{+\infty}Y^{2j}\sum_{m=0}^{+\infty}c_{j+1,m}X^{m}\right)\right).

Notice that the function defined by the double sum in the previous term converges for
(X,Y)\in(-1,1)^{2}
. We deduce the power series expansion for any
(X,Y,\tilde{Y})\in(-1,1)^{3}
, for some real coefficients
d_{j,m,p}
with
d_{0,0,0}=1
,

\displaystyle\exp\left(-|\mathbf{x}+(z,0)|\right)=e^{-z-x}\sum_{(j,m,p)\in\mathbb{N}^{3}}d_{j,m,p}Y^{2j}X^{m}\tilde{Y}^{p}.

(B.24)

With (
B.23
) and (
B.24
) in hands, we define

\displaystyle I_{q}:=\left\{\left(l,s,t,j,m,p\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{6};\quad l+s+2t+2j+m+p=q\right\},

and the polynomials
P_{q}
by

\displaystyle\forall q\in\mathbb{N},\quad P_{q}(\mathbf{x}):=\sum_{\left(l,s,t,j,m,p\right)\in I_{q}}b_{l,s,t}d_{j,m,p}x^{s+m}y^{2t+2j+2p}.

The polynomials
P_{q}
are even in
y
, at most of order
2k
, and the first polynomial
P_{0}
is given by
P_{0}(\mathbf{x})=a_{0}
.

For a given
n>0
, we choose
k>n
such that
z^{-(k+\frac{3}{2})}\lesssim z^{-(\frac{1}{2}+(n+1)(1-2\beta))}
. Hence, it follows from the above identities that

\displaystyle\left|L_{k}\left(|\mathbf{x}+(z,0)|\right)-\frac{e^{-z-x}}{z^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left(a_{0}+\sum_{q=1}^{n}\frac{P_{q}(x,y)}{z^{q}}\right)\right|\lesssim\frac{e^{-z-x}}{z^{\frac{1}{2}+(n+1)}}\left(x^{n+1}+y^{2(n+1)}\right)\lesssim\frac{e^{-z-x}}{z^{\frac{1}{2}+(n+1)(1-2\beta)}},

(B.25)

under the conditions
|(x,y)|\leq z^{\beta}
and
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
.

Therefore, we conclude the proof of Proposition
B.12
in the case
\mathbf{j}=(0,0)
by gathering (
B.22
) and (
B.25
).
The proof for
|\mathbf{j}|\geq 1
is similar.
∎

Appendix C
Estimates involving exponentially decaying functions

This appendix is dedicated to derive some useful estimates on exponentially decaying functions.

Lemma C.1
.

Let
f,\,g\in\mathcal{Y}
and let
Z^{*}\geq 1
be chosen large enough. Assume that
z>Z^{*}
and recall the notation
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}=(z,0)
. Then, the following estimates hold.

(i)

Pointwise estimate.

There exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that,
for all
\mathbf{x}=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\left|f\left(\mathbf{x}+\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}{2}\right)g\left(\mathbf{x}-\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}{2}\right)\right|\lesssim\min\left\{e^{-z}\left(z^{n}+\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{n}\right),e^{-\frac{31}{32}z}\left(e^{-\frac{|y|}{64}}{\bf 1}_{-\frac{z}{2}<x<\frac{z}{2}}+e^{-\frac{1}{64}\left|\left(x,y\right)\right|}\mathbf{1}_{|x|>\frac{z}{2}}\right)\right\}.

(C.1)

(ii)

L^{p}
-estimate.
For any
1\leq p<\infty
,

\displaystyle\left\|f\left(\cdot+\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}{2}\right)g\left(\cdot-\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}{2}\right)\right\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{15}{16}z}.

(C.2)

Proof.

We start by proving (
C.1
). In the case where
x>\frac{z}{2}
, we have
|\mathbf{x}+\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}{2}|\geq|x+\frac{z}{2}|>z
and
|\mathbf{x}+\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}{2}|\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|x|+|y|)\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}|(x,y)|
. Thus, it follows from the definition of
\mathcal{Y}
in (
1.24
) that, for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\left|f\left(\mathbf{x}+\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}{2}\right)g\left(\mathbf{x}-\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}{2}\right)\right|\lesssim e^{-\left|\mathbf{x}+\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}{2}\right|}\left\langle\left|\mathbf{x}+\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}{2}\right|\right\rangle^{n}\lesssim\min\left\{e^{-z}\left(z^{n}+\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{n}\right),e^{-\frac{31}{32}z}e^{-\frac{1}{64}\left|\left(x,y\right)\right|}\right\}.

The case where
x<-\frac{z}{2}
is treated in a similar way. In the case where
-\frac{z}{2}\leq x\leq\frac{z}{2}
, we have
|\mathbf{x}+\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}{2}|\geq|x+\frac{z}{2}|=x+\frac{z}{2}
and
|\mathbf{x}-\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}{2}|\geq|x-\frac{z}{2}|=\frac{z}{2}-x
, so that it follows for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\begin{split}\left|f\left(\mathbf{x}+\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}{2}\right)g\left(\mathbf{x}-\frac{\tilde{\mathbf{z}}}{2}\right)\right|&\lesssim\min\left\{e^{-z}\left(z^{n}+\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{n}\right),e^{-\frac{31}{32}(x+\frac{z}{2})}e^{-\frac{1}{64}|y|}e^{-\frac{31}{32}(\frac{z}{2}-x)}e^{-\frac{1}{64}|y|}\right\}\\
&\lesssim\min\left\{e^{-z}\left(z^{n}+\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle^{n}\right),e^{-\frac{31}{32}z}e^{-\frac{1}{64}\left|y\right|}\right\}.\end{split}

The proof of (
C.1
) follows then gathering these estimates, while the proof of (
C.2
) is a direct consequence of (
C.1
) and integration over
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
∎

Remark C.2
.

By arguing as in the proof of Lemma
C.1
(i) and using Proposition
B.5
, we deduce that for any
z>Z^{\star}
,
\mathbf{j}
and
\bf{k}
in
\mathbb{N}^{2}
with
|\mathbf{j}|,|\bf{k}|\leq 2
, and for all
\mathbf{x}=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\left|\partial^{\mathbf{j}}Q\left(\mathbf{x}+\tilde{\mathbf{z}}\right)\partial^{\bf{k}}Q(\mathbf{x})\right|\lesssim e^{-z}z^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

(C.3)

Remark C.3
.

Estimate (
C.2
) still holds under the weaker assumption that
f
,
g\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
satisfy
|f(\mathbf{x})|+|g(\mathbf{x})|\lesssim e^{-\frac{31}{32}|\mathbf{x}|}
. The proof is identical to the one of (
C.2
).

Lemma C.4
.

There exist
0<\nu^{*}\leq 1
small enough and
Z^{*}\geq 1
large enough such that the following holds. Let
R_{i}(\mathbf{x}):=Q_{1+\mu_{i}}\left(x-z_{i},y-\omega_{i}\right)
and
\tilde{R}_{i}(\mathbf{x}):=Q\left(x-z_{i},y\right)
, with

|\mu_{i}|\leq\nu^{*}
,
|\omega_{i}|\leq\nu^{*}
and
|z|>Z^{*}
, where
z=z_{1}-z_{2}
. Then, for
i=1,2
,

\displaystyle\left|R_{i}(\mathbf{x})-\tilde{R}_{i}(\mathbf{x})-\mu_{i}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{i}(\mathbf{x})-\omega_{i}\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right|\lesssim e^{-\frac{31}{32}|\mathbf{x}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{i}|}\left(\mu_{i}^{2}+\omega_{i}^{2}\right);

(C.4)

\displaystyle\left\|R_{i}-\tilde{R}_{i}-\mu_{i}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{i}-\omega_{i}\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{i}\right\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim\mu_{i}^{2}+\omega_{i}^{2}.

(C.5)

We also have,

\left\|\partial_{x}R_{i}-\partial_{x}\tilde{R}_{i}\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|\partial_{y}R_{i}-\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{i}\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|\Lambda R_{i}-\Lambda\tilde{R}_{i}\right\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim|\mu_{i}|+|\omega_{i}|.

(C.6)

Moreover, for any
\mathbf{j}
and
\bf{k}
in
\mathbb{N}^{2}
with
|\mathbf{j}|,|\bf{k}|\leq 2
,
1\leq p<\infty

\displaystyle\left\|\partial^{\mathbf{j}}R_{1}\partial^{\bf{k}}R_{2}\right\|_{L^{p}}+\left\|\partial^{\mathbf{j}}R_{1}\partial^{\bf{k}}\Lambda R_{2}\right\|_{L^{p}}+\left\|\partial^{\mathbf{j}}\Lambda R_{1}\partial^{\bf{k}}R_{2}\right\|_{L^{p}}+\left\|\partial^{\mathbf{j}}\Lambda R_{1}\partial^{\bf{k}}\Lambda R_{2}\right\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{15}{16}z};

(C.7)

\displaystyle\left\|R_{1}R_{2}-\tilde{R}_{1}\tilde{R}_{2}-\left(\mu_{1}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{1}+\omega_{1}\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{1}\right)\tilde{R}_{2}-\tilde{R}_{1}\left(\mu_{2}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{2}+\omega_{2}\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{2}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{15}{16}z}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\left(\mu_{j}^{2}+\omega_{j}^{2}\right).

(C.8)

Proof.

Estimate (
C.4
) is a direct application of Lemma
B.4
. Then it follows by integrating (
C.4
) over
\mathbb{R}^{2}
that

\left\|R_{i}-\tilde{R}_{i}-\mu_{i}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{i}-\omega_{i}\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{i}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\mu_{i}^{2}+\omega_{i}^{2}.

The estimates for the other pieces of the left-hand term in (
C.5
) are obtained arguing similarly.

Estimate (
C.6
) is proved arguing as in the proof of estimate (
C.5
) after deriving expansions for
\partial_{x}R_{i}
,
\partial_{y}R_{i}
and
\Lambda R_{i}
similar to the one for
R_{i}
in (
C.4
).

We explain how to deal with the estimate for
\left\|\int R_{1}R_{2}\right\|_{L^{p}}
in (
C.7
). The estimates for the other terms in (
C.7
) are similar. By using the decomposition of
R_{i}
in (
C.4
) and estimate (
C.2
) together with Remark

C.3
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\int R_{1}R_{2}-\int\left(\tilde{R}_{1}-\mu_{1}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{1}-\omega_{1}\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{1}\right)\left(\tilde{R}_{2}-\mu_{2}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{2}-\omega_{2}\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{2}\right)\right\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{15}{16}z}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(\mu_{i}^{2}+\omega_{i}^{2}\right).

Therefore, we conclude the proof of
C.7
by using (
C.2
).

Now we prove (
C.8
). By the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\left\|R_{1}R_{2}-\tilde{R}_{1}\tilde{R}_{2}-\left(\mu_{1}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{1}+\omega_{1}\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{1}\right)\tilde{R}_{2}-\tilde{R}_{1}\left(\mu_{2}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{2}+\omega_{2}\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{2}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\left(R_{1}-\tilde{R}_{1}-\mu_{1}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{1}-\omega_{1}\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{1}\right)\tilde{R}_{2}\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|\tilde{R}_{1}\left(R_{2}-\tilde{R}_{2}-\mu_{2}\Lambda\tilde{R}_{2}-\omega_{2}\partial_{y}\tilde{R}_{2}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}
