\displaystyle\qquad+\left\|\left(R_{1}-\tilde{R}_{1}\right)\left(R_{2}-\tilde{R}_{2}\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}.

Then, we conclude the proof of (
C.8
) by using (
C.2
) together with Remark
C.3
.
∎

Lemma C.5
.

Let
Z^{*}\geq 1
be chosen large enough. Assume that
z=z_{1}-z_{2}>Z^{*}
,
-z\leq z_{2}\leq-\frac{1}{4}z
,
\frac{1}{4}z\leq z_{1}\leq z
, and recall the definition of
X_{i}
,
W_{i}
and
l
in (
2.19
) and (
2.20
). Then, the following estimates hold.

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{R}_{1}X_{2}\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|\tilde{R}_{2}X_{1}\right\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{15}{16}z};

(C.9)

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{R}_{1}W_{2}\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|\tilde{R}_{1}Y_{2}\right\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{15}{16}z};

(C.10)

\displaystyle\left\|\tilde{R}_{2}\left(W_{1}-l\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|\tilde{R}_{2}\left(Y_{1}-h\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{15}{16}z}.

(C.11)

Proof.

Observe that
X_{i}(x,y)=(-\Delta+1)^{-1}Q(x-z_{i},y)
with
(-\Delta+1)^{-1}Q\in\mathcal{Y}
(see Lemma
A.2
). Thus, estimate (
C.9
) follows combining (
C.2
) with a change of variable.

Next, we prove estimate (
C.10
). We have from the definition of
W_{2}
and
Y_{2}
in (
2.19
), the one of
\Lambda Q
in (
1.28
) and lemma
A.2
that

\left|\tilde{R}_{1}W_{2}(x,y)\right|+\left|\tilde{R}_{1}Y_{2}(x,y)\right|\lesssim e^{-|(x-z_{1},y)|}\int_{x-z_{2}}^{+\infty}e^{-\frac{15}{16}|\tilde{x}|}d\tilde{x}.

In the case where
x>z_{1}
, we have
|x-z_{2}|=x-z_{2}>z
, so that

\left|\tilde{R}_{1}W_{2}(x,y)\right|+\left|\tilde{R}_{1}Y_{2}(x,y)\right|\lesssim e^{-\frac{15}{16}z}e^{-|(x-z_{1},y)|}.

In the case where
z_{2}<x<z_{1}
, we use that
|(x-z_{1},y)|\geq\frac{31}{32}(z_{1}-x)+\frac{1}{32}|y|
. Hence,

\left|\tilde{R}_{1}W_{2}(x,y)\right|+\left|\tilde{R}_{1}Y_{2}(x,y)\right|\lesssim e^{-\frac{31}{32}z}e^{-\frac{1}{32}|y|}{\bf 1}_{z_{2}<x<z_{1}}.

In the case where
x<z_{2}
, we have
|x-z_{1}|=z_{1}-x>z
, so that

\left|\tilde{R}_{1}W_{2}(x,y)\right|+\left|\tilde{R}_{1}Y_{2}(x,y)\right|\lesssim e^{-\frac{15}{16}z}e^{-\frac{1}{16}|(x-z_{1},y)|}.

We deduce combining these estimates and integrating over
\mathbb{R}^{2}
that

\left\|\tilde{R}_{1}W_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\tilde{R}_{1}Y_{2}\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{15}{16}z}.

The estimate

\left\|\nabla\left(\tilde{R}_{1}W_{2}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}+\left\|\nabla\left(\tilde{R}_{1}Y_{2}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{15}{16}z}

is proved in a similar way. This concludes the proof of (
C.10
).

Finally, we observe from the definitions of
W_{1}
,
Y_{1}
in (
2.19
) and
l
and
h
(
2.20
) that

\displaystyle\left|\left(\tilde{R}_{2}\left(W_{1}-l\right)\right)(x,y)\right|=\left|Q(x-z_{2},y)\int_{-\infty}^{x-z_{1}}\left(-\Delta+1\right)^{-1}\Lambda Q(\tilde{x},y)d\tilde{x}\right|

\displaystyle\left|\left(\tilde{R}_{2}\left(Y_{1}-h\right)\right)(x,y)\right|=\left|Q(x-z_{2},y)\int_{-\infty}^{x-z_{1}}\left(-\Delta+1\right)^{-1}\partial_{y}Q(\tilde{x},y)d\tilde{x}\right|

and thus

\displaystyle\left|\left(\tilde{R}_{2}\left(W_{1}-l\right)\right)(x,y)\right|+\left|\left(\tilde{R}_{2}\left(Y_{1}-h\right)\right)(x,y)\right|\lesssim e^{-|(x-z_{2},y)|}\int_{-\infty}^{x-z_{1}}e^{-\frac{15}{16}|\tilde{x}|}d\tilde{x}.

Hence, the proof of (
C.11
) follows arguing as in the proof of (
C.10
).
∎

Remark C.6
.

Under the assumptions of Lemmata
C.4
and
C.5
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|(R_{1}-\tilde{R}_{1})X_{2}\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|(R_{2}-\tilde{R}_{2})X_{1}\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|(R_{1}-\tilde{R}_{1})W_{2}\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|(R_{2}-\tilde{R}_{2})\left(W_{1}-l\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\|(R_{1}-\tilde{R}_{1})Y_{2}\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|(R_{2}-\tilde{R}_{2})\left(Y_{1}-h\right)\right\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim e^{-\frac{15}{16}z}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(|\mu_{i}|+|\omega_{i}|\right).

These estimates are proved by using (
C.4
) and arguing as in the proof of Lemma
C.5
.

Next, we give the proof of Claim
3.9
, which relies on the following estimates.

Lemma C.7
.

Let
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be such that
|f(\mathbf{x})|\lesssim e^{-\frac{7}{8}|\mathbf{x}|}
, for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and let
Z^{*}\geq 1
be chosen large enough. Assume that
z_{1}>Z^{*}
and
z_{2}<-Z^{*}
with for any
i
,
|z_{i}|>\frac{1}{4}z
and recall the notation
\tilde{\mathbf{z}_{i}}=(z_{i},0)
, for
i=1,2
. Then, the following estimates hold.
For all
\mathbf{x}=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\left|f\left(\mathbf{x}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{1}\right)(1-\psi)(x)\right|\lesssim e^{-\rho z}e^{-\frac{1}{8}|(x-z_{1},y)|};

(C.12)

\displaystyle\left|f\left(\mathbf{x}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{2}\right)\psi(x)\right|\lesssim e^{-\rho z}e^{-\frac{1}{8}|(x-z_{2},y)|};

(C.13)

\displaystyle\left|f\left(\mathbf{x}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{i}\right)\psi^{\prime}(x)\right|\lesssim e^{-\rho z}e^{-\frac{1}{8}|(x-z_{i},y)|},\quad i=1,2.

(C.14)

Proof.

We only prove (
C.12
), since the proofs of (
C.13
) and (
C.14
) are similar.

In the region
x>z_{1}/2
, observe from the definition of
\psi
and the second identity in (
3.15
), that
1-\psi(x)=\psi(-x)\lesssim e^{-8\rho x}\lesssim e^{-4\rho z_{1}}
. Since
f\in\mathcal{Y}
, it follows that

\left|f\left(\mathbf{x}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{1}\right)(1-\psi)(x)\right|\lesssim e^{-\rho z}e^{-\frac{1}{2}|(x-z_{1},y)|}.

In the region
x<z_{1}/2
, we have that
|(x-z_{1},y)|\geq|x-z_{1}|\geq\frac{|z_{1}|}{2}
. Thus,

\left|f\left(\mathbf{x}-\tilde{\mathbf{z}}_{1}\right)(1-\psi)(x)\right|\lesssim e^{-\frac{3}{8}z_{1}}e^{-\frac{1}{8}|(x-z_{1},y)|}.

Combining these two estimates yields (
C.12
).
∎

Proof of Claim
3.9
.

The proofs of estimates (
3.28
) and (
3.27
) are consequences of Lemma
C.7
and Lemma
B.4
.

By using the definition of
V
in (
2.25
), we observe that

V\psi^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{2}R_{i}\psi^{\prime}+V_{A}\psi^{\prime},

so that the proof of (
3.29
) follows from (
2.35
), (
2.35
), (
C.14
) and Lemma
B.4
.

By using the definitions of
V
in (
2.25
) and of
\psi_{+}
in (
3.17
), we decompose
\partial_{t}V+\partial_{x}V\psi_{+}
as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}V+\partial_{x}V\psi_{+}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(-(\dot{z}_{i}-\mu_{i})\partial_{x}R_{i}-\dot{\omega}_{i}\partial_{y}R_{i}+\dot{\mu}_{i}\Lambda R_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+(\mu_{1}-\mu_{2})\left((\psi-1)\partial_{x}R_{1}-\psi\partial_{x}R_{2}\right)+\partial_{t}V_{A}+\partial_{x}V_{A}\psi_{+}.

Thus, we conclude the proof of estimate (
3.30
) by gathering (
2.36
), (
2.37
), (
C.12
), (
C.13
) with
0<\rho<\frac{1}{32}
and Lemma
B.4
.

Finally, by using the definitions of
V
in (
2.25
) and of
\psi_{-,e}
and
\psi_{-,m}
in (
3.18
)-(
3.19
), we decompose
\partial_{t}V\psi_{-,e}+\partial_{x}V\psi_{-,m}
as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}V\psi_{-,e}+\partial_{x}V\psi_{-,m}

\displaystyle=-(\dot{z}_{1}-\mu_{1})\frac{\psi}{(1+\mu_{1})^{2}}\partial_{x}R_{1}-(\dot{z}_{2}-\mu_{2})\frac{1-\psi}{(1+\mu_{2})^{2}}\partial_{x}R_{2}-(\dot{z}_{1}-\mu_{2})\frac{1-\psi}{(1+\mu_{2})^{2}}\partial_{x}R_{1}

\displaystyle\quad-(\dot{z}_{2}-\mu_{1})\frac{\psi}{(1+\mu_{1})^{2}}\partial_{x}R_{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left(-\dot{\omega}_{i}\partial_{y}R_{i}+\dot{\mu}_{i}\Lambda R_{i}\right)\psi_{-,e}+\partial_{t}V_{A}\psi_{-,e}+\partial_{x}V_{A}\psi_{-,m}.

Hence, we conclude the proof of (
3.31
) by using (
2.36
), (
2.37
), (
C.12
), (
C.13
) with
0<\rho<\frac{1}{32}
and Lemma
B.4
, and recalling from
Assumption
2.3
that
|\mu_{1}|+|\mu_{2}|\leq C\nu_{0}^{\star}
, for
\nu_{0}^{\star}
small enough.
∎

Appendix D
Solutions to the second order dynamical system

The goal of this appendix is to study the solutions
Z
of the autonomous ODE

\displaystyle\ddot{Z}(t)=\frac{2}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q(x+Z(t),y)\partial_{x}(Q^{2})(x,y)dxdy.

(D.1)

The function
Z
is an approximation of the distance between the two solitary waves. In particular, at
-\infty
the two solitary waves are far away one from another and are repulsing at a velocity
-2\mu_{0}
, and thus, we should look for the solutions of (
D.1
) satisfying
\lim_{t\rightarrow-\infty}(Z(t),\dot{Z}(t))=(+\infty,-2\mu_{0})
. We also expect the collision to be almost symmetric, in the sense that
Z
should be even.

Remark D.1
.

In the one dimensional case of the gKdV equation, the authors in
[
41
]
use the first order asymptotic of (
D.1
),
\ddot{Z}=ce^{-Z}
, which admits explicit solutions.
In the
2
-dimensional case, the equation and its asymptotics cannot be solved explicitly. Instead, we describe the set of solutions by using ODE arguments.

D.1.
Phase portrait of the solutions

In this subsection, we consider for a positive constant
\kappa
, the ODE

\displaystyle\ddot{Z}(t)=\kappa\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q(x+Z(t),y)\partial_{x}\left(Q^{2}\right)(x,y)dxdy,

(D.2)

which corresponds to (
D.1
) in the case
\kappa=2\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle^{-1}
.
We define the associated Hamiltonian function

\displaystyle H(Y_{0},Y_{1}):=\frac{Y_{1}^{2}}{2}+\kappa\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q(x+Y_{0},y)Q^{2}(x,y)dxdy.

(D.3)

Lemma D.2
.

The Hamiltonian
H(Z(t),\dot{Z}(t))
is preserved along the trajectories of (
D.2
).

More precisely, let
I
be an interval containing
0
and let
Z:I\to\mathbb{R}
be a
C^{2}
solution to (
D.2
) with initial datum
(Z(0),\dot{Z}(0))=(Y_{0},Y_{1})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then,
H(Z(t),\dot{Z}(t))=H(Y_{0},Y_{1})
, for all
t\in I
.

Proof.

We observe that for any solution
Z
of (
D.2
),
\frac{d}{dt}H(Z(t),\dot{Z}(t))=0
. Hence, we conclude the proof by integrating between
0
and
t
.
∎

Proposition D.3
.

Let
(Y_{0},Y_{1})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then, there exists a unique solution
Z\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R}:\mathbb{R})
of (
D.2
) satisfying
\left(Z(0),\dot{Z}(0)\right)=(Y_{0},Y_{1})
.
Furthermore, the phase portrait of (
D.2
) is given by Figure
3
.

Z
\dot{Z}
\bullet

Figure 3.
Phase portrait of equation (
D.2
). In red: the constant solution. In blue: the four solutions defined on
\mathbb{R}
ending at
(0,0)
when
t
goes to
-\infty
or
+\infty
. In black: all the other trajectories.

Proof.

First, notice for
\tilde{Y}_{0},Y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
that

\displaystyle\left|\int Q(x+Y_{0},y)\partial_{x}Q^{2}(x,y)dxdy-\int Q(x+\tilde{Y}_{0},y)\partial_{x}Q^{2}(x,y)dxdy\right|\leq\|\partial_{x}Q\|_{L^{\infty}}\|Q\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\left|Y_{0}-\tilde{Y}_{0}\right|.

Therefore, for any initial condition
(Y_{0},Y_{1})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we deduce from the Cauchy-Lipschitz-Picard theorem the existence of a unique globally defined solution.

We now describe the phase portrait of the solutions. Let us first focus on initial conditions
(Y_{0},Y_{1})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
. For such initial conditions, we claim that the curve
\left\{(Z(t),\dot{Z}(t)):t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\right\}
remains in the domain
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
and that
(Z(t),\dot{Z}(t))\to(+\infty,l_{1})
when
t\to+\infty
, for some positive
l_{1}
.

Indeed, it follows form (
D.2
) that

\displaystyle\ddot{Z}(t)=\kappa\int_{y\in\mathbb{R}}\int_{x\geq 0}\left(Q(x+Z(t),y)-Q(-x+Z(t),y)\right)\partial_{x}\left(Q^{2}\right)(x,y)dxdy>0

since
\partial_{x}(Q^{2})
is odd in
x
and
Q
is positive and decreasing for
x\geq 0
. Thus
Z
is convex and
\dot{Z}(t)>Y_{1}>0
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. This proves that the curve remains in this domain. Furthermore,
Z
is bounded by below by
Y_{1}t
, so that
Z(t)
tends to
+\infty
at
+\infty
.

To study the limit of
\dot{Z}
, we use the Hamiltonian defined in (
D.3
). Observe that for a fixed
Y_{1}
(respectively
Y_{0}
), the function
H(\cdot,Y_{1})
is strictly decreasing (resp.
H(Y_{0},\cdot)
is strictly increasing). We deduce from Lemma
D.2
and (
C.3
) that

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow+\infty}\dot{Z}(t)^{2}=\lim_{t\rightarrow+\infty}2H(Z(t),\dot{Z}(t))=2H(Y_{0},Y_{1}).

(D.4)

Observe that each curve of the phase portrait in the region
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star}
corresponds to a different Hamiltonian. Indeed, if two initial conditions
(Y_{0},Y_{1})
and
(\tilde{Y}_{0},\tilde{Y}_{1})
are associated to the same Hamiltonian
H(Y_{0},Y_{1})=H(\tilde{Y}_{0},\tilde{Y}_{1})
, let us denote by
Z
and
\tilde{Z}
the two corresponding solutions. Assume without loss of generality that
Y_{0}<\tilde{Y}_{0}
. Then,
Z
is bijective from
[0,+\infty)
to
[Y_{0},+\infty)
, and thus
Z(t_{1})=\tilde{Y}_{0}
for some
t_{1}>0
. Since
H(Z(t_{1}),\dot{Z}(t_{1}))=H(Y_{0},Y_{1})=H(\tilde{Z}(0),\dot{\tilde{Z}}(0))
and
Y_{1}\mapsto H(\tilde{Y}_{0},Y_{1})
is strictly increasing on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we have
\dot{\tilde{Z}}(0)=\tilde{Y}_{1}=\dot{Z}(t_{1})
. By uniqueness of the Cauchy-Lipschitz-Picard theorem, we conclude that the two solutions are the same up to a time translation.

By (
D.4
), we conclude that each curve of the phase portrait has a different limit for
\dot{Z}
at infinity.

We continue by describing the set of solutions on the boundary of the domain of initial conditions
(Z(0),\dot{Z}(0))=(Y_{0},Y_{1})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
. First, notice that if
(Y_{0},Y_{1})=(0,0)
, then the solution is constant
Z(t)=0
. In the case where
(Y_{0},Y_{1})=(Z_{0},0)
with
Z_{0}>0
, then by strict convexity of
Z
, the graph of
(Z(t),\dot{Z}(t))
for any time
t>0
remains in
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
. In the case
(Y_{0},Y_{1})=(0,\mu_{1})
with
\mu_{1}>0
, we have
Z(t)>0
close to
0
and thus the graph remains in the same domain.

We deduce the description of whole set of solutions by symmetry from the set of solutions corresponding to initial conditions in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
. This concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Corollary D.4
.

Let
l_{1}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
. There exists a unique solution, up to time translation, to (
D.2
) with the condition

\displaystyle\lim_{t\rightarrow-\infty}(Z(t),\dot{Z}(t))=(+\infty,-l_{1}).

(D.5)

Proof.

Let us split the different possibilities of
l_{1}
, and compare with the hamiltonian (
D.3
). We begin with the existence of a solution.

•

l_{1}^{2}<2\kappa\|Q\|_{L^{3}}^{3}
. In this case, define
Y_{0}
as the unique positive real coefficient such that

\displaystyle l_{1}^{2}=2\kappa\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q(x+Y_{0},y)Q^{2}(x,y)dxdy.

(D.6)

The solution
Z
associated to the initial condition
(Y_{0},0)
has the hamiltonian
H(Y_{0},0)=\frac{l_{1}^{2}}{2}
, and thus is a solution to the problem by (
D.4
).

•

l_{1}^{2}>2\kappa\|Q\|_{L^{3}}^{3}
. In this case, define
Y_{1}
as the unique negative real coefficient such that to

\displaystyle Y_{1}^{2}=l_{1}^{2}-2\kappa\|Q\|_{L^{3}}^{3}.

The solution
Z
associated to the initial condition
(0,Y_{1})
has the hamiltonian
H(0,Y_{1})=\frac{l_{1}^{2}}{2}
, and is a solution to the problem by (
D.4
).

•

l_{1}^{2}=2\kappa\|Q\|_{L^{3}}^{3}
. This case corresponds to the blue curve on the phase portrait, ending at
(+\infty,-l_{1})
as
t\rightarrow-\infty
and to
(0,0)
as
t\rightarrow+\infty
.

By the previous discussion on the phase portrait, each of the solution is uniquely determined by the hamiltonian, thus the uniqueness of the solution up to time translation.
∎

Lemma D.5

(Asymptote)
.

Let
0<l_{1}
with
l_{1}^{2}<2\kappa\|Q\|_{L^{3}}^{3}
. Then the unique solution
Z
of (
D.2
) with the initial condition
(Z(0),\dot{Z}(0))=(Y_{0},0)
, with
Y_{0}
defined in (
D.6
), is even and has an asymptote at
+\infty
. More precisely, there exist constants
l=l(l_{1})\in\mathbb{R}
and
c=c(l_{1})>0
such that

\displaystyle\left|Z(t)-l_{1}t-l\right|\lesssim e^{-ct}.

Proof.

If
Z(t)
is a solution to (
D.2
) with the initial condition
(Y_{0},0)
, then
t\mapsto Z(-t)
is also a solution, and by uniqueness we have
Z(t)=Z(-t)
. We can thus focus on the case
t\geq 0
.

Since
Z(t)>0
, for all
t>0
(from Proposition
D.3
),

\displaystyle\ddot{Z}(t)\geq\kappa\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q(x+Z,y)\partial_{x}(Q^{2})(x,y)dxdy>0.

Hence, for a fixed
t_{0}>0
and any
t>t_{0}
, we have
Z(t)>\dot{Z}(t_{0})(t-t_{0})
with
\dot{Z}(t_{0})>0
. Moreover, it follows from Lemma
D.2
that

\displaystyle\frac{l_{1}^{2}}{2}=H(Z(t),\dot{Z}(t))=\frac{\dot{Z}(t)^{2}}{2}+\kappa\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q(x+Z(t),y)Q^{2}(x,y)dxdy.

(D.7)

As underlined in the proof of Proposition
D.3
, the function
Z\mapsto\int Q(x+Z,y)Q^{2}(x,y)dxdy
is strictly decreasing on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Thus for
t\geq t_{0}

\displaystyle l_{1}^{2}-2\kappa\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q(x+\dot{Z}(t_{0})(t-t_{0}),y)Q^{2}(x,y)dxdy\leq\dot{Z}(t)^{2}.

From Lemma
C.1
, there exist two positive constants
c
and
C
such that, for
t
large enough,

l_{1}^{2}-Ce^{-ct}\leq\dot{Z}(t)^{2}
, and thus
0<l_{1}-\dot{Z}(t)\leq\frac{C}{l_{1}}e^{-ct}
.
An integration from
t
to
+\infty
yields

\displaystyle 0\leq\lim_{s\rightarrow+\infty}\left(l_{1}s-Z(s)\right)-l_{1}t+Z(t)\lesssim e^{-ct}.

Since
l_{1}t-Z(t)
is concave and bounded on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, it has a finite limit
l
at
+\infty
, which concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

The following proposition gives an approximation of the right-hand-side of (
D.2
) for
Z
large.

Proposition D.6
.

There exist two positive constants
Z^{\star}
and
c
such that it holds, for any
Z>Z^{\star}
and
i\in\{0,1,2\}
,

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q(x+Z,y)\partial_{x}^{i}(Q^{2})(x,y)dxdy-(-1)^{i}c\frac{e^{-Z}}{\sqrt{Z}}\right|\lesssim\frac{e^{-Z}}{Z},

(D.8)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\partial_{x}Q+Q\right)(x+Z,y)Q^{2}(x,y)dxdy\right|\lesssim\frac{e^{-Z}}{Z}.

(D.9)

Remark D.7
.

Proposition
D.6
echoes Remark
D.1
. Indeed, it follows from (
D.8
) that the asymptotic of (
D.1
) has the form
\ddot{Z}=ce^{-Z}Z^{-\frac{1}{2}}
which does not possess explicit solutions, unlike its
1
-dimensional equivalent
\ddot{Z}=ce^{-Z}
(see
[
41
]
).

Proof.

We only prove estimate (
D.8
) in the case
i=0
, since the proof for the other cases is similar. On the one hand, it follows by using (
C.3
) and taking
Z^{\star}
large enough that

\displaystyle\int_{|(x,y)|\geq Z^{\frac{1}{4}}}Q(x+Z,y)Q^{2}(x,y)dxdy

\displaystyle\leq C\sup_{(x,y)}\left(Q(x+Z,y)Q(x,y)\right)\int_{|(x,y)|>Z^{\frac{1}{4}}}|Q(x,y)|dxdy\lesssim e^{-Z-Z^{\frac{1}{4}}}.

On the other hand, we infer from the asymptotic expansion of Proposition
B.12
that

\displaystyle\left|\int_{|(x,y)|\leq Z^{\frac{1}{4}}}Q(x+Z,y)Q^{2}(x,y)dxdy-\frac{e^{-Z}}{Z^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left(\kappa a_{0}\int_{|(x,y)|\leq Z^{\frac{1}{4}}}e^{-x}Q^{2}(x,y)dxdy\right)\right|\lesssim\frac{e^{-Z}}{Z}.

Therefore, we conclude the proof of (
D.8
) in the case
i=0
by gathering these two estimates.

Estimate (
D.9
) follows by combining (
D.8
) in the case
i=0
with (
D.8
) in the case
i=1
and observing that, after integration by parts, the terms of order
e^{-Z}Z^{-\frac{1}{2}}
cancel each other out. Note that it is important that the constant
c
appearing in (
D.8
) is independent of
i\in\{0,1\}
.
∎

D.2.
Quantified evolution of a function
z
close to
Z

While the previous subsection dealt with the general equation (
D.2
) for any arbitrary positive constant
\kappa
, this subsection focuses on the ODEs (
D.2
) which are close to (
D.1
). More precisely, for a parameter
\nu\in(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
, we are interested in the following equation and its associated Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\ddot{Z}(t)=\frac{2(1+\nu)}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q(x+Z(t),y)\partial_{x}(Q^{2})(x,y)dxdy

(D.10)

\displaystyle H_{\nu}(Y_{0},Y_{1}):=\frac{Y_{1}^{2}}{2}+\frac{2(1+\nu)}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q(x+Y_{0},y)Q^{2}(x,y)dxdy.

(D.11)

Recall from (
B.2
) that
\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle>0
. The case
\nu=0
corresponds to (
D.1
), where
Z
is a good approximation of the distance
z
between the
2
solitary waves during the collision. However, due to the error terms,
z
is not an exact solution of (
D.1
). This is why we study the set of equations (
D.10
) with a small parameter
\nu
.

Lemma D.8

(Equivalent of
l_{1}
for
Y_{0}
large)
.

There exist
Y^{\star}>0
large enough and two positive constants
c
and
C
such that the following holds. For any
\nu\in(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
and
Y_{0}>Y^{\star}
, define
l_{1}>0
by
H_{\nu}(Y_{0},0)=\frac{l_{1}^{2}}{2}
. Then the following holds

\displaystyle c\frac{e^{-Y_{0}}}{\sqrt{Y_{0}}}\leq l_{1}^{2}\leq C\frac{e^{-Y_{0}}}{\sqrt{Y_{0}}}.

(D.12)

Proof.

From the definition of
H_{\nu}
in (
D.11
), we have

\displaystyle l_{1}^{2}=\frac{4(1+\nu)}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q(x+Y_{0},y)Q^{2}(x,y)dxdy,

which implies (
D.12
) by using (
D.8
) with
i=0
and choosing
Y^{\star}
large enough. Note that the constants
c
and
C
can be chosen uniformly in
\nu\in(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
.
∎

Lemma D.9

(Uniform lower bound of
\dot{Z}
close to
0
)
.

There exist
Y^{\star}>0
,
\eta^{\star}>0
small and positive constants
c
and
C
such that the following holds. For any
Y_{0}>Y^{\star}
,
\nu\in(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2})
and
\eta\in(0,\eta^{\star})
, consider
Z
the unique even
\mathcal{C}^{2}
-function solving
H_{\nu}(Z,\dot{Z})=H_{\nu}(Y_{0},0)
and
T
the unique positive time such that
Z(T)=Y_{0}+\eta^{2}
. Denote
l_{1}
as in (
D.6
). Then

\displaystyle c\eta l_{1}\leq\dot{Z}(T)\leq C\eta l_{1}.

Proof.

Recall that the existence and uniqueness of the even function
Z
are given in Proposition
D.3
and Corollary
D.4
. The conservation of the Hamiltonian in (
D.11
) associated to
Z
implies that

\displaystyle\dot{Z}(T)^{2}=-\frac{4(1+\nu)}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\int_{0}^{Z(T)-Y_{0}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\partial_{x}Q(x+Y_{0}+s,y)Q^{2}(x,y)dxdy\right)ds.

Let us find an equivalent on the inner integral for
Y_{0}
large enough and a fixed value
s>0
.

Observe that
0\leq Z(T)-Y_{0}=\eta^{2}
. Inspired from the computation of (
B.11
), we adjust
\eta^{\star}
so that for
s\leq\eta^{2}<(\eta^{\star})^{2}
, it holds on
\mathbb{R}^{2}

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{x}Q(x+Y_{0}+s,y)-\partial_{x}Q(x+Y_{0},y)\right|\lesssim s\frac{e^{-\left|(x+Y_{0},y)\right|}}{\langle(x+Y_{0},y)\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

Thus, it follows arguing as in (
C.3
) that

\displaystyle\left|\int_{s=0}^{Z(T)-Y_{0}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\partial_{x}Q\left(x+Y_{0}+s,y\right)-\partial_{x}Q(x+Y_{0},y)\right)Q^{2}(x,y)dxdyds\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\eta^{4}\sup\left(\frac{e^{-\left|(x+Y_{0},y)\right|}}{\langle(x+Y_{0},y)\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}}Q(x,y)\right)\|Q\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim\eta^{4}\frac{e^{-Y_{0}}}{Y_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

Then, we deduce from (
D.9
) that

\displaystyle\left|\dot{Z}(T)^{2}-\frac{4(1+\nu)}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\int_{s=0}^{Z(T)-Y_{0}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q(x+Y_{0},y)Q^{2}(x,y)dxdyds\right|\leq\int_{s=0}^{Z(T)-Y_{0}}C\frac{e^{-Y_{0}}}{Y_{0}}ds+C\eta^{4}\frac{e^{-Y_{0}}}{Y_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}},

which combined with (
D.6
) yields

\displaystyle\left|\dot{Z}(T)^{2}-\eta^{2}l_{1}^{2}\right|\leq\eta^{2}\left(C\frac{e^{-Y_{0}}}{Y_{0}}+C\eta^{2}\frac{e^{-Y_{0}}}{Y_{0}^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right).

By using Lemma
D.8
, where the constants can be chosen uniformly in
\nu
, we obtain that for
l_{1}
small enough

\displaystyle\eta^{2}l_{1}^{2}\left(1-\frac{C}{|\ln(l_{1})|^{\frac{1}{2}}}-C\eta^{2}\right)\leq\dot{Z}(T)^{2}\leq\eta^{2}l_{1}^{2}\left(1+\frac{C}{|\ln(l_{1})|^{\frac{1}{2}}}+C\eta^{2}\right).

We conclude the proof of the lemma by choosing
l_{1}^{\star}
and
\eta^{\star}
smaller if necessary and by taking the square root of the previous estimate.
∎

The next proposition quantifies the defect of closeness of a function to a solution to the equation (
D.10
). More precisely, let us denote by
Z
the even solution to (
D.10
) with
\nu=0
. If a function
z
defined on a time interval
[t_{0},t_{1}]
is close to
Z
at time
t_{0}
and the flows
H_{\pm\nu}(z,\dot{z})
are well controlled along the time, then
z
is close to
Z
at any time
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
.

Proposition D.10

(Evolution of
(z,\dot{z})
with
H_{0}(z,\dot{z})
almost constant)
.

Let
\eta\in(0,1)
. There exist some constants
C>0
,
\varepsilon_{0}^{\star}
and
\nu_{6}^{\star}\leq\frac{1}{2}
such that the following holds. Let
\nu\in(0,\nu_{6}^{\star})
and
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}^{\star})
. Let us define a constant
h<H_{-\nu_{6}^{\star}}(0,0)
small and the three hamiltonian
H:=H_{0}
,
H_{+}:=H_{\nu}
and
H_{-}:=H_{-\nu}
. We set
Z
the unique even
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-function such that
H(Z,\dot{Z})=h
and
t_{0}>0
satisfying
Z(t_{0})\geq Z(0)+\eta^{2}
. Let a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-function
z
defined on a time interval
[t_{0},t_{1}]
that satisfies the initial condition

\displaystyle\left|z(t_{0})-Z(t_{0})\right|\leq\varepsilon_{0},

and for any
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]

\displaystyle H_{-}(z(t),\dot{z}(t))\leq h\leq H_{+}(z(t),\dot{z}(t)).

(D.13)

Then for any
t
in
[t_{0},t_{1}]
we have

\displaystyle\left|z(t)-Z(t)\right|\leq C\nu+C\varepsilon_{0}.

Z_{-}
\dot{Z}_{-}
h_{0}
h
h_{1}
Z
\dot{Z}
h_{0}
h
h_{1}
Z_{+}
\dot{Z}_{+}
h_{0}
h
h_{1}

Figure 4.
Comparison of a trajectory
(z,\dot{z})
in red with the phase portrait associated to the equations related to
H_{-}
(left),
H
(middle) and
H_{+}
(right). On each graph, the three lines are associated to three values
h_{0}<h<h_{1}
.

Remark D.11
.

In the proof of the collision, we will set
h=2\mu_{0}^{2}
.

Proof.

For a constant
C_{\star}
to define later, define the inequality

\displaystyle\left|z(t)-Z(t)\right|\leq C_{\star}\nu+C_{\star}\varepsilon_{0}.

(D.14)

The coefficients
\nu_{6}^{\star}
and
\varepsilon_{0}^{\star}
are adjusted so that

\displaystyle C_{\star}\nu_{6}^{\star}+C_{\star}\varepsilon_{0}^{\star}\leq\frac{\eta^{2}}{4}.

(D.15)

Define the time

\displaystyle T^{\star}:=\sup\left\{t\in[t_{0};t_{1}];\,\forall t\in[t_{0};T^{\star}),\text{ estimate }\eqref{bootstrap_estimate_z_Z}\text{ holds}\right\}.

T^{\star}
is well-defined by assumption and continuity of
z
and
Z
. By a bootstrap argument, we claim that
T^{\star}=t_{1}
. We suppose that
T^{\star}<t_{1}
and show that this statement leads to a contradiction. To do so, we compare the two functions
z
and
Z
along the flow of two well-chosen functions
Z_{-}
and
Z_{+}
associated to the two hamiltonian
H_{+}
and
H_{-}
. We thus define
Z_{-}
and
Z_{+}
as the unique even functions satisfying

\displaystyle H_{-}(Z_{-},\dot{Z}_{-})=h,\quad\text{and}\quad H_{+}(Z_{+},\dot{Z}_{+})=h.

(D.16)

Those two functions are represented in blue in Figure
4
. Remark that the constraint
h<H_{-\nu_{6}^{\star}}(0,0)
justifies that the function
Z_{-}
is associated to an initial condition under the form
(Y_{0},0)
for
Y_{0}>0
, and not under the form
(0,Y_{1})
as explained in Proposition
D.3
.
Z
(respectively
Z_{-}
,
Z_{+}
) goes from
\mathbb{R}
to
(Z(0);+\infty)
(resp.
Z_{-}(0)
,
Z_{+}(0)
). By abuse of notations, we denote by
Z^{-1}
(resp.
Z_{-}^{-1}
,
Z_{+}^{-1}
) the bijection from
(Z(0);+\infty)
(resp.
Z_{-}(0)
,
Z_{+}(0)
) to
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
.

We will make use of the following claims, whose proofs are postponed.

Claim D.12

(Comparison of the three functions)
.

At the initial time, we have

\displaystyle Z_{-}(0)<Z(0)<Z_{+}(0).

(D.17)

In particular, the function
\nu\mapsto Z_{+}(0)
for
\nu
close to
0
is a decreasing function and

\displaystyle\lim_{\nu\rightarrow 0}Z_{+}(0)=Z(0).

(D.18)

For any
s>Z_{+}(0)
it holds

\displaystyle\dot{Z}_{+}\circ Z_{+}^{-1}(s)\leq\dot{Z}\circ Z^{-1}(s)\leq\dot{Z}_{-}\circ Z_{-}^{-1}(s).

(D.19)

Claim D.13

(Comparison of the derivatives of the inverse functions)
.

The two following inequalities hold

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\dot{Z}\circ Z^{-1}(s)}-\frac{1}{\dot{Z}_{-}\circ Z_{-}^{-1}(s)}\leq\frac{2\nu}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\frac{\int Q(x+s,y)Q^{2}(x,y)dxdy}{\left(\dot{Z}\circ Z^{-1}(s)\right)^{3}},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\dot{Z}\circ Z^{-1}(s)}-\frac{1}{\dot{Z}_{+}\circ Z_{+}^{-1}(s)}\geq-\frac{2\nu}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\frac{\int Q(x+s,y)Q^{2}(x,y)dxdy}{\left(\dot{Z}_{+}\circ Z_{+}^{-1}(s)\right)^{3}}.

To continue the proof of (
D.14
), we split the inequality into the lower and the upper bound.

Upper bound of (
D.14
)
. We prove in this part an upper bound of the quantity
z(t)-Z(t)
in terms of the initial condition
z(t_{0})-Z(t_{0})
and
\nu
. Notice first that
Z_{-}
is a bijection from
(0;+\infty)
to
(Z_{-}(0);+\infty)
. From (
D.17
), the bootstrap assumption (
D.14
) and the restriction (
D.15
) on
\epsilon_{0}
and
\nu
, we have for any
t\in[t_{0},T^{\star})
and the assumption on
t_{0}

\displaystyle Z_{-}(0)<Z(0)<Z(0)+\frac{3}{4}\eta^{2}\leq z(t).

The functions
Z_{-}^{-1}\circ z
and
Z_{-}^{-1}\circ Z
are well-defined on
[t_{0};T^{\star})
.
We now study the derivative of the difference of those functions for
t\in[t_{0},T^{\star})
. The assumption (
D.13
) implies the bound

\displaystyle\dot{Z}_{-}\circ Z_{-}^{-1}\circ z(t)=\left(2h-\frac{4(1-\nu)}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Q(x+z(t),y)Q^{2}(x,y)dxdy\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\geq\dot{z}(t)

and thus

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(Z_{-}^{-1}\circ z(t)-Z_{-}^{-1}\circ Z(t)\right)\leq\dot{Z}(t)\left(\frac{1}{\dot{Z}\circ Z^{-1}}-\frac{1}{\dot{Z}_{-}\circ Z_{-}^{-1}}\right)\circ Z(t).

(D.20)

Plugging the estimate of Claim
D.13
in (
D.20
) with Proposition
D.6
with the assumption of
h
small, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(Z_{-}^{-1}\circ z(t)-Z_{-}^{-1}\circ Z(t)\right)\leq\frac{4\nu}{\dot{Z}(t)^{2}}\ddot{Z}(t).

An integration from
t_{0}
to
t\in(t_{0},T^{\star})
with the lower bound provided by Lemma
D.9
yields

\displaystyle\left(Z_{-}^{-1}\circ z(t)-Z_{-}^{-1}\circ Z(t)\right)-\left(Z_{-}^{-1}\circ z(t_{0})-Z_{-}^{-1}\circ Z(t_{0})\right)\leq\frac{4\nu}{c\eta l_{1}}.

We continue using the mean value theorem which states that for any
t\in[t_{0},T^{\star})
there exists a coefficient
\lambda_{t}\in\left[Z(t)-C_{\star}\left(\nu+\varepsilon_{0}\right),Z(t)+C_{\star}\left(\nu+\varepsilon_{0}\right)\right]
such that

\displaystyle Z_{-}\circ z(t)-Z_{-}^{-1}\circ Z(t)=\frac{z(t)-Z(t)}{\dot{Z}_{-}\circ Z_{-}^{-1}(\lambda_{t})}.

Notice that the previous quantity is well-defined since
\lambda_{t}>Z_{0}+\frac{3}{4}\eta^{2}>Z_{-}(0)
, and we have

\displaystyle z(t)-Z(t)\leq\frac{\dot{Z}_{-}\circ Z_{-}^{-1}(\lambda_{t})}{\dot{Z}_{-}\circ Z_{-}^{-1}(\lambda_{t_{0}})}\left(z(t_{0})-Z(t_{0})\right)+\frac{4\nu}{c\eta l_{1}}\dot{Z}_{-}\circ Z_{-}^{-1}(\lambda_{t}).

In particular, Lemma
D.9
applied to
\lambda_{t}>Z_{-}(0)+\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}
yields
\dot{Z}_{-}\circ Z_{-}^{-1}(\lambda_{t})\geq c2^{-\frac{1}{2}}\eta l_{1}
. We thus obtain with
\dot{Z}_{-}\leq l_{1}

\displaystyle z(t)-Z(t)\leq\frac{\dot{Z}_{-}\circ Z_{-}^{-1}(\lambda_{t})}{\dot{Z}_{-}\circ Z_{-}^{-1}(\lambda_{t_{0}})}\left(z(t_{0})-Z(t_{0})\right)+\frac{4\nu}{c\eta}\quad\text{with}\quad\frac{\dot{Z}_{-}\circ Z_{-}^{-1}(\lambda_{t})}{\dot{Z}_{-}\circ Z_{-}^{-1}(\lambda_{t_{0}})}\in\left[\frac{c}{\sqrt{2}},\frac{\sqrt{2}}{c\eta}\right].

(D.21)

Lower bound of (
D.14
)
The proof of the lower bound on
z(t)-Z(t)
follows similar ideas, let us emphasize the main differences. First, the functions
Z_{+}^{-1}\circ Z
and
Z_{+}^{-1}\circ Z
are well-defined on
[t_{0},T^{\star})
. Indeed, by the limit in (
D.18
), decreasing
\nu^{\star}
if necessary and the assumption (
D.15
), we have

\displaystyle Z(0)<Z_{+}(0)<Z(0)+\frac{\eta^{2}}{4}<Z(0)+\frac{3}{4}\eta^{2}<z(t).

These inequalities justify the functions
Z_{+}^{-1}\circ Z
and
Z_{+}^{-1}\circ z
. Since Proposition
D.6
provides the inequality

\displaystyle\frac{2\nu}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\int Q(x+Z(t),y)Q^{2}(x,y)dxdy\leq 2\nu\ddot{Z}_{+}\circ Z_{+}^{-1}\circ Z(t)

we can compute, as for
Z_{-}
, the time derivative of the difference of the functions and using assumption (
D.13
) and Claim (
D.13
)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(Z_{+}^{-1}\circ z-Z_{+}^{-1}\circ Z\right)(t)\geq-2\nu\frac{\ddot{Z}_{+}\circ Z_{+}^{-1}\circ Z(t)}{\left(\dot{Z}_{+}\circ Z_{+}^{-1}\circ Z(t)\right)^{3}}\dot{Z}(t).

An integration from
t_{0}
to
t
yields, by the change of variable
s^{\prime}=Z_{+}^{-1}\circ Z(s)

\displaystyle\left(Z_{+}^{-1}\circ z-Z_{+}^{-1}\circ Z\right)(t)-\left(Z_{+}^{-1}\circ z-Z_{+}^{-1}\circ Z\right)(t_{0})

\displaystyle\qquad\geq 2\nu\left(\frac{1}{\dot{Z}_{+}\circ Z_{+}^{-1}\circ Z(t)}-\frac{1}{\dot{Z}_{+}\circ Z_{+}^{-1}\circ Z(t_{0})}\right).

By the bootstrap assumption (
D.14
) and the mean value theorem, for any
t\in[t_{0},T^{\star})
, there exists
\lambda_{t}
such that
\left|\lambda_{t}-Z(t)\right|\leq C_{\star}\nu+C_{\star}\varepsilon_{0}
and

\displaystyle Z_{+}^{-1}\circ z(t)-Z_{+}^{-1}\circ Z(t)=\frac{1}{\dot{Z}_{+}\circ Z_{+}^{-1}(\lambda_{t})}(z(t)-Z(t)).

Since
Z_{+}
increases on
[0,+\infty)
, we obtain the inequality

\displaystyle z(t)-Z(t)\geq\frac{\dot{Z}_{+}\circ Z_{+}^{-1}(\lambda_{t})}{\dot{Z}_{+}\circ Z_{+}^{-1}(\lambda_{t_{0}})}(z(t_{0})-Z(T_{0}))-2\nu\frac{\dot{Z}_{+}\circ Z_{+}^{-1}(\lambda_{t})}{\dot{Z}_{+}\circ Z_{+}^{-1}\circ Z(t_{0})}.

Since
Z(t)>Z(0)+\frac{3}{4}\eta^{2}>Z_{+}(0)+\frac{1}{2}\eta^{2}
for any
t\in[t_{0},T^{\star})
, by applying Lemma
D.9
, we have
\dot{Z}_{+}\circ Z_{+}^{-1}\circ Z(t_{0})\geq c\eta l_{1}
. We thus obtain the lower bound

\displaystyle z(t)-Z(t)\geq\frac{\dot{Z}_{+}\circ Z_{+}^{-1}(\lambda_{t})}{\dot{Z}_{+}\circ Z_{+}^{-1}(\lambda_{t_{0}})}(z(t_{0})-Z(T_{0}))-\frac{2\nu}{c\eta}\quad\text{with}\quad\frac{\dot{Z}_{+}\circ Z_{+}^{-1}(\lambda_{t})}{\dot{Z}_{+}\circ Z_{+}^{-1}(\lambda_{t_{0}})}\in\left[\frac{c}{\sqrt{2}},\frac{\sqrt{2}}{c\eta}\right].

(D.22)

Gathering the upper bound (
D.21
) and lower bound (
D.22
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\left|z(t)-Z(t)\right|\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{c\eta}\varepsilon_{0}+\frac{4}{c\eta}\nu.

By choosing
C_{\star}>4(c\eta)^{-1}
, we strictly improve the bound on
z(t)-Z(t)
, which contradicts the maximality of
T^{\star}
. Thus
T^{\star}=t_{1}
, and the proposition is proved.
∎

Proof of Claim
D.12
.

By the definition of the Hamiltonian we have

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\int Q(\cdot+Z(0),\cdot)Q^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{2(1-\nu)}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\int Q(\cdot+Z_{-}(0),\cdot)Q^{2}=\frac{2(1+\nu)}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\int Q(\cdot+Z_{+}(0),\cdot)Q^{2}

\displaystyle=h.

Since
\nu
is positive, we obtain
D.17
. Under the previous form, the statement on the limit of
\nu
is straightforward.

Concerning the second inequality, all the derivatives are positive and by the definition of the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\nu}\left(2h-\dot{Z}_{-}\circ Z_{-}^{-1}(s)^{2}\right)=2h-\dot{Z}\circ Z^{-1}(s)^{2}=\frac{1}{1+\nu}\left(2h-\dot{Z}_{+}\circ Z_{+}^{-1}(s)^{2}\right)>0,

which proves (
D.19
) since
\nu>0
.
∎

Proof of Claim
D.13
.

Let us begin with the proof of the inequality with
Z_{-}
. By developing, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\dot{Z}\circ Z^{-1}(s)}-\frac{1}{\dot{Z}_{-}\circ Z_{-}^{-1}(s)}=\frac{4\nu}{\langle\Lambda Q,Q\rangle}\frac{\int Q(x+s,y)Q^{2}(x,y)dxdy}{\dot{Z}\circ Z^{-1}(s)\dot{Z}_{-}\circ Z_{-}^{-1}(s)\left(\dot{Z}_{-}\circ Z_{-}^{-1}(s)+\dot{Z}\circ Z^{-1}(s)\right)}.

Then, estimate (
D.19
) concludes the proof of the first inequality. The second inequality holds with the same computations with the other side of (
D.19
).
∎

In the previous proposition, the constraint on the time interval
[t_{0},t_{1}]
is that the time interval is far enough from
0
so that
Z(t_{0})>Z(0)+\eta^{2}
. The coefficient
\epsilon_{0}^{\star}
and
\nu_{6}^{\star}
are thus adapted in terms of
\eta
. A similar statement thus holds on a time interval
t_{0}<t_{1}<0
.

Proposition D.14
.

With the notations of Proposition
D.10
, consider the same assumptions and definitions on
\eta
,
C
,
\nu_{6}^{\star}
,
\varepsilon_{0}^{\star}
,
h
,
H
,
H_{+}
,
H_{-}
and
Z
. Let
t_{1}<0
satisfying
Z(t_{1})\geq Z(0)+\eta^{2}
. Let
z
be a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-function defined on a time interval
[t_{0},t_{1}]
that satisfies the conditions

\displaystyle\left|z(t_{0})-Z(t_{0})\right|\leq\varepsilon_{0},

and, for any
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
,

\displaystyle H_{-}(z(t),\dot{z}(t))\leq h\leq H_{+}(z(t),\dot{z}(t)).

Then for any
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
, we have

\displaystyle\left|z(t)-Z(t)\right|\leq C\nu+C\varepsilon_{0}.

Proof.

The proof is exactly the same as the one of Proposition
D.10
. We emphasize that at the final time
t_{1}
,
z(t_{1})
and
Z(t_{1})
have to be far enough from
Z(0)
. From (
D.15
), we indeed have

\displaystyle z(t_{1})\geq Z(t_{1})-C_{\star}\left(\nu_{6}^{\star}+\varepsilon_{0}^{\star}\right)\geq Z(0)+\frac{3}{4}\eta^{2},

and the rest of the arguments are similar.
∎
